[19] BleepingComputer - From Vulnerability Noise to Resilience: Rethinking Security in the AI Era
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 10:01:11 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/from-vulnerability-noise-to-resilience-rethinking-security-in-the-ai-era/

[TAM METİN - 1237 kelime]
Written by: George Manuelian, Chief Strategy Officer, RapidFort

The modern software stack has become an iceberg. The application code customers and security teams see at the surface is only a small portion of what is being shipped. Beneath it sit operating systems, frameworks, libraries, packages, and transitive dependencies, with much of it drawn from open source or third parties.

As AI accelerates software development, that hidden complexity has become an even more critical security challenge.

For business and technology leaders, the question is no longer simply how many vulnerabilities exist. It is where does meaningful risk live, which vulnerabilities can be exploited in our environment, and how we can reduce that risk without turning developers into full-time vulnerability managers.

The answer requires a fundamental shift from vulnerability accumulation to continuous software resilience.

First step: Recognizing composition of modern software

The typical containerized stack is layers of base operating system software, packages, libraries, language frameworks and dependencies, and the application itself. The application a company writes may represent only a small portion of what it ultimately ships.

That is important because vulnerabilities multiply. A vulnerable package in a base image is not necessarily one vulnerability in practice; it can be replicated across every application and container that uses that image.

In light of this, executives should ask a different question of their security programs: How much of the reported risk is relevant to the way our software operates? Traditional scanning frequently identifies the presence of a vulnerable component without establishing whether that component is loaded, executing, reachable or exposed.

A software bill of materials (SBOM) identifies what exists; a runtime bill of materials (RBOM) can pinpoint what is actually being used. That distinction can dramatically reduce noise.

For example, in the real world from a customer, an NGINX image containing 152 packages and 48 CVEs had only 47 packages actually running in the application. More than 80% of the software was not being exercised. The lesson is not that every unused package is harmless, but that security teams need operational context before assigning remediation urgency.

This is also why severity alone has become an inadequate basis for prioritization. A high or critical Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) score can command attention, but the business question is whether the vulnerable code is relevant and exploitable in the environment.

There are four contextual factors increasingly shaping remediation decisions: Whether a vulnerability is exposed, whether it is a known exploited vulnerability, whether exploitation is automatable, and whether exploitation would have meaningful technical impact.

The shift from severity to exploitability and reachability is important because it changes the operating model. Instead of asking engineering teams to chase the largest number on a scanner dashboard, leaders should ask whether the organization has enough visibility to determine what is running, what is reachable, and what is exposed.

As a result, security becomes a business-risk exercise rather than a race to close tickets.

Eliminate Up to 99.9% of Container CVEs Without Code Changes

RapidFort Curated Images give your team 35,000+ near-zero CVE container images, rebuilt and hardened every 24 hours.

Swap in a hardened base image and remove exploitable vulnerabilities at the source, with no code, OS or pipeline changes.

Hardening is the important next step

Hardening should not mean merely passing a configuration benchmark. It should also mean removing software and functionality that the application does not need

. Every unnecessary library, shell, package manager, build utility, or default component increases the amount of software that must be maintained and potentially gives an attacker additional tools if a container is compromised.

Runtime profiling is a proven approach for identifying these unused components and removing them systematically.

AI makes this discipline more important

AI-assisted development can rapidly generate code and pull in packages, but developers may not always know which dependencies were introduced or whether they remain necessary as the application evolves.

For example, a development environment in which developers may have to ask the AI system which Python version or packages were used because the model generated much of the implementation is a warning sign for governance.

Speed of development cannot come at the expense of knowing what is being shipped.

No one is going to slow AI development. The answer is to put security controls into the pipeline so developers do not have to perform every security task manually. Clean, curated base images; runtime profiling; removal of unused components; vulnerability context; and automated rebuilding can become repeatable parts of CI/CD.

The objective is to make the secure path the default path.

Introducing Zero Trust software architecture

Zero Trust has traditionally been associated with network access and identity. But when organizations continuously ingest open source and third-party software, they also need to question the software entering their environments.

Software should be verified, trusted components should be maintained in controlled registries, and the software should be continuously evaluated as it moves through development, testing and production – following the common principles of Zero Trust.

This represents a meaningful change in how software supply chain security should be managed. The goal should not be a perfect vulnerability dashboard.

The goal should be a development system that continuously produces software with less unnecessary code, fewer meaningful vulnerabilities, and a smaller attack surface.

For example, one customer reduced an initial 5,286 findings to three unresolved vulnerabilities within 48 hours by combining profiling, curated images, and removal of unused components.

The strategic lesson learned

Vulnerability remediation should become a manufacturing process, not a recurring emergency. If organizations wait until a scanner produces a massive backlog, they have already allowed complexity to become technical debt.

If instead they control what enters the software supply chain, understand what actually runs, prioritize based on reachability and impact, remove what is unnecessary, and continuously rebuild from cleaner components, security becomes part of software production itself.

This approach also changes the conversation between security and engineering. Developers should not be expected to fix every finding simply because a scanner found it. They need context, automation and a clear understanding of which risks require action.

Security leaders need visibility into runtime behavior and the ability to distinguish a theoretical exposure from a vulnerability that can actually affect the business.

The future of software security in the AI era

The future of software security belongs to organizations that manage complexity continuously. AI will accelerate development, it’s a given. Open source will remain foundational, that’s understood. Neither trend is going away, nor should it of course.

But both make it more important to establish disciplined software intake, strong supply-chain controls, runtime visibility, effective hardening, and automated remediation.

Ultimately, the objective is not simply to reduce the number of CVEs on a report. It is to build software that contains less unnecessary functionality, relies on cleaner components, exposes fewer opportunities to attackers, and can be continuously refreshed without overwhelming those responsible for delivering it

. Software supply chain security works best when profiling, verification, clean images, and removal of unused components are built directly into the pipeline.

The executive mandate for the AI era must stop treating vulnerability management as a downstream cleanup exercise. Build security into the way software is assembled, tested and deployed.

When continuous remediation becomes part of the normal development life cycle, organizations can move faster without accepting vulnerability volume as an unavoidable cost of innovation.

Eliminate Attack Vectors at the Source. Contact us today to find out more.

[20] BleepingComputer - McDonald's Japan data breach exposes customer location information
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 09:09:37 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/mcdonalds-japan-data-breach-exposes-customer-location-information/

[TAM METİN - 510 kelime]
McDonald's Japan, the fast-food giant's Japanese subsidiary, has disclosed a data breach after attackers accessed a database containing sensitive information for users of the company's official smartphone app.

Founded in 1940, McDonald's is now a multinational fast-food chain that operates more than 40,000 restaurants in more than 100 countries and serves tens of millions of customers daily.

In a Friday statement, the company's Japanese subsidiary confirmed that threat actors gained access to its official app's database and said the exposed information includes the users' product purchase history, IP addresses, mobile device location data, and customer management IDs (which cannot be used to identify the customers' email addresses or member IDs).

McDonald's Japan also added that the exposed data doesn't include the affected customers' names, email addresses, phone numbers, home addresses, or credit card information.

While it has yet to reveal when it detected the attack or how many individuals had their data stolen, the company said a follow-up investigation found no service disruption or misuse of official apps as a result of the incident.

"We are working to invalidate any credentials used for unauthorized access, investigate the relevant systems, and report to the Personal Information Protection Commission," it said. "In addition, we will strive to strengthen security measures and prevent recurrence."

This isn't the first data breach to affect McDonald's in recent years, with the fast food giant disclosing a breach in June 2021 after attackers breached its systems and stole information belonging to employees and customers from the US, South Korea, and Taiwan.

In 2017, McDonald's also patched a cross-site scripting (XSS) security flaw that affected its official website and exposed customers' plain-text passwords.

Japanese companies under attack

Last month, Burger King Japan also disclosed a major data breach after attackers stole 11.18 million customer records that were later leaked online.

The two incidents affecting the two Japanese fast food chains add to a large wave of cyberattacks impacting high-profile Japanese companies since the start of the year, including Japan Airlines, entertainment system maker Daiichi Kosho, cloud and digital infrastructure firm IDC Frontier, Japanese publishing giant Nikkei, cloud and data center service provider Sakura Internet, taxi operator Nihon Kotsu, telecommunications giant KDDI, and many others.

Macnica researcher Yutaka Sejiyama has logged 288 cybersecurity incidents affecting Japanese organizations involving personal information theft or exposure since the start of the year.

Sejiyama found that attackers are now probing websites and APIs for access-control, configuration, and authentication weaknesses, and exploiting known (n-day) vulnerabilities to find other victims.

[21] BleepingComputer - New Shirudo tool uses AI to shield unpatched Linux systems from exploits
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 08:34:23 -0500
Link: https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/new-shirudo-tool-uses-ai-to-shield-unpatched-linux-systems-from-exploits/

[TAM METİN - 673 kelime]
OpenAI and Cisco launched an open-source Linux security tool named Shirudo that deploys temporary runtime protections to block vulnerability exploitation while administrators prepare and roll out patches, without restarting services.

Shirudo leverages the eBPF (extended Berkeley Packet Filter) mechanism in Linux to monitor for suspicious activity and deploy targeted protections called "shields."

eBPF is a Linux technology that lets programs run in a controlled environment within the operating system's kernel without modifying its source code or requiring a restart.

It enables real-time monitoring of system and network activity, performance analysis, and enforcement of targeted security protections, making it an ideal operational basis for Shirudo.

Shirudo’s shields aim to block malicious behavior in a targeted way, leaving legitimate application operations undisturbed. OpenAI highlights four core capabilities for the tool:

Live protection: Blocks vulnerable code paths without restarting systems, letting operators assess shield impact before enforcing them.

AI-assisted defense: Enables agents to investigate vulnerabilities, develop shields, and validate their effectiveness, with deployment remaining under human control.

Trusted deployment: Uses cryptographic signatures, Linux's eBPF verifier, and kernel security controls to prevent unauthorized modifications.

Hardware-backed verification: Uses Trusted Platform Modules (TPMs) to provide verifiable records of shields being loaded or removed.

OpenAI and Cisco researchers tested custom-generated shields and demonstrated that they could successfully block a Java4Shell exploit attempt against a running Elasticsearch service without interrupting its operation.

Marcus Hutchins, principal threat researcher at managed detection and response company Expel, says that while there is "genuinely so much runtime exploit mitigation potential with eBPF," it may not be a good idea to let AI agents write rules that could impact the kernel.

He also noted that the tool's "capabilities are limited to Linux because Mac/Windows haven't implement the components needed to be effective."

Researchers at Isovalent, a cloud networking and security company acquired by Cisco in 2024, explain that "Shirudo is a rewrite of Tetragon," an open-source eBPF-based runtime security platform the company introduced in 2022, which also serves as the foundation for Cisco's existing Live Protect technology.

Shirudo is designed for agentic control plane, and a configurable degree of autonomy when it comes to deploying mitigations. Isovalent says the tool isn’t limited to eBPF and can also modify application or userspace runtime bytecode to extend protection where needed.

When a shield is deployed, they also provide operators with hit counts, giving them insight into attempted exploits and where defenses need strengthening.

The two companies plan to provide “ready-to-use” mitigations in the form of tested shields, along with vulnerability disclosures, until patching is possible. Shields will be distributed as cryptographically signed ‘.bee’ packages through an OCI registry.

Isovalent says that Shirudo can be activated through a command-line interface and doesn’t require a daemon to run permanently on the machine.

"The Shirudo project is only the beginning, but it already shows the true potential of combining agents as a workforce with machine interfaces to let agents output what they are best at: code," the researchers say.

They expect the tool to evolve and other projects to emerge around it, to the point of building self-defending that can run applications and agents side by side.

Shirudo is open-source (BSD + GPL) and comes with a quick-start guide as well as "getting started" documentation from Isovalent.

In a deep-dive webinar on December 15, OpenAI will provide a technical rundown of how the tool works and how it combines eBPF and artificial intelligence to detect and counter new attacks.

[22] Security Affairs - Maritime industry faces growing cyber threats as 50 ships targeted
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 08:23:10 +0000
Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203918/intelligence/maritime-industry-faces-growing-cyber-threats-as-50-ships-targeted.html

[TAM METİN - 507 kelime]
Maritime industry faces growing cyber threats as 50 ships targeted

US authorities investigate suspected cyberattacks on up to 50 commercial ships, with Iran and Russia under scrutiny and Fortinet flaws involved.

The United States is investigating a wave of cyberattacks targeting commercial vessels carrying oil, liquefied petroleum gas, chemicals, and other potentially hazardous cargo. According to a Wall Street Journal report, as many as 50 ships may have been targeted, with Iran emerging as the primary suspect.

“The U.S. is investigating Iran’s potential involvement in dozens of cyberattacks against energy tankers amid concerns that Tehran is widening the war with cyber operations and terror plots against Americans far from the Middle East.” WSJ states.

“As many as 50 ships carrying oil, liquefied petroleum, chemical products and other explosive substances are believed to have been attacked, according to U.S. officials and others familiar with the matter.”

US authorities are also considering possible Russian involvement. However, a Trump administration official told the WSJ that investigators have found no evidence linking Iran to the incidents and described four of the attacks as minor disruptions.

The incidents reportedly involved both cyber intrusions and intimidation campaigns. Some vessels received bomb threats, ransom demands, and messages warning them against transporting US products. Other ships may have been compromised without their crews being aware of the intrusions.

The US Coast Guard has reportedly deployed specialized cyber protection teams to at least four vessels approaching US ports to investigate and mitigate potential threats. The VL Prosperity and Kohaku were among the ships inspected after suspected compromises of their onboard networks.

The attackers are reportedly exploiting vulnerabilities in Fortinet networking devices widely used across the maritime industry. In September, the US Coast Guard Cyber Command and the FBI issued a joint alert warning shipping companies about state-sponsored actors exploiting FortiGate vulnerabilities in maritime environments.

The investigation also concerns vessels operating near Europe and Asia. One of them, the Vivit Africa LNG carrier, was reportedly targeted while transporting liquefied natural gas from the United States to Italy. The vessel experienced onboard system problems and received a bomb threat, although publicly available information does not establish that a cyberattack caused the technical incident.

The incidents highlight the growing exposure of commercial shipping to cyber threats. Modern vessels rely on interconnected IT and operational technology (OT) systems to manage communications, navigation, and cargo operations. A compromise of onboard networks could disrupt operations, delay deliveries, and create safety concerns, although the reported incidents do not demonstrate that attackers gained control of critical navigation or propulsion systems.

Alon Ayalon, co-founder of maritime cybersecurity firm Cydome, told the WSJ that most vessels have “less cyber protection than a midsize office.”

Even without causing physical damage, cyber intrusions and threats can force shipping companies to suspend operations, undergo security inspections, and face costly delays. The investigation highlights the need for maritime operators to address exposed network devices, strengthen remote access controls, and improve the separation between IT and OT environments.

Link: https://securityaffairs.com/203900/data-breach/eu-academy-users-names-and-emails-exposed-in-eu-commission-hack.html

[TAM METİN - 922 kelime]
EU Academy Users’ Names and Emails Exposed in EU Commission Hack

The EU Commission confirms an AI-powered cyberattack leaked EU Academy users’ names and emails. China-backed threat actors are suspected.

The European Commission has confirmed that the cyberattack it disclosed last week exposed personal data.

The European Commission (EC) confirmed that the cyberattack exposed personal information.

When the attack was discovered, several applications were temporarily shut down. At the time, the Commission said it had found no evidence that staff data had been compromised. EU cybersecurity experts have still not confirmed that any staff data was stolen, but the attack affected another group of users.

On Monday, Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier told reporters that the investigation had uncovered a data breach involving EU Academy. The platform offers online courses about EU policies to people outside the Commission. According to Euronews, it also provides professional training and specialised learning opportunities for young people.

What was taken is limited so far, but the picture isn’t final. Compromised information includes names and email addresses of EU Academy users. Access to the platform is suspended, and the European Data Protection Supervisor has been notified.

“The Commission has confirmed a leak of names and email addresses and is warning users of possible follow-ups from the attacker, such as phishing campaigns based on their personal data.” Euronews reports.

“That is the first major consequence of the incident. For other IT systems, EU cyber experts have not confirmed data theft affecting Commission staff, though several internal apps were taken offline as a precaution and some remain unavailable.”

Regnier said the scale of the leak is still under investigation, so the Commission posted one general message for everyone on the website, out of transparency, instead of contacting individuals.

CERT-EU, the cybersecurity team that protects EU institutions, detected the activity on 22 October and identified ARTEX, an AI-powered penetration-testing tool, as a possible tool used in the attack. An EU official told Politico that some threat actors misuse tools like ARTEX and have targeted the Commission. In a technical note published on 5 November, CERT-EU described ARTEX as a system that uses AI agents. An operator gives it an objective in plain language, and the software breaks it down into smaller tasks that the agents carry out using real hacking tools.

Last week EU experts said the attack had largely been contained, but Euronews tells a different story:

“On 22 October, the Commission’s IT department detected a malicious actor in its internal networks.” states euroNews. “A month after the attack began, the EU executive has yet to fully contain the breach and was forced to take the EU Academy website offline on Saturday.”

Regnier wouldn’t say on Monday whether the incident is over. Euronews’ sources describe an attack that’s still moving through Commission IT systems. Several internal apps were taken offline as a precaution, and some remain unavailable.

At this time, nobody has linked the attack to a specific actor, because Regnier said attribution has to wait until the investigation ends.

Euronews reports, citing two sources familiar with the matter, that the European Commission suspects a China-backed group of hackers is behind the large-scale cyberattack. The attack is reportedly still spreading through the Commission’s IT systems.

Inside the Commission, the suspected attackers are reportedly referred to as “Panda”. The term is used by the cybersecurity industry to loosely link threat actors to China. However, no official source has confirmed this attribution publicly.

The suspicion is based on the attack’s level of sophistication and the reported use of ARTEX, a Chinese-developed tool that uses AI to find vulnerabilities that attackers can exploit.

Euronews says Microsoft and CrowdStrike have tied ARTEX-driven espionage campaigns against hundreds of organisations to China-linked groups they call Storm-0940 and Panda Vapor.

The current threat assessment says the actor wasn’t going after specific systems or people. It wanted to test how far it could get into the Commission’s networks. A narrower target would have called for a quieter attack.

The Commission’s handling of the breach upset its staff union, which says employees learned about it from the press. The Commission also says public disclosure may alert attackers to its response and cause panic too. For EU Academy users, the risk is phishing: stolen names and email addresses could make scam messages more convincing. Users should be cautious with unexpected messages, visit the Academy website directly, and avoid sharing personal information with anyone claiming to represent the Commission.

This isn’t the Commission’s first hit this year. On March 24, the European Commission detected a cyberattack affecting the cloud infrastructure hosting its Europa.eu websites. The incident was quickly contained, with mitigation measures applied and no disruption to website availability. CERT-EU attributed a European Commission cloud breach to the TeamPCP threat group, revealing that data from at least 30 EU entities was exposed.

TeamPCP reportedly accessed the EU’s AWS environment on March 10 using a stolen API key from the Trivy supply-chain attack.

They then used tools like TruffleHog to find more credentials, created new access keys to stay hidden, and carried out reconnaissance and data theft. TeamPCP is also linked to supply-chain attacks on platforms like GitHub, PyPI, NPM, and Docker, including a compromised LiteLLM package used to spread data-stealing malware.

On 30 January, the European Commission detected another cyberattack that hit its mobile device management system. The organization pointed out that no mobile devices were compromised. The Commission contained and cleaned the system within nine hours.

[24] SANS ISC - Scanning from Subnet 109.160.32.0/24, (Mon, Nov 23rd)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 02:32:00 GMT
Link: https://isc.sans.edu/diary/rss/33550

[TAM METİN - 751 kelime]
Scanning from Subnet 109.160.32.0/24

This subnet is assigned to a web hosting and virtual private server (VPS) provider BANATSYNC SRL with AS198364, located in Timisoara, Romania. This graph shows the DShield sensor activity over the past 90 days for this subnet and it is still ongoing as of this writing.

Using the ES|QL query listed below, starting the query from the 1 September 2026, 159 IPs from this block scanned at least once to multiple times my DShield sensors against mostly the SSH service and when found, attempted and sometimes successfully login the sensor. This analysis from this subnet started on the 5 September 2026 and capture regularly by 2 separate DShield sensors.

A review of both the firewall and cowrie logs, all IPs involved attempted to login into the SSH service with almost all (152 out 159) of them succeeding to login into the sensors using with the standard usernames (root, ubuntu, admin, etc.) using a single command (uname -s -v -n -r -m) followed by a immediate disconnection. One sensor received 127 unique IPs while the other received 134 unique IPs.

The firewall logs show some interesting characteristics with a time-to-live mostly between 45 to 52 with some exception that also include ranges between 232 to 243. In order to understand the discrepancies, I reviewed the packet capture from each sensor and found some packets where 69 IPs stared with a static IPID of 54321 and every one of them had a TTL that range between 232 to 243. All the inbound backets behaved the same way, SYN, SYN-ACK, RST targeting various ports including TCP/2323, TCP/22 and TCP/2222 where the actors/bots sometimes after multiple attemps, successfully login.

Using the logs from the DShield SIEM and selecting only the traffic with the IPID 54321 and reviewing the Vega-Lite graph, it is easy to see the activity was consistent with the previously identified 69 IPs that each time each IP would come live, the first packet was always starting with high TTL (230+) and the remainder packets were all using normal TTL related to a moderl Linux system. In order to inspect why this would be happening, using packet capture by the sensors based on matching high TTL for one specific IP address (109.160.32.146) and what appeared was interesting. Using tshark to replay the initial packet for 13 Nov 2026 started with a TTL of 239 and when that traffic responded with a reset (0x0004), the TTL was now 50. Any traffic that followed the first SYN packets all had TTL of 50 (picture below).

This is a sample of 4 IPs (DSL query below) out of the 69 that shows the first packet starting with a TTL 230+ followed by normal TTL after the activity continue. Reviewing  the reports from VirusTotal of those 4 IPs [7][8][9][10] shows all the same characteristics with SSH brute-force if SSH is listening and running some commands before disconnecting.

Shodan Open Ports
This is a random list of IPs checked against Shodan that list the same open ports. The two sample lists show these IPs have SSH available as the only service while other have nothing open. If the SSH service is listening, it is using 2 different version of Ubuntu 24.04 LTS and 26.04 LTS.

Shodan Information about SSH TCP/22

109.160.32.141 SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_9.6p1 Ubuntu-3ubuntu13   (Ubuntu 24.04 LTS)
109.160.32.147 SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_9.6p1 Ubuntu-3ubuntu13   (Ubuntu 24.04 LTS)
109.160.32.150 SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_9.6p1 Ubuntu-3ubuntu13   (Ubuntu 24.04 LTS)
109.160.32.200 SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_10.2p1 Ubuntu-2ubuntu3.2 (Ubuntu 26.04 LTS)

Shodan No Open Port for this IP sample

109.160.32.80
109.160.32.88

Kibana Traffic Analysis with DSL and ES|QL

{
  "range": {
    "related.ip": {
      "gte": "109.160.32.0",
      "lte": "109.160.32.255"
    }
  }
}

DSL Query for IP with a TTL Greater than 230

{
  "range": {
    "network.ttl": {
      "gte": "230"
    }
  }
}

DSL Query IP with Low & High TTL

{
  "terms": {
    "related.ip": [
      "109.160.32.88",
      "109.160.32.141",
      "109.160.32.147",
      "109.160.32.150"
    ]
  }
}

FROM cowrie* 
| WHERE event.reference == "no match"
| WHERE CIDR_MATCH(related.ip, "109.160.32.0/24")
| STATS  BY related.ip

FROM cowrie* 
| WHERE event.reference == "no match"
| WHERE CIDR_MATCH(related.ip, "109.160.32.0/24")
| WHERE network.ttl >= 230
| STATS  BY network.ttl

This is the command used to verify the activity between low and high TTL:

tshark.exe -nnr tcp2323_54321_random.pcap -T fields -e frame.time -e ip.src -e ip.dst -e ip.ttl -e tcp.flags

[25] Recorded Future - The Cyber Threats Behind Space Race 2.0
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 00:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/cyber-threats-space-race

[TAM METİN - 1475 kelime]
The Cyber Threats Behind Space Race 2.0

There were 329 orbital launch attempts in 2025, up from 263 the year before. In the same year, a record 4,517 satellites were placed in orbit. 87% of these satellites were commercially owned; they are part of a space economy that reached $686 billion in 2025 and is set to skyrocket further.

In April 2026, Artemis II completed a crewed lunar flyby, the first humans beyond low Earth orbit since 1972. In March, NASA paused the Gateway station and pivoted to building a lunar base instead. Beijing expects taikonauts on the Moon's surface by 2030 and a functioning International Lunar Research Station by 2035.

The first space race ended with the Cold War, but now that geopolitical competition is escalating, the second space race has begun, and cybersecurity will play a vital role in the outcomes.

This is because thousands of new satellites are computers running software written by people. So is every lander, rover, and relay that follows them to the Moon. They also all have a connection to a ground station on Earth, which is connected to the internet. Therefore, as the second space race accelerates, the risk of cyberattacks affecting space operations is rising exponentially year after year.

Commercial Space Becomes the Target

At around five in the morning on February 24, 2022, roughly an hour before Russian armor crossed into Ukraine, tens of thousands of satellite modems across Europe went dark.

Russia had used a weapon called AcidRain, a data-wiping malware tool that targets modems, routers, and satellite communications, to target the KA-SAT broadband network run by Viasat, which the Ukrainian military relied on for command and control. AcidRain was pushed out through the operator's own management network.

The collateral damage extended far beyond the battlefield: around 5,800 wind turbines in Germany lost remote monitoring, and ordinary customers from Poland to France lost their internet access. The UK, US, and EU later attributed the attack to Russia.

Nobody launched a rocket. Nobody fired a jammer. It was a cyberattack that used satellites.

This will be part of the story of the second space race.

Space Operations Have a Postcode

We imagine satellites as remote and therefore safe; however, they are not.

Every satellite has a terrestrial address: the ground stations that command it, the links that carry its telemetry, the user terminals at the edge, and the software supply chain behind it all. That is where attackers go, because that is where the doors are.

The doors are often unlocked. Researchers at UC San Diego and the University of Maryland pointed an $800 dish at the sky and found that roughly half the geostationary satellites they could see were transmitting in the clear: telecommunications backhaul, corporate traffic, military communications.

In March, the NSA and its Five Eyes partners issued joint guidance warning that as low Earth orbit constellations grow, the attack surface open to adversaries expands across the space, ground, and user segments, as well as the supply chains behind them.

Weapons in Orbit, and Companies in the Crossfire

Space militarization is no longer implied.

On September 14, 2026, Air Force Secretary Troy Meink confirmed for the first time that the United States has on-orbit space-control weapons capable of offensive and defensive use.

Nine days later, the UK stood up No. III Space Effects Squadron, with a brief to disrupt, degrade, and deny hostile activity in orbit. Announcing it, the Chief of the Air Staff pointed to five Russian military satellites maneuvering close to two Finnish commercial spacecraft, to weekly Russian attempts to jam British military satellites, and to a £1.4 billion daily bill if the UK lost GPS.

The private sector did not ask to be a combatant, but in an increasingly geopolitically volatile world, it is one.

Starlink kept the Ukrainian army talking and the Ukrainian economy functioning when terrestrial networks were targeted, and in doing so, a consumer broadband product became a military target. The extent to which Ukraine relies on Starlink has become very clear in recent weeks, as it faces service disruptions creating real challenges on the battlefield.

In July, Der Spiegel, Le Monde, and The Insider published documents from a secret China-Russia military-technical forum held in Guangzhou in November 2023. One presentation by two engineers from China's state aerospace corporation was devoted entirely to countering Starlink.

It laid out an escalation ladder: diplomatic and regulatory pressure first, then joint jamming over chosen territory alongside cyber operations and Starlink-specific malware, then, finally, kinetic destruction.

Nobody Touched the Satellite

In March 2025, a group calling itself Lab-Dookhtegan claimed it had cut communications on 116 vessels belonging to Iran's sanctioned tanker and shipping lines.

In August 2025, it struck again, this time disabling 39 tankers and 25 cargo ships. The route in was Fanava Group, a Tehran IT company that supplied the fleet's satellite communications. From there, the attackers gained root access to the Linux terminals aboard each ship, killed the Falcon software that kept the satellite links alive, and took down automatic identification system (AIS) tracking in the process.

According to incident analysis, they had been inside the network for five months before pulling the trigger, wiping storage partitions, logs, and recovery data on the way out.

On August 7, 2026, the VL Prosperity, a 333-meter crude carrier bound for Texas, lost its communications for around 30 hours near Gibraltar after its onboard network was compromised. The FBI and Coast Guard cyber teams boarded the VL Prosperity and a second tanker in the Gulf of Mexico before they could reach port, and Washington is reportedly looking at foreign threat actors as the culprit. The technical analysis so far shows where investigators looked first: the satellite terminals and remote maintenance portals that connect a ship at sea to the shore.

Meanwhile, Viasat itself was identified as a victim of Salt Typhoon, a state-sponsored group known for burrowing into American telecommunications.

To take things a step further, SpaceX’s Starlink users were allegedly targeted by the advertising firm Discover-Tech in a suspected effort to locate or monitor users of satellite internet. The incident highlights how users of space-based communications can be surveilled through online advertising, without attackers needing to compromise the satellites themselves.

Pay attention to the pattern. In none of these cases did anyone touch an object in orbit. The attackers went for the vendor, the terminal, the management plane.

When the Data Center Leaves Earth

Last November, a start-up called Starcloud put an Nvidia H100 into orbit and ran a large language model on it. In October 2026, Google launched a prototype for Project Suncatcher, its plan for solar-powered AI clusters in space, carrying four of its TPU chips.

SpaceX says it expects to deploy orbital AI compute satellites from 2028, and in January 2026, it asked the Federal Communications Commission (FCC) to license an orbital data center of up to a million satellites.

Whatever you think of the economics, the direction is clear: Within a decade, a meaningful share of the world's AI compute, and the data that feeds it, could live 300 miles up in space, reachable through ground stations and optical links that someone has to defend.

If this materializes, it will be all the more important to ensure systems are protected from cyberattacks.

The Reality We Have Not Yet Accepted

Launch rates are at record highs and still climbing. The economy built in orbit is heading for trillions of dollars, four-fifths of which are private. Two great powers are racing to build permanent bases on the Moon, and a new era of astropolitics is arriving.

Weapons are confirmed in orbit. And the cheapest, most deniable way to attack any of it runs through the same networks, vendors, and terminals attackers already know how to breach.

So what does securing the sector actually mean? Treating space as critical national infrastructure in law and in budgets. Holding vendors and terminals to the same standard as the spacecraft, because that is where Viasat and Iran’s fleet were breached. Encrypting the links an $800 dish can still read. And widening the threat model beyond nation-states to hacktivists and ad buyers.

In Guangzhou, cyber sat one rung below kinetic destruction. Every incident here happened on that rung, and none required a rocket.

2027 will see an acceleration of the trends discussed. We are being warned.

Space has a postcode. We have a duty to protect it.

Insikt Group: Space and Cyber: The Race Above and the Battle BelowRecorded Future: The convergence of space and cyber: An evolving threat landscape

Insikt Group adheres to responsible disclosure procedures in conducting its research.

Recorded Future’s Insikt Group, the company’s threat research division, comprises analysts and security researchers with deep government, law enforcement, military, and intelligence agency experience. Its mission is to produce intelligence that reduces risk for customers, enables tangible outcomes, and prevents business disruption.

[26] Cyberscoop - Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 22:01:23 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/cia-odni-osint-data-breach/

[TAM METİN - 363 kelime]
Unclassified intel system hit by hackers, affecting CIA, ODNI

The CIA and Office of the Director of National Intelligence have discovered a data breach of an open-source intelligence analysis system, CyberScoop has confirmed.

The New York Times first reported the breach Tuesday, writing that the breach had exposed some unclassified information.  A source familiar with the breach said there was a disruption, but the exposure was limited.

‘The hack did not involve our classified networks or classified information, and no other systems are currently at risk,” the source said.

The discovery is the latest in a series of intrusions affecting U.S. security agencies, including two at the FBI and another at the National Security Agency.

Ben Bernstein, manager of cybersecurity advisers at Huntress, said in an emailed comment that while the reported breach didn’t hit classified data, open-source (OSINT) information can still be sensitive.

“Calling this ‘unclassified’ doesn’t make it harmless,”  he said. “These systems pull in public and commercial info like news, satellite imagery, and paid data feeds, so the raw material isn’t a state secret. The value for an adversary is seeing what the CIA and ODNI are doing with it.”

“If you can see what analysts are searching for and what topics they’re tracking, you get a solid look at U.S. intelligence priorities and blind spots without ever touching a classified file,” he continued. “These OSINT networks are also tough to secure because they have to stay open to the public internet to do their job.”

The CIA declined to comment and the Office of the Director of National Intelligence did not immediately respond to a request for comment Tuesday.

[27] Cyberscoop - Patel mum on FBI crime info database hack, citing ongoing investigation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 17:48:34 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/kash-patel-fbi-ncic-database-hack/

[TAM METİN - 416 kelime]
Patel mum on FBI crime info database hack, citing ongoing investigation

There’s an active investigation into a hack of the FBI’s National Crime Information Center, bureau director Kash Patel said Tuesday, but he otherwise declined to comment on details of the breach.

Patel said “the matter is ongoing” when asked at a new conference about who was behind the hack reported Friday and what information they obtained from the NCIC, a national database for sharing information on warrants, fugitives, missing persons and background checks.

“We’ve publicly released, as we always do, information we are permitted to at this time, but it’s an ongoing investigation,” he said, adding that the FBI was working with other agencies on the matter. “So we’re working with the interagency, and as we’ve done in the past, we will make all the information public either through our partners at the Department of Justice or through our engagements on Capitol Hill.”

President Donald Trump over the weekend said the breach was “not good” and that he’d just learned about it, but otherwise downplayed it, something he often does in response to cyberattacks against the United States.

“I don’t like that,” Trump said. “But maybe we will learn things that we would’ve never learned otherwise.”

It was the second breach in recent months at the FBI, which suffered an attack from ShinyHunters in September that saw the bureau’s website defaced and the hacking group claiming it stole sensitive information on FBI agents and jobseekers.

Patel made his remarks at a Justice Department press conference to tout progress in its battle against scammers — including cybercrime — since Trump signed an executive order in March. As part of his answer to the question about the FBI breach, Patel claimed intrusions were “down 36% based on FBI caseload” under Trump compared to his predecessor, Joe Biden.

[28] Cyberscoop - UK cyber agency, threat companies tracking rise in AI-assisted hacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 17:27:09 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/uk-ncsc-ai-assisted-cyberattacks/

[TAM METİN - 822 kelime]
UK cyber agency, threat companies tracking rise in AI-assisted hacks

As U.S. policymakers focus on outages that appear partly  caused by widespread patching of AI-identified vulnerabilities, the U.K.’s top cyber intelligence agency says it is seeing more cyberattacks carried out with AI assistance.

On Tuesday, three officials from the National Cyber Security Centre said they had observed “a marked rise” in AI-assisted cyberattacks since September.

“Organizations across multiple sectors are reporting incidents as a result of threat actors using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’; software originally designed for red teaming being applied beyond its defensive purpose,” wrote David Chismon, chief technology officer for architecture, and two partially-named NCSC staffers, Kris B., incident handler, and Ellen T, lead data scientist on the vulnerability management team.

The incidents resemble past AI-enabled hacks, defined by rapid, automated vulnerability discovery and attempted exploitation of all of a target’s internet-facing assets “in quick succession.” The impact on victims ranged from outages to system compromise.

“What has changed, is how quickly and persistently threat actors can conduct attacks – at low cost – using these frameworks combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers,” the officials wrote.

The NCSC officials called the threat sobering but noted that “none” of the vulnerabilities or attacks are novel. They’re known weaknesses that organizations should have patched sooner, and most can still be patched. Additionally, hacking operations moving at speed and scale generate a great deal of “noise” and telemetry that defenders can track.

Large language models are powerful on their own, but combined with technical harnesses, offensive security frameworks, and human expertise, they can become genuine national security threats.

Technical harnesses – systems and tools that can guide and contextualize LLM-driven actions, manage memory and resource requirements, and dictate when human operators need to step in – are particularly valuable to both cybersecurity defenders and malicious hackers.

Eric Loui, a principal intelligence analyst at CrowdStrike, said the company is tracking dozens of threat actor groups that increasingly delegate decisions to agentic AI.  Sophisticated harnesses means that even easily accessible open-source LLMs, like Chinese-made DeepSeek, can become powerful offensive hacking tools in the right hands.

“These are older models, the safeguards are easy to overcome,” Loui said last week at the CYBERWARCON hacker conference in Arlington, Va. “And you know, in the worst-case scenario you can just start a new session and try, try again and that worked well enough” for many threat actors.

In one attack on a healthcare organization in Argentina, the threat actor identified the target and asked the model to find an initial access point. Loui emphasized how rudimentary the instructions were.

“They don’t tell it to go after the services, they don’t tell it how to do the recon,” he said. “They just trust that the harness [and] the training data in the AI model are sufficient, and then after 90 minutes the agent has identified and exploited a SQL injection vulnerability and [is] able to get it checked.”

Other threat analysts told CyberScoop they’ve observed hackers increasingly building bespoke software and malware for each engagement. That makes it harder to identify threat actors by their toolsets or track malware “families,” as threat intelligence companies have traditionally done.

The NCSC and many cybersecurity experts have said these vulnerabilities reflect decades of accumulated technical debt, leaving them outdated, insecure and unmonitored assets to manage.

As AI tools become more accessible and cheaper, offensive security faces no meaningful financial or resource constraints when determining targets.

Some, like former NSA cybersecurity director Rob Joyce, liken the coming years of exploitation and patching to a forest fire: widespread damage that also clears the brush that makes such fires so dangerous. Organizations that address built-up technical debt, through proactive patching or in response to malicious hacks, may emerge with more secure and resilient systems.

“Nearly everything the NCSC has seen can be prevented by implementing cyber security best practice, the application of modern cyber security approaches like zero trust and by the diligent application of technical controls in line with your organization’s technologies and processes,” the NCSC officials wrote.

Artificial Intelligence (AI)

National Cyber Security Centre

[29] Cyberscoop - Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 11:00:00 +0000
Link: https://cyberscoop.com/ot-security-post-quantum-cryptography-op-ed/

[TAM METİN - 1223 kelime]
Tomorrow’s quantum threat is today’s open door

Earlier this month, attackers switched refrigeration systems in supermarkets across nine European countries into defrost mode. Stores were forced to throw out spoiled stock, and as is now being reported, some blood banks in France were hit as well. Around the same time, U.S. agencies including the FBI and CISA warned that internet-exposed refrigeration and temperature controllers were being actively targeted.

The attackers didn’t get in by breaking encryption or cracking passwords. They found admin interfaces exposed to the internet that never should have been public facing, along with known vulnerabilities and, in some cases, default credentials. In other words, unnecessary attack surfaces, unpatched systems, and factory default passwords. They didn’t need a quantum cryptography breakthrough. They had an open door.

That’s worth keeping in mind as the post-quantum cryptography (PQC) conversation picks up speed. New algorithms have been standardized, governments are setting migration timelines, and vendors are urging organizations to start migrating. Quantum computing’s potential to break widely used encryption deserves serious preparation. But nearly all of that guidance is targeted at IT. In operational technology (OT), the PQC timeline looks very different, and the risks hurting organizations today are frankly much lower-hanging fruit.

Before worrying about tomorrow’s lockpick technology, lock today’s open doors.

The devices that run water, electric, critical infrastructure, and manufacturing environments were built for a different era and a different purpose. Some examples are PLCs (programmable logic controllers), HMIs (human-machine interfaces) and OITs (operator interface terminals), as well as RTUs (remote terminal units) and PMRs (pole-mounted routers) in the electric sector.

Start with lifecycles. NIST’s “Guide to Operational Technology (OT) Security” (SP 800-82 Rev. 3) notes that OT technology often remains deployed for 10 to 15 years, and sometimes longer. In practice, it’s often much longer. Organizations don’t always inventory these devices, or even realize they’re on the network, so they can sit in public-sector environments for 20 years or more. I’m a millennial, and I’ve assessed equipment at an electrical site that was last updated before I was born.

Then there’s the hardware factor. OT devices are purpose-built to do exactly what they need to do and nothing more. Many run on less than 1 gigabyte of RAM, and some on 512 MB or less. Many don’t support current encryption standards and still rely on protocols and encryption methods that are a decade old or more. Post-quantum algorithms would ask more from hardware that can already barely handle its workload.

These devices aren’t ready for today’s security landscape, let alone tomorrow’s.

Over the years, I’ve assessed more than 35 utility sites and dozens of manufacturing plants. Only a handful had OT networks I’d consider mature or hardened, with clear governance, documented policies and procedures, and controls that held up under testing. Most shared the same weaknesses: default credentials, little or no segmentation, insecure protocols, incomplete asset inventories, and missing policies and procedures. I can say with confidence that fewer than 20% of utilities are ready for a genuine penetration test by today’s standards, let alone a quantum-capable adversary.

None of those gaps are cryptographic. They’re foundational, and they’re the same gaps attackers used in the refrigeration attacks. When a device can be reached from the internet and logged into with a factory password, the strength of its encryption doesn’t matter.

For much of OT, the threat isn’t a future adversary decrypting traffic. It’s a present-day adversary reading cleartext traffic or using a default credential to authenticate to a device that corporate security didn’t even know was connected to the wider internet.

The classic CIA triad is often helpful to keep in mind—it defines three core components of security: confidentiality, integrity, and availability. Historically, uptime and safety requirements have pushed OT manufacturers to prioritize availability and integrity, giving confidentiality (encryption) less attention. Confidentiality is more often than not Velcroed onto these devices to meet contract requirements.

Even when confidentiality is considered, many legacy OT devices lack the processing power, memory, or upgrade paths needed to adopt post-quantum cryptography. Newer devices may be better equipped, but long replacement cycles and demanding validation requirements mean OT’s transition to PQC could lag IT’s by years, or even decades.

Additionally, to further mitigate risk, consider that many PLCs and HMIs don’t need direct outbound access. Limit them to strictly what they need. Border gateways and firewalls between environments are great. For legacy devices, go further: consider truly air-gapping them, or placing them in segments with no or extremely limited outbound access. Data diodes, gateways that let data flow in only one direction, are a strong option for getting information out of OT without opening a path back in.

Understanding what you have, making security a focus point of purchasing decisions, implementing basic OT security controls via a mappable framework, and writing associated policies, procedures, etc. is the best way to protect your OT environment.

If I had to pick three priorities for the year ahead, they’d be these. First, segment your networks. If an attacker in your IT environment can reach OT, you have a problem. While you’re at it, remove unnecessary internet exposure, especially admin interfaces, and change every default credential you find. Second, build a complete asset inventory. You can’t protect what you don’t know you have. Invest in a platform, and keep inventory of both hardware and software, especially the devices that have quietly been in service for decades. Finally, run tabletop exercises. Practice what we preach. Test your incident response and recovery capabilities for real, not just on paper. Update your policies, procedures, and playbooks with the lessons learned.

What about procurement? Some guidance suggests writing PQC readiness into OT purchasing requirements today. I’d advise against it. No OT vendor is ready for PQC, and no vendor can credibly assure anything about a threat we haven’t yet seen in action. Make it known that PQC is on your roadmap and ask your vendors about theirs. But don’t select a vendor purely on PQC claims. Put that energy into today’s hard-to-achieve OT challenges instead, like zero trust.

Quantum computing inspires anxiety, and its potential impact on encryption is real. But quantum readiness is a long-term responsibility. Closing obvious security gaps is an immediate need. Companies must do both and stop letting tomorrow’s threat overshadow today’s failures.

Put PQC on your OT roadmap, just not at the top of it. Prepare for tomorrow’s lockpick. But close today’s open doors first.

[30] The Register - This could mess with Thanksgiving, says VMware as it drops third critical patch bundle in a week
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 06:15:08 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/virtualization/2026/11/25/this-could-mess-with-thanksgiving-says-vmware-as-it-drops-third-critical-patch-bundle-in-a-week/5312017

[TAM METİN - 536 kelime]
This could mess with Thanksgiving, says VMware as it drops third critical patch bundle in a week

Four more critical vulns, including another unauthorized access flaw in vCenter

'We are not ShinyHunters,' says criminal hacking crew that looks and acts just like ShinyHunters
12 hours ago

An AI coding agent, a browser, and a Mac tool found a financial firm's access key in under 6 minutes
16 hours ago

CrowdStrike splashes $44B on Zscaler, customers brace for the bill
20 hours ago

European Commission confirms personal data stolen in AI-assisted cyberattack
21 hours ago

Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
1 day ago

VMware has issued its third critical security advisory in a week, this time to warn of four critical flaws and address a total of 23 issues.

VMSA-2026-0011 landed on November 24, just five days after two other advisories. VMware’s FAQ for the new advisory includes the following remarks:

Because of coordinated disclosure and release pipeline timing this VMSA comes on the heels of VMSA-2026-0009, and atop a major holiday in the United States.The security vulnerability landscape is more dynamic than ever; please know that while we acknowledge this timing is not optimal, your ability to operate and maintain a secure environment amidst modern, AI-assisted threats is paramount to us.

Make of that what you will as you ponder how and when to patch the four critical flaws, including the 9.8-rated CVE-2026-123903, which allows a malicious actor to bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to vCenter, the management tool VMware users rely on to manage hosts and VMs.

This is the third 9.8-rated authentication issue VMware has fixed in vCenter since July.

Two of the other critical flaws in this new advisory allow an attacker to run code on an ESX host, earning the bugs a 9.8 rating. CVE-2026-123910 makes that possible thanks to a heap out-of-bounds write vulnerability in the I/O filter service, while CVE-2026-123911 is a use-after-free vulnerability.

The other critical flaw, CVE-2026-123912, can result in a guest escape from VMware’s desktop Workstation and Fusion desktop hypervisors. It’s also present in VMware’s ESX hypervisor, but attackers thankfully can’t escape that product’s sandbox. This one’s easy to mitigate because the problem is in a USB controller that users can turn off.

Most of the other flaws require installation of updated software.

There’s a little good news in that some of the software updates VMware issued last week include fixes for the newly disclosed flaws. But there are still plenty of fresh fixes to apply, across ten products: ESX, vCenter, Workstation, Fusion, VMware tools, Cloud Foundation, vSphere Foundation, the Telco Cloud Platform, Telco Cloud Infrastructure, and the VMware Avi Load Balancer.

VMware’s advisory thanks external researchers for finding and reporting most of the flaws. The Broadcom business unit is known to be a member of Project Glasswing, the Anthropic-run effort to use the Mythos model to find flaws that has seen many vendors dramatically increase the number of patches they produce.

In past years, VMware has often published considerably more than this year’s 11 security advisories. With five weeks left in the year, perhaps vAdmins need to worry Christmas might also get complicated. ®

[31] The Register - An AI coding agent, a browser, and a Mac tool found a financial firm's access key in under 6 minutes
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 18:00:00 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/24/an-ai-coding-agent-a-browser-and-a-mac-tool-found-a-financial-firms-access-key-in-under-6-minutes/5311922

[TAM METİN - 1168 kelime]
An AI coding agent, a browser, and a Mac tool found a financial firm's access key in under 6 minutes

No sophisticated malware or attack framework required

Signal slams door on prying AI agents to protect privacy
4 days ago

Google continues model chemistry experiment with Neon
6 days ago

California sues OpenAI for misleading public about AI safety
8 days ago

Attackers are using AI agents to automate cybercrime at 'industrial scale,' FBI and AWS warn
8 days ago

Agents reveal secrets when offered to team up with each other
18 days ago

A Romanian hacker, armed with only an open source coding agent, a Chrome browser, and a Mac, tried multiple ways to break into a financial services firm, ultimately found an access key - luckily, it was a decoy - and carried out an entire (almost) attack in under six minutes.

It’s a stark reminder that miscreants don’t need sophisticated malware - as in the Binance heist - or AI pen-testing tools like ARTEX to carry out intrusions into critical networks. Open source coding agents, and specifically OpenCode in this case, also work just fine.

“What surprised me most was how little it took and the toolkit: a free coding agent, the curl that comes with every Mac and an ordinary Chrome, all from one laptop,” Jesta co-founder and CTO Lior Finkelshtein told The Register. “Until now I thought that we had more time until tools like OpenCode are used in offensive operations. This incident shows us that it's already in use in the field, and we will probably see more of that in the future.”

Jesta is a Tel Aviv-based security startup that builds defensive tools to detect and stop autonomous AI attacks. In a report shared exclusively with The Register, Finkelshtein documented an attempted intrusion targeting a major financial services company that’s also a Jesta customer - the security shop declined to say which one.

Jesta’s investigation into the incident determined that someone in Bucharest downloaded a free AI coding agent called OpenCode and pointed it at the financial services customer on November 16 at 4:24:57 a.m. ET.

'Nothing in OpenCode was built for hacking'

OpenCode is an open source coding agent that lets users connect to more than 200 model providers or local models, including Anthropic’s Claude models, OpenAI’s GPTs, Google’s Gemini, DeepSeek, and Z.ai’s GLM. Jesta doesn’t know which model the attacker used, as there was nothing in the traffic that named the model. OpenCode's web fetch tool, however, did name itself, so Jesta knows the attacker used this coding agent - not a scanner - to do the dirty work.

“Nothing in OpenCode was built for hacking,” Finkelshtein said in a Tuesday report about the agent-led attack. But, he added, nothing in the coding agent “stops you from pointing it at someone else's website either. When you do, the agent goes through the site the same way it goes through code. It reads, tries something, reads the result and tries the next thing, for as long as you let it run.”

That’s what it did in this operation, which took less than six minutes and cost the human operator nothing.

The first call came from a Chrome browser on a Mac, requesting the front page of one of the financial firm’s systems. The site refused it, and six seconds later, the same IPv6 address in Bucharest, on an ordinary DIGI Romania business line, tried again. It used the same Chrome version, but said it was an Android Pixel 10 phone (it wasn’t) to see if the site would let a phone in (it didn’t).

The bar for doing this is now installing an app. Think about how many people can do that.

After failing to gain access through the front page, the agent asked the system for its API definition, which was also refused. Five seconds later, it tried a different address - this one was a decoy set up by Jesta - and it answered the agent, providing the readable file defining how the API works.

The agent read the document, and 13 seconds later followed a link it contained. At 4:26:01 a.m. ET, Jesta’s defenses marked the website visitor as an AI agent, opened a case, and from here on out, everything the agent accessed was contained inside a fake environment designed to trick intruders. This included a phony key, which the attacker then used to attempt to break into the company’s systems.

“It worked like a developer,” Finkelshtein said about the coding agent. “It read the API docs and did what they said. It found a key and used it. It checked whether the lock was real, then made up a key of its own and tried that. None of the threat feeds the customer has knew the address, so a blocklist would never have stopped it. The bar for doing this is now installing an app. Think about how many people can do that.”

'No blocklist would have helped'

In addition to OpenCode, the attacker used only Chrome 157, both in desktop and mobile modes, and curl/8.7.1, a command-line tool that has shipped with every Mac since macOS 15. The agent passed information between the three tools. It included the planted key in a command-line request and then tested three scenarios – using the decoy key, no key, and a fabricated key – to determine whether what it found in the API documentation was legit.

It also tried multiple URLs using the browser. While it ultimately identified a valid endpoint, it attempted to read from one that accepts only data submissions, so its request was blocked again. Five minutes and 44 seconds after the first request, it gave up, and the logs haven’t detected any activity from this same intruder since then.

The security shop and its customer disclosed the activity to law enforcement and told us it’s “being handled.”

While the attacker wasn’t able to steal anything in this attempt, security teams still need to take note, according to Finkelshtein.

“No blocklist would have helped” detect the malicious traffic, he explained. “Most security tools still decide by asking whether an address is known to be bad, and for this one the answer was no. If that key had been real, it would have been in.”

Instead of blocklists, Finkelshtein suggests paying attention to what the visitor does. In this case, several of its actions gave it away - including following a link that only existed inside an API doc, using a desktop browser and a fake phone, then curl, all from the same address within minutes, and trying multiple keys - including one with a made up name and then no key - within seconds.

“Open source lets anyone build software without a big team,” Finkelshtein wrote in the Tuesday blog. “AI is doing the same for hacking. The skill is in the model, the model is a setting, and the person giving the instructions doesn't need to know how any of it works.” ®

[32] The Register - CrowdStrike splashes $44B on Zscaler, customers brace for the bill
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 13:22:17 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/security/2026/11/24/crowdstrike-splashes-44b-on-zscaler-customers-brace-for-the-bill/5311877

[TAM METİN - 703 kelime]
CrowdStrike splashes $44B on Zscaler, customers brace for the bill

Record-breaking cybersecurity deal promises tighter integration, but sysadmins fear higher prices and vendor lock-in

Prolific Microsoft 0-day hunter drops CrowdStrike Falcon exploit PoC
September 3, 2026

Cops, CrowdStrike disrupt Sality botnet by poisoning the network and diverting into sinkholes
September 2, 2026

Google goes it alone with a new cybercrime crew taxonomy
July 27, 2026

Self-destructing Mistic backdoor linked to access broker selling corporate footholds to ransomware gangs
June 26, 2026

CrowdStrike, Google shatter Glassworm botnet
May 27, 2026

CrowdStrike has agreed to buy Zscaler for $43.9 billion in what would be the biggest cybersecurity acquisition on record, and customers are already bracing for pricier renewals.

The all-stock deal, announced on Monday, will see Zscaler shareholders receive 1.135 CrowdStrike shares for every share they own, representing a 34 percent premium on Friday's closing price. The transaction dwarfs even Google's $32 billion acquisition of cloud security upstart Wiz.

Buying Zscaler gives CrowdStrike a sizeable foothold in the network security market, where it will be going up against the likes of Palo Alto Networks and Fortinet. The plan is to combine Falcon's endpoint protection capabilities with Zscaler's zero-trust network security technology, giving customers a single platform for securing devices and the connections between them.

Naturally, there's an AI angle to the biggest cyber deal in history. CrowdStrike reckons owning both technologies will allow it to keep tabs on what AI agents are doing on corporate devices, where they're connecting, and what data they're getting their hands on.

CrowdStrike CEO George Kurtz described the proposed combination as "cybersecurity’s operating system."

The acquisition also gives CrowdStrike's salespeople plenty to get excited about. Around 8,000 Zscaler customers don't currently buy CrowdStrike's wares, and the companies already have more than 2,000 customers in common. CrowdStrike reckons it can boost its annualized revenue by around $1 billion within 30 months of closing the deal by selling more products to the two companies' existing customers.

Some of those customers, however, don't sound particularly keen on being cross-sold to. Over on Reddit's r/sysadmin subreddit, news of the purchase was met with a familiar set of concerns from IT professionals who've lived through big-ticket enterprise software acquisitions before.

"As a zscaler customer, this does worry me. Cost increase, forced move to Falcon, integration issues, etc," wrote one sysadmin.

Another, whose organization already uses both vendors' products, added: "I think they're both great products but I imagine they'll jack up the price for our next renewal. Fun."

CrowdStrike intends to make Zscaler's products available through its Falcon Flex licensing scheme, which allows customers to commit to a certain level of spending and pick and choose the products they deploy. Monday's acquisition announcement didn't spell out what the deal would mean for customers who want to continue using Zscaler alongside a rival endpoint security product.

Others raised concerns about the fate of Red Canary, the managed detection and response specialist snapped up by Zscaler in August 2025. CrowdStrike already operates a competing service in Falcon Complete.

There may also be some nervous employees at both companies. CrowdStrike is targeting $500 million in annual cost savings within two years of completing the acquisition, identifying datacenter infrastructure, supplier spending, and wider operations as areas where it can trim the fat. Neither company disclosed whether that would involve job cuts.

Then there's the matter of putting more of the world's corporate security infrastructure in the hands of CrowdStrike, whose botched July 2024 software update took down an estimated 8.5 million Windows machines, grounding flights and disrupting hospitals, banks, and businesses worldwide. The incident gave IT departments a rather painful lesson in the risks of relying heavily on a single technology supplier.

The deal is expected to close in the second half of CrowdStrike's fiscal 2028, assuming regulators and Zscaler shareholders give it the green light. Zscaler founder and CEO Jay Chaudhry will remain in charge of the business and take a seat on CrowdStrike's board.

CrowdStrike reckons the combined business will have more than $10 billion in annual recurring revenue when the deal closes. Judging by the reaction from some customers, they'll be keeping a particularly close eye on how much of that revenue comes from them. ®

[33] The Register - European Commission confirms personal data stolen in AI-assisted cyberattack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 12:32:41 +0100
Link: https://www.theregister.com/cyber-crime/2026/11/24/european-commission-confirms-personal-data-stolen-in-ai-assisted-cyberattack/5311834

[TAM METİN - 673 kelime]
European Commission confirms personal data stolen in AI-assisted cyberattack

EU Academy names and email addresses exposed, with users warned via website notice rather than contacted directly

This could mess with Thanksgiving, says VMware as it drops third critical patch bundle in a week
3 hours ago

'We are not ShinyHunters,' says criminal hacking crew that looks and acts just like ShinyHunters
12 hours ago

An AI coding agent, a browser, and a Mac tool found a financial firm's access key in under 6 minutes
16 hours ago

CrowdStrike splashes $44B on Zscaler, customers brace for the bill
20 hours ago

Orgs that leak data 'prioritize corporate interests over customers' says Have I Been Pwned’s Troy Hunt
1 day ago

The European Commission has confirmed that hackers made off with personal data in the AI-assisted cyberattack disclosed last week, but has relied on a website notice rather than contacting affected users directly.

Speaking at a press briefing on Monday, Commission spokesperson Thomas Regnier confirmed that EU Academy, an online learning platform offering courses on EU policies and initiatives, was among the systems compromised during the cyber campaign targeting the bloc's executive arm.

The stolen data includes the names and email addresses of people outside the EU institutions, though the Commission has yet to figure out how many users are affected or whether other personal information was compromised. Public access to the platform has been disabled as a precaution.

Despite confirming the theft, the Commission hasn't contacted affected individuals directly. Instead, it has posted a notice on the EU Academy website, a decision Regnier defended when quizzed by reporters on Monday.

"There was no reach out to individual users, but rather a general message to all users on the website out of transparency," Regnier said.

The notice warns users that they could find themselves on the receiving end of spearphishing attacks, impersonation attempts, and spam as a result of the breach. The Commission is also advising users not to hand over personal information to anyone who contacts them claiming to be involved in EU Academy activities.

The trouble with notifying users via the EU Academy website is that they've already been told not to come back until January. A separate notice says the platform will be offline from November 11 through January 14, 2027, while the Commission rebuilds it. When it reopens, users will have to register again, and any record of their previous coursework won't be transferred.

The Register has asked the Commission whether the downtime and the requirement to create new accounts are connected to the cyberattack.A spokesperson didn't answer those points.

The Commission has informed the European Data Protection Supervisor (EDPS), the watchdog responsible for policing EU institutions' handling of personal data.

The EDPS told The Register that the Commission had notified it of the incident in stages, but declined to say whether it considered the Commission's approach to notifying affected users sufficient.

"I can confirm that the EDPS has been informed and notified in phases by the European Commission," a spokesperson said. "Our colleagues remain in regular contact with EC services on the cyberattack and its implication. This is an ongoing case, so I cannot tell you more at this stage on the actions the EDPS might take as supervisory authority."

The Commission first publicly acknowledged the cyberattack last Thursday, almost a month after suspicious activity was detected on October 22. Several internal applications were temporarily taken offline, and while most had since been restored, some remained unavailable at the time of Thursday's briefing.

The Commission has confirmed the attackers used an AI-assisted hacking tool, and the incident comes amid mounting concerns over ARTEX, an AI-powered penetration testing framework that security researchers have linked to large-scale cyber espionage campaigns.

More than a month after the Commission first spotted the intrusion, Regnier was unwilling to say on Monday whether its security teams had finally seen off the hackers.

"As long as the investigation is ongoing, I will not make any conclusive statements at this stage on where we are," he said. ®
