[34] TechCrunch Security - Maple raises $5.3M to build AI products that protect your data, even from itself
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 21:19:52 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/24/maple-raises-5-3m-to-build-ai-products-that-protect-your-data-even-from-itself/

[TAM METİN - 1290 kelime]
As AI assistants evolve into more capable agents that can access our files, emails, calendar, browser, and more, the amount of trust we’re expected to place in AI companies is growing. One startup called Maple, co-founded by a former Apple engineer who worked on privacy-preserving technologies, is taking a different approach with its AI app, where users’ data remains private — even from the company itself.

“We wanted to build something that was not only encrypted but also could be proven, and that people could go look at the open-source code, and then they could look at what we call trusted execution environments,” explains Maple co-founder and CEO Mark Suman. “They can look at and verify the code that’s running on our servers, so they don’t really have to take our word for it.”

“They can audit everything themselves and know how their data is being handled,” he adds.

The idea of trust is Maple’s larger theme, as the startup proposes that there’s a way to build an AI app where consumers don’t have to trade privacy for convenience, as its competitors often require with their invasive privacy policies and data collection practices, and sometimes even training on users’ personal data.

Instead of asking users to trust that Maple won’t misuse their personal data, it’s attempting to build a business where it can’t access that data in the first place.

Like other AI apps on the market, Maple allows users to engage in a chat experience where they ask questions and get answers. Meanwhile, its newly launched desktop app, Maple Work, offers a more agentic experience where the AI can control your computer, work with your files and apps, and control your browser to get online tasks handled.

With the company’s recently closed $5.3 million round, it’s moving on to its next product — a cloud-based agent that will be able to continue its work even if your computer isn’t on. This addition would make Maple more competitive with other popular consumer AI services, like Meta’s Muse, Instinct, and other AI agents that work over chat.

Suman, who previously spent six years at Apple, already had a background where AI and privacy overlapped, he tells TechCrunch.

At Apple, he worked on AI and machine learning with a particular focus on computer vision. Given Apple’s stance on consumer privacy, the challenge he faced was building products that would protect people’s identity while benefiting from computer vision techniques. His co-founder, Anthony Ronning (CTO), has a background in defense tech, security, and networking.

Ronning started the company that later became Maple AI back in 2023, initially as a privacy-focused fintech. When Suman teamed up with Ronning in August 2024, the idea to create Maple AI was born. The following January, the product became available to the public as a mobile app.

The app offers a familiar interface for those used to AI chatbots, but under the hood it runs a variety of open models, including versions of DeepSeek, Kimi, GLM, Inkling from Thinking Machines, Google’s Gemma, and OpenAI’s GPT OSS. Users can pick and choose the models they want or, for a simpler experience, just toggle between a quick model and the more powerful one.

The models themselves run inside a trusted execution environment (TEE) — meaning a server with hardware encryption on it — within a U.S.-based data center. (Maple also offers support for European customers through a partner that runs its server in Europe.)

“We actually don’t have access to [the data] while it’s inside the TEE,” explains Suman. “The data gets encrypted first on your device using a unique password that’s only known by you, the user — we don’t have access to it. It then goes into this TEE, and it gets decrypted inside there. The AI does its thing, and then it re-encrypts it with your password and sends it back to you,” he continues. “So we provide an end-to-end experience that’s very easy for the user, but we actually don’t have access to the raw data as it’s in flight or even when it’s at rest,” he adds.

This allows Maple to protect its users’ data, even from outsiders — like if hackers breached its system or the government came with a subpoena, for instance. All it could do would be to hand over an encrypted file.

“We just simply don’t want access to it,” Suman says of customers’ data. “That’s not part of our business model. And we also don’t want it to be part of our threat model.”

With the new desktop app, Maple is looking to small businesses that may want to benefit from AI without the data privacy risks. Inside the app, there’s an integration store where users can connect with other apps, like their Gmail or calendar. The desktop app can also spin up a subagent that works inside another AI app you may have on your desktop, like Claude, Codex, or others that support the Agent Client Protocol (ACP). This allows customers to use their preferred AI app for specific tasks, without having to give it all their information — just what’s needed for the given task.

Next year, Maple plans to enter the enterprise market with pilot work, where it will offer its own version of zero data retention. (Maple doesn’t retain most of the customers’ data, but when it has to, that data is encrypted.) It’s now in the process of completing SOC 2 compliance and security audits to get ready for this expansion.

The company declined to share its registered customer numbers but noted it’s seen 60x growth over the past three months, as people have been looking for ways to increase their security with AI.

Maple currently generates revenue via subscriptions, which are comparable to those from other AI model providers. Its Pro plan is $20 per month, and it offers two Max plans, Max and Max Plus, at $100 and $200 per month, respectively.

The Austin- and San Francisco-based startup is a team of four and is actively hiring.

[35] TechCrunch Security - Dozens of oil and shipping tankers hacked in recent weeks, per report
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 21:09:04 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/24/dozens-of-oil-and-shipping-tankers-hacked-in-recent-weeks-per-report/

[TAM METİN - 497 kelime]
Dozens of oil and shipping tankers hacked in recent weeks, per report

U.S. officials suspect Iranian hackers may be behind as many as 50 cyberattacks targeting shipping vessels in recent weeks, according to a report by The Wall Street Journal.

The ships carrying oil, liquefied gas, and other explosive chemicals were hacked as they traveled across Asia and Europe. Several oil tankers headed toward the U.S. coast in August also had their navigation systems breached, prompting cybersecurity teams within the FBI and Coast Guard to board the vessels.

The report said officials are investigating if Iran is expanding its recent wave of hacks against the U.S. by targeting large vessels to sow fear among shippers, disrupt routes, or to cause an explosion at a port. (Ships have backup controls in the event that automated systems are hacked or fail.)

Hackers may have been abusing a bug dubbed FortiBleed, which allows hackers to break into networks by compromising vulnerable Fortinet-made routers. A joint FBI-Coast Guard security alert from September warned that Fortinet routers are used on shipping vessels.

Iran has been firing back on all sides since the start of the U.S./Israel-led war earlier this year, which killed the country’s supreme leader. Many of Iran’s retaliations have been in the form of cyberattacks, with U.S. officials blaming Iran for hacks targeting hundreds of American water providers and U.S. medical tech companies.

A spokesperson for the FBI did not respond to a request for comment about the shipping hacks or whether it had a more accurate figure.

[36] TechCrunch Security - UK warns AI-driven cyberattacks are exposing years of weak security
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 18:02:59 +0000
Link: https://techcrunch.com/2026/11/24/uk-warns-ai-driven-cyberattacks-are-exposing-years-of-weak-security/

[TAM METİN - 643 kelime]
The U.K. government’s cybersecurity unit says it has seen a “marked rise” in AI-driven cyberattacks in recent months, as hackers increasingly rely on autonomous hacking tools to identify and exploit simple weaknesses to break into systems and networks.

The U.K. National Cybersecurity Center (NCSC) said it has seen several cyber incidents since September involving autonomous hacking tools, such as penetration testing tools used by defenders to stress-test their cybersecurity defenses. These incidents have caused intrusions at organizations and resulted in downtime, the unit said in a blog post.

The NCSC says that while none of the vulnerabilities being exploited are new, the speed at which these AI tools are able to carry out simple hacks has changed.

The rise in AI-related cyberattacks coincides with the broader release earlier this year of open source autonomous penetration testing tools like Artex and Cairn, among others, which anyone can download and use at no cost. The U.S. Federal Bureau of Investigation recently warned that malicious hackers are using AI penetration testing tools to carry out cyberattacks at unprecedented speeds.

While cyberattacks and intrusions are on the rise, the message from London is that many of these attacks are preventable with basic cybersecurity defenses and best practices.

“Notably, the issues being exploited currently are not novel memory corruption vulnerabilities (or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities), but unmitigated weaknesses that are often the result of years of technical debt that organisations have accumulated,” the NCSC wrote.

The unit said that agentic hacking tools often look for exposed or internet-facing systems, then quickly run through lists of weak or easy-to-guess passwords or abuse API endpoints often used by developers to access data.

The NCSC said that AI “does not make established cyber security controls obsolete,” but rather that “building strong foundations has never mattered more.”

The unit added that AI has made it easier to find vulnerabilities and faster to exploit them, warning that weaknesses that have gone unnoticed for years can now be found in minutes.

[37] CSO Online - Despite doom and gloom headlines, AI attacks are thin on the ground
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 08:25:00 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250533/despite-doom-and-gloom-headlines-ai-attacks-are-thin-on-the-ground.html

[TAM METİN - 1147 kelime]
The drumbeat of apocalyptic warnings about AI-powered cyberattacks may be constant, but the biggest threats are still coming through familiar channels.

When Anthropic announced its Mythos AI model, it was touted as being so cyber capable that only a handful of organizations could have early access to harden their defenses before the public — and attackers — got their hands on the technology.

Soon after, AI companies, one after the other, warned that their AI models were smart enough to discover previously unknown exploits and escape containment.

And a new worldview took hold, that security organizations were soon to see a wave of new kinds of attacks, never seen before, and every company was at risk — unless it immediately started using the latest and greatest AI to defend against all these new threats.

Has that happened? Well, kind of.

What CISOs are seeing

In the first quarter of 2026, RSAC asked Fortune 1000 CISOs whether they’d seen definitive evidence of AI-powered attack techniques being used against their companies, beyond social engineering, and 44% said yes.

“We asked the same question in Q4 of a similar cohort,” says Laura Koetzle, RSAC’s head of community research. According to preliminary results — the survey isn’t complete yet — nearly 58% now say they’ve seen evidence of AI-powered attack techniques.

As a result, the majority of CISOs are revamping their vulnerability management programs, according to the forthcoming survey. But they’re not upending how they approach cybersecurity, Koetzle adds. “The same foundational principles still apply.”

The main difference today is in speed and quantity, she says.

Lincoln Wallen, CTO at Framestore, an Academy Award–winning visual effects company, isn’t turning a blind eye to what AI could be used for by attackers.

“We take the potential very seriously,” he says. But he’s taking an old-school approach to dealing with this new threat. “We’re putting a lot of emphasis on cleanliness, hygiene, and patching.”

So far, the signs of AI use have been limited to more sophisticated phishing attacks, he says.

“They feel anecdotally more well put-together, more convincing, involving many more disparate elements of data,” he says. “That’s the most concrete thing that we’ve seen.”

To deal with that, the company has updated its anti-phishing training.

Another area that Framestore is investing in is more efficient and prioritized patching and more frequent penetration testing.

Enhanced productivity but familiar threats

The data seems to support Framestore’s approach, even if the headlines don’t.

Last week, headlines quoted Google’s announcement that “the era of autonomous attacks had arrived” — a reference to Google’s original blog post teaser to its latest AI Threat Tracker report.

But in the actual report, John Hultquist, chief analyst at the Google Threat Intelligence Group, seemed to undercut this top line teaser, saying: “For the most part, attackers aren’t using new groundbreaking methods to break into systems — they are still going after known, unfixed issues, just with much greater speed and scale.”

(Google later changed its headline to “Outpace automated cyber threats with security built for nonstop defense.”)

According to Google’s report, the mechanics of intrusion remain the same. Even attackers using new AI-powered attack frameworks are using them to leverage compromised credentials and unpatched systems. It’s the speed and scale at which these frameworks can be used that’s the challenge.

In fact, using LLMs can even hurt the attackers, as the frameworks “trade stealth for velocity,” researchers say. The attacks can be “forensically noisy” and can hallucinate non-existent details or targets. “Simultaneously, the lower barrier to entry has fueled a wave of LLM-generated exploits that are claimed to work but don’t.”

Or, as Sergej Epp summed up as a takeaway from a recent Cloud Security Alliance CISO huddle call on autonomous attacks, “detecting these attacks is — at least for now — not the hard part. They make a lot of noise, like 1000 interns.”

Austin Berglas, managing director and head of cyber operations at Nardello & Co., a global investigations firm, says he’s not seeing AI-discovered zero days or self-replicating AI agents out in the wild.

“This is one of those examples that is still in the lab environment,” he says.

Most attackers are using AI the way everyone does, he says. “Just like individuals use it for their daily productivity and efficiency, same with the bad actors. They’re able to scale, do it cheaper, do it quicker. And you’re seeing people who typically wouldn’t have the technical chops to do something, who are now able to do it because AI can do the technical side.”

The industry is seeing evidence of faster weaponization of vulnerabilities, confirms Gartner analyst John Watts. “Where it starts to have less evidence is for fully autonomous attacks. What happened with Hugging Face is a fully autonomous attack, and that isn’t that common.”

How CISOs should respond

But if the wave of autonomous attacks and AI-powered malware hasn’t yet fully arrived, what should CISOs do given the reality on the ground?

“The feedback that we get from our client base is that they believe there’s a lot of value in using the controls they already have,” Gartner’s Watts says.

“The wake-up call for the practitioners is to get busy with the patching,” confirms Paul Webber, an analyst at Software Analyst Cyber Research, adding that security by obscurity is no longer an option.

The biggest problem enterprises are facing, he notes, is that they need better automation to handle the increased workload, and the tools aren’t yet where they need to be.

“People need more trust in the security tools so they can move from human-in-the-loop to human-on-the-loop towards fully autonomous response,” Webber says. “And that’s going to take some discipline, some standardization, and some proof in the efficacy of the security tools themselves. At the moment, they’re neither built to a specific standard or definition and people are being asked to make purchasing decisions without physical proof that the tools can do what they purport to do.”

And, in some ways, the much-lauded defensive AI capabilities can make things worse — such as by exposing legacy vulnerabilities that companies now have to hurry up and patch, on top of their already large backlog.

“This is a conversation that we’re actively having right now,” says Christina Powers, partner and cybersecurity consulting leader at West Monroe, a management consulting firm.

“We’re working with a mid-sized bank, and they are just completely lost on how to keep up with the number of vulnerabilities that they got,” she says. “They’re sitting on millions of vulnerabilities. They don’t even know where to start. There’s a bit of analysis paralysis that’s happening because there’s so much information coming in.”

The tools are improving all the time, Powers says, but she recommends security leaders not to wait for better technology to come along.

“If you’re sitting and waiting, you become more of a target because you’re falling behind,” she says.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4250533/despite-doom-and-gloom-headlines-ai-attacks-are-thin-on-the-ground.html, csoonline.com, 25.11.2026)

[38] CSO Online - Cisco and OpenAI dispatch AI agents to shield vulnerable systems before permanent patches arrive
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 04:04:12 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4252893/cisco-and-openai-dispatch-ai-agents-to-shield-vulnerable-systems-before-permanent-patches-arrive-2.html

[TAM METİN - 1009 kelime]
A new open source project, Shirudo, generates and deploys temporary security mitigations directly into running Linux systems, with testing, verification, and human approval designed into the process.

Autonomous AI agents are rapidly and dramatically transforming the cybersecurity landscape for both defenders and adversaries.

In hopes of giving security teams a head start and pitting machine against machine, Cisco and OpenAI have released a new open source project, dubbed Shirudo (Japanese for “shield”). The Shirudo platform launches small, temporary mitigations to help protect systems until patches can be deployed. AI agents can replace flawed code with fixed code, which human teams can test and approve (or not), then deploy, setting boundaries and holding onto the keys, with no need for a hard reset.

The project has already been proven to block a Java4Shell exploit attempt against the search and analytics platform Elasticsearch, while the site continued to run as usual.

“For years, we’ve treated security like a coat of paint,” Cisco president and CPO Jeetu Patel wrote in a blog post. “You first build infrastructure, then wrap security around it.”

But, he asserted, this approach is “fundamentally mismatched” to AI. Security should live inside the infrastructure itself. “If protection sits outside the system, it’s always a step behind,” he said.

He noted that AI agents are getting better at discovering security vulnerabilities; for instance, startup Calif used AI to help discover the PHP2Shell flaw and a major Java bug.

Dovetailing with this, the agents themselves are both a risk vector and an adversarial actor. Attackers are using swarms of them to exploit discovered flaws; with both PHP2Shell and Java4shell, campaigns were underway “within a day of disclosure, if not before,” he pointed out. Flaws unfold over minutes and hours, as opposed to the days and weeks of the past, but permanent fixes take time to build, test, and roll out.

“We need ways to shield systems and buy our teams time to get patches in place,” Patel said.

Making the infrastructure secure from the start

Shirudo combines Cisco’s infrastructure with OpenAI’s frontier models and AI research. It is built on extended Berkeley Packet Filter (eBPF), which supports the running of small, sandboxed programs inside a Linux kernel without changing source code or loading additional modules.

“This makes it possible to observe suspicious activity and apply targeted security protections to running systems,” OpenAI explained in its blog post announcing the project.

Speaking in their own language, guardian agents investigate and detect flaws and build shields, then test whether they stop the attack, all without disrupting the targeted service. The shields are cryptographically signed and only loaded when verified and deemed safe. Linux’s eBPF verifier checks eBPF programs against safety rules, and kernel security controls restrict unauthorized changes.

Shirudo also uses a Trusted Platform Module (TPM) to support a verifiable record of protections being added or removed.

All this takes place even if attackers have already gained access to a system.

Further, self-defending agents can monitor for false positives, performance, and overall system health, and incorporate red-team findings and other feedback as well as signals for continuous improvement. Evidence backing every control travels across the system.

The idea, according to Cisco and OpenAI, is to make the infrastructure itself secure by default. “We want to strengthen the operating system foundations needed to deploy these protections at scale, including hardening the kernel and core services, limiting workload permissions, and making updates and recovery safer,” OpenAI wrote in its post.

Shirudo has its roots in Tetragon, an eBPF-based security platform developed and open-sourced by Isovalent, which was acquired by Cisco in 2024. It is already incorporated into Cisco’s Live Protect capability.

But Tetragon has been rewritten for the agentic era; its previous iteration pointed AI to human-oriented consoles, configuration files, and policy languages. In that workflow, the agent thought in machine code, which was then decoded for humans and processed, then translated back again into machine code. With Shirudo, agents interact in their natural language, which is code.

“Agents are different,” Isovalent co-founder Thomas Graf and its chief open source officer Liz Rice noted in a blog post: They are very good at generating code, can absorb huge amounts of context quickly, and “[don’t] mind reverse engineering several million lines of assembly code.”

In one controlled testing environment, researchers fed information about the Java4Shell vulnerability into Shirudo, which automatically created a shield against exploitation, generating a mitigation in Java bytecode which was injected into any Java app and added validation on the base64 input to guard against exploit attempts by threat actors.

Graf and Rice explained that agent loops can either be tasked to actively search for new vulnerabilities on their own, or work off external sources like newly-available security disclosures. Agents can generate mitigation code, reproduce and validate exploits, and determine the most effective patching measures. This step is repeated until the agent identifies a successful path. The shield is then signed and securely rolled out to vulnerable nodes; it retires when a patch is installed.

“This step is continuously monitored and repeated to achieve the highest possible security score while closely monitoring the health of applications,” Graf and Rice explained. It can be designed to be “more or less autonomous.”

Once mitigation shields are deployed, the system employs a “hit” count of active exploitation attempts, so that exploits can be investigated on demand.

Ultimately, the Shirudo project is only the beginning, Graf and Rice noted, but it already shows “true potential” and the hope is it will continue to evolve in the open source ecosystem. “We believe that building secure infrastructure for the AI era is an industry-scale problem,” they said.

Shirudo is licensed under BSD 2-Clause, a permissive open-source software license approved by the Open Source Initiative. This allows anyone to use, modify, and redistribute software, including in proprietary or commercial products, with minimal restrictions. Its BPF programs are dual licensed under GPL and BSD.

Developers, security researchers, and infrastructure operators are encouraged to inspect the code, test outcomes, and contribute to the project’s development.

This article originally appeared on InfoWorld.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4252893/cisco-and-openai-dispatch-ai-agents-to-shield-vulnerable-systems-before-permanent-patches-arrive-2.html, csoonline.com, 25.11.2026)

[39] CSO Online - Attackers exploited an Azure API Management flaw to steal secrets, Microsoft warns
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 11:48:59 +0000
Link: https://www.csoonline.com/article/4252513/attackers-exploited-an-azure-api-management-flaw-to-steal-secrets-microsoft-warns.html

[TAM METİN - 842 kelime]
Norway’s security agency links the flaw to attacks on two energy companies and tells all customers to assume their secrets are exposed.

Microsoft is warning customers that attackers exploited a flaw in Azure API Management Classic tiers to steal stored secrets, an intrusion that Norway’s National Security Authority has linked to breaches at two energy companies and says organizations should assume exposed credentials were compromised.

Microsoft said in an Azure Service Health security advisory sent to customers that it identified the vulnerability on Nov. 18 and deployed a mitigation the following day. The advisory, tracking ID MZK9-GH0, said the exposure began before Microsoft found the flaw, reported Bleeping Computer.

The company said in the advisory that “an unauthorized party could have used the issue to access secrets stored in Azure API Management Classic instances.”

The potentially exposed information includes secrets stored in Named Values, client secrets associated with identity providers and OpenID Connect providers, subscription keys, and tokens used to call direct management APIs, the report added.

Microsoft warned that its mitigation does not invalidate previously compromised credentials and that affected customers must rotate potentially exposed secrets and investigate suspicious activity. The advisory said only the Classic tiers of the service are affected, not Azure API Management v2, the report added.

The potential exposure was not limited to a theoretical security risk. Norway’s National Security Authority (NSM) subsequently linked cyberattacks against two energy companies to the vulnerability, providing evidence of exploitation in an operational environment.

Norway tells customers to assume exposure

In a security advisory published November 20 and updated November 23, NSM said an unidentified actor had actively targeted access tokens, secrets, and Named Values stored in Azure API Management.

“Earlier this week, NSM became aware of a vulnerability in Azure API Management (APIM) that affects several Norwegian businesses,” NSM said in the advisory, translated from Norwegian.

It said the vulnerability should now be closed, but asked organizations to take measures anyway.

“Anyone with Azure API Manager must assume that their secrets and named values ​​are out of whack,” the advisory added. “This means that the business must roll back all tokens and secrets.”

In the advisory, NSM’s director of operational cybersecurity, Martin Albert-Hoff, said the vulnerability was first discovered by Norway’s energy sector.  The agency identified Equinor and Skagerak Energi among the affected organizations and said the vulnerability had also affected organizations in other countries and sectors.

Equinor disclosed a cyberattack against some of its IT systems on November 17. In an update published November 20, the company said the attack had been stopped, production was running normally, and there was no indication that data had been stolen or altered.

A day later, Skagerak Energi reported an attempted cyberattack against some of its IT systems. The company said the incident had not affected its operations or production and that there was no indication that information had been compromised.

NSM warned that public disclosure of the vulnerability could encourage additional threat actors to attempt exploitation.

“When a vulnerability becomes known, multiple actors may attempt to exploit it,” the agency said in its advisory (translated from Norwegian), adding that the rapid escalation in the use of artificial intelligence was affecting the threat environment.

Neither Microsoft’s advisory nor NSM’s notice includes a CVE identifier for the flaw.

Urgent need to rotate credentials

Microsoft’s mitigation closed the unauthorized management-plane access path, but it advised customers to investigate whether their APIM instances were accessed before the fix and whether stored credentials could have been exposed, the report added.

In a November 21 post on its Azure Integration Services blog, Microsoft published guidance on identifying and rotating credentials configured in Azure API Management.

The guidance covers subscription keys, Named Values containing secrets, backend credentials, OAuth 2.0 and OpenID Connect client secrets, connection strings, and credentials written directly into API policies.

Microsoft recommended that organizations rotate credentials at the systems where they were originally issued, rather than simply updating their values in APIM.

“Updating the value in API Management alone does not invalidate the old credential,” the company said in the blog post, as per the report.

NSM has issued more extensive recommendations, advising organizations using Azure API Management to assume that their secrets and Named Values may have been exposed and to rotate all associated tokens and secrets.

The agency also published a patch check. According to NSM, instances on the 0.52.x branch are patched from build 31936, and those on 0.53.x are from build 31938. V2 and Consumption tier instances are not vulnerable, the agency said.

Organizations with unpatched instances should contact Microsoft directly, and those that see signs of compromise should contact NSM, the advisory said.

Cloud security researcher Anders Kristiansen, who published detection and hunting guidance after the advisories, said patching alone is not enough.

“If you run APIM, patching isn’t the end of it,” Kristiansen wrote in a blog post.

His rotation list includes backend credentials, app registration secrets, subscription keys, the Key Vault secrets the service references, and tenant access keys. Microsoft did not immediately respond to CSO’s request for comment.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.csoonline.com/article/4252513/attackers-exploited-an-azure-api-management-flaw-to-steal-secrets-microsoft-warns.html, csoonline.com, 24.11.2026)

[40] Dark Reading - Cyber Insurers See New Divide: AI Defense 'Haves' &amp; 'Have Nots'
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 15:00:00 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/cyber-insurers-see-new-ai-divide

[TAM METİN - 3000 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
Cyber insurance providers are approaching 2027 facing a fundamental challenge. Claims rose 40% in 2024 and another 20% in 2025, even as premiums have stayed largely flat. This trend is something both Coalition and Travelers say isn't sustainable; and at the center of that shift is AI, which is reshaping the threat landscape not by inventing new attack types, but by making existing ones — ransomware, business email compromise, and phishing — dramatically easier to execute at scale.

In this episode of Dark Reading Confidential, Dark Reading media and projects director [Becky Bracken](https://www.darkreading.com/author/becky-bracken) and Dark Reading senior news director [Rob Wright](https://www.darkreading.com/author/robert-wright) sit down with Lauren Winchester, vice president of cyber-risk services at Travelers; and Daniel Woods, head of emerging risk at Coalition, to unpack what the data actually shows. The conversation moves past the headline-grabbing surge in disclosed vulnerabilities to a more nuanced picture: exploitation is rising, but not as sharply as disclosure volume, while AI-powered phishing kits are bring sold SaaS-style to criminals with little technical skill, driving a measurable spike in BEC and ransomware activity.

Related:[US Cyber Director Pick Signals Shift Toward Expertise](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/us-cyber-director-pick-signals-shift-toward-expertise)

The two insurers also agree on where the real exposure lies: not necessarily with large enterprises, which have the resources to track threat intelligence and patch quickly, but with small and mid-sized businesses that often lack an in-house IT team altogether. That awareness gap, they argue, is where insurers now have to step in — not just to underwrite risk, but to actively help policyholders defend their organizations against it.

For more Dark Reading insights, check out [all of our podcasts](https://www.darkreading.com/podcasts), and subscribe to our [daily and weekly newsletters](https://dr-resources.darkreading.com/free/w_defa3135/prgm.cgi?a=1)!

[41] Dark Reading - Satellites Have a Ground-Level Cybersecurity Problem
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 22:05:34 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/satellites-ground-level-cybersecurity-problem

[TAM METİN - 982 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
4 Min Read

![Image 1: Satellite hovering over Earth.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt6866e786d2fd0c3c/6b05fc1bdcebc97b86eb1502/satellite_Y_alax_shutterstock.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: Y'alax via Shutterstock

The satellites circling Earth may seem far removed from the reach of cyberattackers, but the ground-based networks and infrastructure supporting them have long offered plenty of ways in. That exposure is now becoming increasingly consequential as the number of satellites in orbit and the services depending on them grow rapidly.

In a [new analysis](https://www.recordedfuture.com/blog/cyber-threats-space-race) of cyber threats emerging around the booming space ecosystem, Recorded Future's Insikt Group warned that in 2025 alone, organizations and nations around the world placed 4,517 satellites in orbit; 87% of them are commercially owned, contributing to a $686 billion space economy that is growing rapidly.

## Space Cyber Is a Very Terrestrial Problem

Satellite services now underpin a growing range of everyday services and critical operations, from communications and navigation to shipping and infrastructure monitoring. As dependence on them grows, so does the potential [fallout from a cyberattack](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/white-house-cybersecurity-space/646417/) that disrupts them, Recorded Future researchers warned. The growing militarization of space and the emergence of orbital AI data centers, such as those that Google and SpaceX are exploring with [Project Suncatcher](https://blog.google/innovation-and-ai/models-and-research/google-research/google-project-suncatcher-facts/) and [Starmind](https://www.spacex.com/spacexsi/starmind), respectively, introduces another [layer of risk](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/space-final-frontier-cyberattacks), the company said.

Related:[What We Missed: Nvidia CEO Scandal Rocks AI Industry](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/what-we-missed-nvidia-ceo-scandal-rocks-ai-industry)

Recorded Future pointed to several incidents where attackers exploited weaknesses in the ground-based systems supporting satellite communications to cause widespread disruption. One of the most consequential examples was in February 2022, just before Russia invaded Ukraine. Russian attackers used a destructive, data-wiping malware, dubbed [AcidRain](https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/acidrain-a-modem-wiper-rains-down-on-europe/), to disable tens of thousands of modems connected to Viasat's KA-SAT satellite broadband network, after first gaining access to the network through a misconfigured VPN appliance. The disruption affected Ukrainian military communications as well as ordinary customers across Europe. In Germany, some 5,800 wind turbines lost their remote monitoring connections.

A similar pattern emerged in attacks against Iran's commercial shipping fleet in 2025, when a group calling itself [Lab-Dookhtegan](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/hackers-knocked-out-iran-ship-comms) disabled communications aboard dozens of tankers and cargo vessels. The attackers reportedly gained access through Fanava Group, an Iranian IT provider serving the fleet, and obtained administrator-level control of shipboard Linux systems. They used that access to disable satellite communications software and software for identifying and tracking ship movements.

Recorded Future also pointed to an incident last August, where an oil tanker lost communications for about 30 hours after attackers seemingly compromised the ship's onboard network. Investigators focused on the satellite terminals aboard the ship and the remote-maintenance systems connecting them to shore as possible points of entry, the company noted.

Related:[Qwen Incidents Show Self-Hosted AI Models Need Stronger Steering](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/qwen-incidents-self-hosted-ai-models-stronger-steering)

"In none of these cases did anyone touch an object in orbit. The attackers went for the vendor, the terminal, the management plane," Recorded Future explained.

Even as geopolitical rivalries fuel a new space race, the basics remain broken. "Every satellite has a terrestrial address: the ground stations that command it, the links that carry its telemetry, the user terminals at the edge, and the software supply chain behind it all. That is where attackers go, because that is where the doors are," according to the firm.

Its report dovetails with research by teams at the University of California, San Diego, and the University of Maryland. Using a satellite dish costing just $800, the researchers found that roughly half of the geostationary satellites they could observe were transmitting data unencrypted. The exposed traffic included telecommunications backhaul, corporate communications, and military data.

## US Warns on Satellite's Stratospheric Cyberattack Surface

The US National Security Agency has also warned of the [expanding attack surface](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/outer-space-next-attack-surface) created by the proliferation of low-Earth-orbit (LEO) satellite networks. In a [joint advisory](https://www.nsa.gov/Press-Room/Press-Releases-Statements/Press-Release-View/Article/4443075/nsa-and-asds-acsc-release-joint-guidance-on-leo-satcom-system-risks-and-mitigat/) with the Australian Signals Directorate and other international partners, the NSA examined security risks across the satellite communications ecosystem. This included satellites, ground stations and control centers, user terminals, end-user devices, radio links, and third-party suppliers. The agencies highlighted threats ranging from radio-frequency jamming, signal spoofing, and interception to weaknesses in ground infrastructure and user equipment.

Related:[Strike Graph Launches Integration AI to Automate Continuous Compliance Evidence Using Any Existing Tool](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/strike-graph-launches-integration-ai-to-automate-continuous-compliance-evidence-using-any-existing-tool)

"The ground segment is highly exposed to cyber threats due to its extensive connectivity with terrestrial networks," the NSA and its partners warned. "Ground segments are typically the most interconnected and vulnerable points in a space system." Highlighted risks included malware injection, social engineering, DoS attacks, identity-based attacks and poor user practices.

To address these risks, the advisory recommended continuous monitoring of ground systems, stronger endpoint security and access controls, and technical safeguards such as frequency-hopping signals, backup communications paths, and anti-jamming antennas to protect satellite links.

Recorded Future's own recommendations are for all stakeholders to begin treating space infrastructure as critical national infrastructure, holding vendors to stronger security standards, and implementing basic measures like encryption for satellite communication.

[![Image 2: Jai Vijayan](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt1a674355ce57f27b/64f14f228727730c7f926217/Jai-Vijayan.jpeg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/jai-vijayan)

His information security reporting has explored everything from ransomware, nation-state threats, and identity security to AI risk, critical infrastructure protection, software supply chain security, cloud security and emerging enterprise technologies.

Over the course of his career, Jai has written news stories, feature articles, survey reports, white papers, and e-books for enterprise and technology audiences. He has also moderated panel discussions and executive roundtables featuring CISOs, security researchers, and industry leaders.

Jai previously served as senior editor at Computerworld, where he covered information security and data-privacy issues. His work has also appeared in CSO Online, InformationWeek, The Christian Science Monitor Passcode, The Economic Times, and other publications.

His work has earned multiple industry honors, including a Joint ASBPE Excellence Award for Best Coverage of Government IT, and a Joint Jesse H. Neal Award for wireless LAN security coverage. Jai holds a Master’s degree in statistics from Bangalore University, and studied broadcasting and electronic communication at Marquette University in Milwaukee.

[42] Dark Reading - Webworm APT Slithers Into Networks With Customized SoftEther VPN
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 21:48:06 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/webworm-slithers-customized-softether-vpn

[TAM METİN - 656 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
2 Min Read

![Image 1: A snake on a laptop.](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blt90d6b1405473fe5b/6b05fadd5bf26ea3ca9d2638/snake_computer-the-lightwriter-GettyImages-530757188.jpg?width=1280&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)

Source: the-lightwriter via Getty Images

An infamous cyber-espionage group has added a new trick to its tool belt, in the form of VPN bridge obfuscation.

Rapid7 published research concerning Webworm, a [China](https://www.darkreading.com/cyber-risk/china-hackers-industrializing-botnets)-aligned advanced persistent threat (APT) group [first reported on in 2022](https://www.techtarget.com/news/Webworm-retools-old-RATs-for-new-cyberespionage-threat/838152/) and believed to be active since at least 2017. Webworm's activity predominately targets government organizations, [with an emphasis on Europe](https://www.darkreading.com/endpoint-security/chinas-webworm-discord-microsoft-graphs). The group has also been tracked as [Erudite Mogwai](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/longnosedgoblin-caught-snooping-on-asian-governments) and [Space Pirates](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/space-pirates-train-cyber-sabers-on-russian-serbian-organizations).

Webworm has been known to deploy the open source SoftEther VPN on previously compromised government networks. While SoftEther is a legitimate multiprotocol VPN, threat actors abuse its capabilities in attacks. Webworm actors in particular include a "VPN Bridge" component allowing them to link a victim's internal network directly to attacker-controlled servers to facilitate long-term access.

[The new research](https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/tr-spying-on-governments-no-malware-custom-webworms-toolkit-softether-bridge/), published on Nov. 19, covers what Rapid7 researcher and blog post author Milan Spinka called "a so-far unknown custom SoftEther-based tool, which implements various tricks to hide itself from security tools, and which we attribute to Webworm with moderate confidence."

Related:['TheMailer' Phishing Kit Puts AI in the Driver's Seat](https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/themailer-phishing-kit-ai-drivers-seat)

## Rebranding SoftEther as Something More Generic

Rapid7 has identified a range of samples of the tool, which is internally described as "NtMonitor." The tool is a modified build of SoftEther VPN Bridge compiled for Windows x64 and appears to be specifically designed to disguise legitimate SoftEther VPN software as a benign network monitoring utility so the attacker can maintain long-term, stealthy access to compromised network while evading detection tools made to recognize standard SoftEther software artifacts.

At its core, Spinka wrote, it appears the attacker "methodically analyzed SoftEther's detection surface and implemented counter-measures to all of the easy ways a detection engineer may target it."

Attackers changed the metadata and internal branding to refer to generic "Network Monitor Software," for example. Additionally, they changed internal string references to refer to "NtMonitor VPN" like debug banners, service names, registry pages, and firewall rule names (though this was inconsistently applied); renamed the HTTP endpoints used by the VPN protocol; changed the handshake watermark so standard SoftEther detection signatures wouldn't pick it up; stripped down a core file (hamcore.se2) and embedded it inside the executable; and renamed and encrypted SoftEther's plaintext configuration file with a custom cipher.

## Defending Against a Webworm Attack

Rapid7 attributed the software to Webworm with moderate confidence, due to alignment with previously reported Webworm tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTPs), as well as remote IP addresses traced to infrastructure repeatedly linked to Webworm activity in the past. Rapid7's research blog includes indicators of compromise and YARA rules for defenders.

Related:[Anthropic Finds Ingredients for Future AI Worms](https://www.darkreading.com/perimeter/anthropic-ingredients-future-ai-worms)

Dark Reading contacted Rapid7 for additional information, but the research team was unavailable at press time.

One of Webworm's primary initial access methods appears to be exploiting vulnerabilities through external Web-facing applications, based on [prior vendor research](https://proudsec.com/reports-pdf/webworm-apt.en.pdf) and the APT's [affinity for vulnerability scanner Nuclei](https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/webworm-new-burrowing-techniques/). Defenders wary of Webworm should prioritize internet-facing patching, Web Application Firewall (WAF) enforcement, and strict [network](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/aviation-cybersecurity-faa-tsa-gao-report/825416/) egress controls.

## About the Author

[![Image 2: Alexander Culafi](https://eu-images.contentstack.com/v3/assets/blt6d90778a997de1cd/blte0c88f087d1ad6f5/67c609df64f983454c3dd40b/1571751486317.jpg?width=400&auto=webp&quality=80&disable=upscale)](https://www.darkreading.com/author/alexander-culafi)

Senior News Writer, Dark Reading

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

He has received numerous awards, including TechTarget's Writer of the Year in 2022 as well as more than 10 Azbee awards for his reporting between 2022 and today.

[43] Dark Reading - 'God Mode' Flaws Could Wreak Border-Control Havoc
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 21:36:11 GMT
Link: https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/god-mode-flaws-wreak-border-control-havoc

[TAM METİN - 1156 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
Imagine trying to enter a country you've always wanted to visit, only to find yourself not only denied entry but potentially arrested because you've mistakenly ended up on a list of known terrorists.

This is one of several scenarios that could have unfolded if attackers had exploited flaws that independent security researcher [Eaton Zveare](https://github.com/EatonZ) discovered in the online passport and border-control systems of several countries: Nigeria, Ethiopia, Fiji, Zambia, Sri Lanka, and Ghana.

Though specific to each system, the flaws generally allowed unauthorized access to millions of travelers' [sensitive visa and passport data](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/qantas-airlines-breached-6m-customers) — potentially including biometric information such as fingerprints — and, in some cases, even granted access to administrative functions that could be used to approve or deny entry into a country.

Specifically, Zveare discovered critical [API vulnerabilities](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/api-security-losses-billions-complicated), including broken object-level authorization (BOLA), broken function-level authorization (BFLA), and missing authentication, in online systems these countries use to process visa applications and authorize travelers to enter.

Related:[Navigating Vulnerability Hysteria in the AI Era](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/navigating-vulnerability-hysteria-ai-era)

Many of the systems were built by third-party contractors with varying levels of expertise, which likely contributed to the relatively simple and easily avoidable security gaps, Zveare tells Dark Reading. In Nigeria's case, an over-reliance on AI to build the systems may also have introduced flaws into the code.

"I think they've gotten on the AI hype train, and they've kind of used it to build their border control a little bit," he says.

## Failure to Secure Critical Infrastructure

Taken together, the vulnerabilities — which should never exist in "the systems that are vital to national security" — highlight a broader failure to [secure critical national infrastructure](https://www.cybersecuritydive.com/news/wiz-ai-critical-infrastructure-scan-for-good/831255/), potentially with significant consequences for people traveling to these countries, Zveare says.

"The tip of the iceberg is not only viewing all the people who ever applied for these [visas and passports](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/identity-alone-wont-save-us-tsa-paradigm-mgm-hack), but actually being able to approve or deny applications that are currently coming in," he says, describing the potential for bad actors to exploit the flaws.

Zveare describes the level of access the flaws could provide as "God mode at the border" — also the title of [a session](https://blackhat.com/europe/briefings/schedule/?#god-mode-at-the-border-compromising-global-passport-visa-and-immigration-systems-56153) he will lead at Black Hat Europe next month about his findings. By exploiting the vulnerabilities, attackers could effectively "have the master key to these countries and their borders," potentially threatening the safety of both citizens and people entering the country. Zveare will disclose the details of the flaws during his session, which is scheduled for Dec. 10.

Related:[Java4Shell Flaw Threatens Global Enterprises, Java Application Ecosystem](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/java4shell-flaw-threatens-global-java-application-ecosystem)

## Bing Search Leads to "God Mode" Flaw Discovery

The discovery began almost by accident, when Zveare searched Microsoft Bing for the phrase "admin panel" about a year ago. One of the results led him to a private back-end portal belonging to [Nigeria](https://www.darkreading.com/cybersecurity-operations/nigeria-sovereign-cloud-cyber-national-security).

After investigating the portal, Zveare uncovered security flaws that gave him access to sensitive immigration systems. Intrigued by what he had found, he began looking for similar systems in other countries, focusing on online [passport-control and visa platforms](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/malawi-immigration-department-halts-services-amid-cyberattack) built using modern Web technologies such as Angular and React.

His search eventually led him to five more countries, where he discovered a range of [vulnerabilities](https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/old-unpatched-flaws-attackers-philippines-nuclear-agency), including missing authentication and broken access controls — the kinds of common Web flaws that you often see but ones that "you just can't have in critical systems like this," Zveare tells Dark Reading.

In Nigeria alone, Zveare identified roughly a dozen vulnerabilities, while the other countries had several each. He went on to develop exploits to demonstrate how the flaws could allow an attacker not only to view sensitive [personally identifiable information](https://www.techtarget.com/news/personally-identifiable-information-PII/837124/) (PII) belonging to visa applicants, but also to access administrative functions that could determine whether someone was granted entry into a country.

Related:[Major Brazilian Signature Apps Enable RCE & Fraud](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/major-brazilian-signature-app-rce-fraud)

The potential consequences extend well beyond disrupted travel plans and compromised personal information, Zveare says. One worst-case scenario would involve an attacker identifying a high-level executive or other person of interest who might be targeted for kidnapping or assassination, then accessing their travel itinerary, hotel information, and other details that could be used to track their movements.

"You would know exactly when they land, where they are staying, and how long they're staying," Zveare says. "I'm sure you can imagine what bad people could do with that info."

## Disclosure and Remediation

Because the systems were spread across multiple foreign countries, many of which rely on outside companies to build their online infrastructure, identifying the parties responsible for fixing the vulnerabilities proved trickier than a typical bug disclosure, Zveare says.

"So all these countries have their computer emergency response teams (CERTs)," he tells Dark Reading. "Unfortunately, a lot of them did not respond."

One country whose response was particularly strong was also the one whose systems had the most problems: Nigeria, which moved quickly to resolve the vulnerabilities. Its response team had "a generally excellent response," Zveare says.

Indeed, Nigeria is emerging as "the big tech hub of Africa," and he expected its authorities to be relatively on top of these issues, he says.

When a country's [CERT](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/ukraine-cert-phishing-campaign-poses-as-nations-security-service) failed to respond, Zveare had to dig deeper to identify the independent contractor that had built the system and contact the company directly. In many cases, this proved to be the quickest route to remediation.

Overall, response times ranged from a few days to more than six months. By now, however, "everything is pretty much fixed," he says.

## Crucial Contractor Vetting

While Zveare says all [the contractors](https://www.darkreading.com/application-security/contractor-software-targeted-mssql-loophole) he contacted were helpful and responsive in addressing the vulnerabilities, their security practices may have contributed to the problems in the first place.

"[The contractors] might have come in at the lowest bid, and that's what the country went with," Zveare says. "I feel as though, with how simple some of these flaws were, they might not have the most sophisticated security reviews."

One quick test Zveare uses when assessing a [Web API](https://www.darkreading.com/vulnerabilities-threats/cisco-zero-day-api-endpoint-authentication-issues) is to look for an administrative endpoint that retrieves a list of all users. "So I'll uncover that, and I'll just copy that endpoint into my browser and see what happens."

In the case of the vulnerable border-control systems, "a lot of the time, they would just return the entire users list because they don't check for the authentication token," he says.

The problems went beyond improper token handling, however. In some cases, authentication tokens were missing altogether, meaning sensitive endpoints could be accessed without the required authentication.

This lack of fundamental Web security points to a broader failure of oversight by national authorities when selecting the contractors entrusted with building and maintaining their systems, Zveare says. For [infrastructure as sensitive](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/poland-energy-attack-wind-solar-infrastructure) as border control systems, that lack of scrutiny is not only unacceptable but potentially dangerous.

"You have to properly vet these contractors because you're giving [your nation's security](https://www.darkreading.com/threat-intelligence/buckle-up-a-closer-look-at-airline-security-breaches) to them," Zveare says. "And if they don't know basic Web security, you're going to have a bad time."

[44] SecurityWeek - Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 08:12:24 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/palo-alto-networks-patches-fragnesia-flaw-used-to-root-firewalls/

[TAM METİN - 2891 kelime]
Palo Alto Networks Patches Fragnesia Flaw Used to Root Firewalls

A China-linked hacking group chained PHP2Shell with Fragnesia in AI-assisted attacks that hit 25 servers.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Palo Alto Networks on Tuesday announced PAN-OS updates that patch the Fragnesia Linux kernel vulnerability exploited to root its firewalls.
Tracked as CVE-2026-46300, Fragnesia was disclosed in May 2026 as a privilege escalation bug in the kernel’s XFRM ESP-in-TCP subsystem. It allows unprivileged attackers to overwrite sensitive system files to gain root permissions.



On October 26, cybersecurity firm Dream warned that a Chinese-speaking threat actor successfully exploited the critical PHP2Shell bugs and the Linux kernel weakness to hack two servers of a US government-affiliated organization.



As part of the AI-assisted campaign, the attackers compromised 25 servers (24 firewalls and one internal firewall management server), exploiting PHP2Shell to gain remote code execution (RCE), and executing a Fragnesia exploit to deploy webshells with root privileges.



On October 29, Palo Alto Networks confirmed that the threat actor hit PAN-OS firewalls with management interfaces exposed to the internet in attacks chaining PHP2Shell with Fragnesia.



By October 30, the company had released patches for PHP2Shell across all supported PAN-OS versions, closing the known initial-access path.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


“If you are running a fixed PAN-OS listed in the CVE-2026-109618 [PHP2Shell] advisory, you are protected,” Palo Alto Networks notes in a Tuesday advisory.



Regardless, the company released new PAN-OS versions that address CVE-2026-46300: 12.2.4-h3, 12.1.10-h6, 12.1.7-h15, 12.1.4-h15, 11.2.16-h3, 11.2.13-h6, 11.2.10-h19, 11.2.7-h25, 11.2.4-h25, 11.1.16-h6, 11.1.13-h18, 11.1.10-h38, 11.1.7-h14, 11.1.6-h42, 11.1.4-h40, 10.2.18-h15, 10.2.16-h14, 10.2.13-h29, 10.2.10-h45, and 10.2.7-h41.



Palo Alto Networks recommends applying the fixes for Fragnesia, although the only known way to exploit it against PAN-OS is to first exploit PHP2Shell via an internet-exposed management interface.



The company also recommends not allowing direct access to the management interface from the web. Instead, users should only allow access from the management network or route the access via a VPN.



“Exploitation of CVE-2026-46300 has been observed targeting the Palo Alto Networks management interface exposed to the internet,” Palo Alto Networks notes.

[45] SecurityWeek - Drupal Flaw Found by Anthropic Scanner Exploited in Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 08:01:06 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/drupal-flaw-found-by-anthropic-scanner-exploited-in-attacks/

[TAM METİN - 3476 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

Drupal Flaw Found by Anthropic Scanner Exploited in Attacks

CISA has added the vulnerability to its KEV catalog and instructed federal agencies to patch it within three days.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

A recently patched Drupal core vulnerability is being exploited in the wild, according to the cybersecurity agency CISA and Drupal developers.
The vulnerability, CVE-2026-132165, can be exploited for remote code execution. It was patched on November 18, and Drupal’s advisory says in-the-wild exploitation began shortly after.



“The Security Team has received a report this was exploited on live sites as early as 2026-11-19 19:00 UTC,” Drupal noted.



The discovery of the flaw is credited to Anthropic’s OSS Scanner, a free opt-in service that regularly scans enrolled open source projects for vulnerabilities.



Anthropic has not said how many vulnerabilities OSS Scanner has found since its October launch, but as of early November, it reported disclosing more than 5,000 vulnerabilities overall, across 680 open source projects.



CISA added CVE-2026-132165 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog on November 24, instructing federal agencies to address it by November 27. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


No public information appears to be available on attacks exploiting CVE-2026-132165. It’s unclear how many websites are affected.



The security hole affects the installer that ships with Drupal core, which enables users to set up a new site. An unauthenticated attacker — under certain conditions — can abuse the installer to reinstall Drupal, which can allow them to hijack the website or execute arbitrary code. 



“Some (but not all) exploit paths are mitigated if the web server cannot write to or change permissions on settings.php. In addition to upgrading, the Drupal Security Team recommends securing the Drupal installer after installation,” Drupal said.



Patches are included in Drupal 11.4.13, 11.3.22 and 10.6.22; end-of-life branches will not get a fix.



It’s worth noting that Drupal raised the vulnerability’s rating to ‘highly critical’ (CMSS score of 20/25) after it learned of the attacks. 



This is the third Drupal vulnerability exploited in the wild this year. Drupal warned in late October that CVE-2026-118539, a highly critical access bypass flaw in the Commerce Core module, had been exploited against online stores. 



In May, security firms reported seeing attacks exploiting a vulnerability tracked as CVE-2026-9082 against thousands of websites. 



Related: Apache Patches Critical HTTP Server RCE Found by Anthropic, Google
Related: VMware Updates Patch Critical ESX, vCenter Vulnerabilities
Related: Check Point Security Management Servers Hacked in AI-Assisted Campaign

[46] SecurityWeek - An Unclassified CIA System Was Breached in a Cyberattack, AP Source Says
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 22:41:58 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/an-unclassified-cia-system-was-breached-in-a-cyberattack-ap-source-says/

[TAM METİN - 2523 kelime]
An Unclassified CIA System Was Breached in a Cyberattack, AP Source Says

It was not clear what specific information was compromised or who was responsible.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

An unclassified CIA system was breached in a cyberattack, according to a person familiar with the incident who spoke to The Associated Press on condition of anonymity.
The breach did not involve any classified information and the issue has been resolved, said the person, who was not authorized to speak by name and spoke on condition of anonymity.



The breach affected a system that handles open source intelligence, which involves the collection and analysis of publicly available data from sources including the internet, commercial satellites and private data brokers. Though the information is generally public or semi-public, open-source information can give intelligence analysts significant insight on its own or in combination with intelligence obtained from classified spying programs.



It was not clear what specific information was compromised or who was responsible.



The breach was first reported by The New York Times on Tuesday.



The incident comes to light following a series of highly disruptive system failures that unnerved many Americans. An internal network the National Security Agency uses to communicate classified information was offline for a time last week, and the FBI is investigating an apparent hack of a national law enforcement database that it maintains.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Those incidents followed a nationwide ground stop that snarled air travel after air traffic controllers struggled to communicate with pilots. Apple, JP Morgan Chase and Microsoft also had systems go down this month.



The incidents have put a spotlight on the vulnerabilities inside critical infrastructure, banking systems and the digital tools that millions of people use daily.



President Donald Trump’s administration says the series of outages is not part of a coordinated cyberattack and dispatched Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton to calm Americans in appearances on Sunday morning news shows.



Clayton said the outages largely stem from the rush to patch and upgrade systems to stay ahead of hackers who are now empowered with sophisticated artificial intelligence tools to attack at an unprecedented scale.



Related: FBI Investigating Apparent Hack of Its Key National Criminal Justice Database, AP Source Says

President Donald Trump’s administration says the series of outages is not part of a coordinated cyberattack and dispatched Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton to calm Americans in appearances on Sunday morning news shows.



Clayton said the outages largely stem from the rush to patch and upgrade systems to stay ahead of hackers who are now empowered with sophisticated artificial intelligence tools to attack at an unprecedented scale.



Related: FBI Investigating Apparent Hack of Its Key National Criminal Justice Database, AP Source Says

It was not clear what specific information was compromised or who was responsible.



The breach was first reported by The New York Times on Tuesday.



The incident comes to light following a series of highly disruptive system failures that unnerved many Americans. An internal network the National Security Agency uses to communicate classified information was offline for a time last week, and the FBI is investigating an apparent hack of a national law enforcement database that it maintains.Advertisement. Risk managers are trained to spot weak signals, plan for disruption and make hard decisions under uncertainty. But the discipline looks different when the risk is not a system or an enterprise, but you.
(Steve Durbin)

[47] SecurityWeek - European Commission Uncovers Data Breach in Cyberattack Investigation
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 14:49:59 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/european-commission-uncovers-data-breach-in-cyberattack-investigation/

[TAM METİN - 2675 kelime]
European Commission Uncovers Data Breach in Cyberattack Investigation

Names and email addresses of EU Academy users were exposed in the AI-assisted cyberattack the European Commission detected in October.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

The European Commission (EC) on Monday confirmed that a recently disclosed AI-assisted cyberattack resulted in the compromise of personal information.
The attack, disclosed last week, prompted the temporary shutdown of several applications. At the time, the organization said it had found no evidence that personal information belonging to Commission staff had been compromised.



Spokesperson Thomas Regnier revealed during a press briefing on Monday that the investigation has since uncovered a data breach affecting EU Academy, an online learning platform used by people outside the EC to take courses and learn about European Union policies.



Compromised information includes names and email addresses of EU Academy users, but the full extent of the breach is still being investigated. 



Access to EU Academy has been suspended during the investigation and the European Data Protection Supervisor (EDPS) has been notified. Potentially affected individuals have not been informed directly.



“Important to keep in mind at this point in time that the exact scale of the data leak is still subject to the investigation, so there was no reach-out to individual users, but rather a general message to all users on the website out of transparency,” Regnier explained.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The cyberattack was detected on October 22 and the EC said last week that it had largely been contained. The spokesperson declined to comment on Monday on whether the incident was over, and said attribution could only be made once the investigation is concluded. 



Euronews reported that the Commission suspects a China-backed threat group. According to the publication, the attack has yet to be fully contained.



The EC said the intrusion involved AI, but it did not publicly identify the tool leveraged by the hackers. According to Euronews, the suspected China link is based partly on the use of Artex, an AI-powered penetration testing framework with Chinese roots that has been widely used by threat actors.



Related: Azure Flaw Exploited to Target Secrets at Norwegian Energy Firms
Related: Peninsula Energy Halts Trading After Cyber Incident Hits US Uranium Mine
Related: 1.5 Million Impacted by Data Breach at South Korean Electric Company KEPCO

[48] SecurityWeek - Anthropic Expands Enterprise Safeguards for Detecting AI Misuse
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 13:30:23 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/anthropic-expands-enterprise-safeguards-for-detecting-ai-misuse/

[TAM METİN - 3587 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

Anthropic Expands Enterprise Safeguards for Detecting AI Misuse

EFS helps zero-retention customers spot Claude misuse spread across many requests while keeping conversation data in their own cloud.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Anthropic on Monday announced the public beta of Enterprise Frontier Safeguards (EFS), a system designed to help organizations detect malicious use of AI.
Unveiled in September, EFS has been developed with input from over 100 Anthropic customers, with companies such as Stripe and Salesforce evaluating it in private beta. A quarter of Fortune 100 companies helped develop EFS.



According to Anthropic, existing Claude protections can identify potentially harmful requests made individually, but EFS adds a monitoring layer that can detect suspicious patterns across multiple requests that may appear benign when analyzed individually.



The system targets organizations that use Claude under zero data retention agreements and have so far relied only on classifiers that analyze each request in isolation.



EFS is designed to detect both deliberate misuse of AI, such as AI-powered cyberattacks, and incidents where AI agents go beyond what their operator intended, such as the cases where agents exploited vulnerabilities to gain access to real-world systems. 



With EFS, organizations store conversation data in their own cloud and protect it with their own encryption keys and access policies. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Neither Anthropic nor its cloud providers retain copies of the conversation data after it’s transferred to the customer’s storage. Anthropic receives monitoring metadata, but not the content of conversations. 



Security teams are provided reports on potentially harmful activity, which can be ingested via SIEM solutions. EFS is currently available via the Claude Platform, AWS, and Google Cloud, with Microsoft Foundry integration in the works.  



Guide to securing enterprise coding agents
Anthropic on Monday also published a guide to securing enterprise coding agents, with a focus on risks associated with privilege abuse, credential exposure, risky dependencies, prompt injection, and data exfiltration. 



Citing previously published testing results, the company said mechanisms designed to detect prompt injection reduced attack success rates from 90% to 28% on Fable 5.2 and from 55% to 11% on Opus 5.5.



Anthropic also highlighted the effectiveness of its auto mode classifier, which evaluates proposed actions against user intent.



In tests conducted this summer, auto mode, which is now enabled by default for interactive Claude Code sessions, blocked 89% of dangerous commands, compared to only 14% in the case of human reviewers. 



However, Anthropic recommends requiring human approval for high-risk actions and enforcing additional controls, such as sandboxing and network access restrictions.



Related: Anthropic Rolls Out Mythos 5.2 With Improved Bug Hunting, Fewer Sandbox Escapes
Related: OpenAI, Cisco Unveil Open Source Tool to Shield Unpatched Linux Systems

Related: OpenAI, Cisco Unveil Open Source Tool to Shield Unpatched Linux Systems

The system targets organizations that use Claude under zero data retention agreements and have so far relied only on classifiers that analyze each request in isolation.



EFS is designed to detect both deliberate misuse of AI, such as AI-powered cyberattacks, and incidents where AI agents go beyond what their operator intended, such as the cases where agents exploited vulnerabilities to gain access to real-world systems. 



With EFS, organizations store conversation data in their own cloud and protect it with their own encryption keys and access policies. Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


Neither Anthropic nor its cloud providers retain copies of the conversation data after it’s transferred to the customer’s storage. Anthropic receives monitoring metadata, but not the content of conversations. 



Security teams are provided reports on potentially harmful activity, which can be ingested via SIEM solutions. EFS is currently available via the Claude Platform, AWS, and Google Cloud, with Microsoft Foundry integration in the works.  



Guide to securing enterprise coding agents
Anthropic on Monday also published a guide to securing enterprise coding agents, with a focus on risks associated with privilege abuse, credential exposure, risky dependencies, prompt injection, and data exfiltration. 



Citing previously published testing results, the company said mechanisms designed to detect prompt injection reduced attack success rates from 90% to 28% on Fable 5.2 and from 55% to 11% on Opus 5.5.



Anthropic also highlighted the effectiveness of its auto mode classifier, which evaluates proposed actions against user intent.



In tests conducted this summer, auto mode, which is now enabled by default for interactive Claude Code sessions, blocked 89% of dangerous commands, compared to only 14% in the case of human reviewers. 



However, Anthropic recommends requiring human approval for high-risk actions and enforcing additional controls, such as sandboxing and network access restrictions.



Related: Anthropic Rolls Out Mythos 5.2 With Improved Bug Hunting, Fewer Sandbox Escapes
