[49] SecurityWeek - OpenAI, Cisco Unveil Open Source Tool to Shield Unpatched Linux Systems
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 12:20:41 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/openai-cisco-unveil-open-source-tool-to-shield-unpatched-linux-systems/

[TAM METİN - 2984 kelime]
Artificial Intelligence

OpenAI, Cisco Unveil Open Source Tool to Shield Unpatched Linux Systems

OpenAI and Cisco’s Shirudo uses AI-generated shields to block Linux exploits while patches are prepared, but it’s not yet production-ready.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

OpenAI and Cisco on Monday unveiled Shirudo, an open source security platform that uses AI agents to prevent the exploitation of vulnerabilities in Linux systems and applications.
The project is an AI-focused rewrite of Cisco-owned Isovalent’s Tetragon runtime security platform. Tetragon was open-sourced in 2022, and it also powers Cisco’s Live Protect vulnerability mitigation technology.



Shirudo is designed to help defenders address the challenges introduced by AI-accelerated vulnerability discovery and exploitation, which is increasing pressure on organizations to deploy patches quickly.



The tool leverages eBPF technology to modify Linux kernel behavior without restarting the OS. 



According to Cisco and OpenAI, agents can analyze vulnerability information, generate so-called ‘shields’ to prevent exploitation, and test their effectiveness without disrupting important services. Security teams still retain control over which shields are deployed.



Protections are digitally signed and verified before deployment, with mechanisms designed to prevent even attackers with root privileges from disabling kernel-level enforcement.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


In a controlled test targeting the recent Java4Shell vulnerability, a shield generated using OpenAI’s Codex blocked an exploitation attempt targeting an Elasticsearch instance without requiring a restart.



Shirudo is currently in pre-release development and not ready for production use. The tool requires specific Linux kernel capabilities, additional kernel patches, and TPM 2.0. 



OpenAI and Cisco hope that down the line Shirudo shields can be distributed alongside vulnerability disclosures to give organizations temporary protection until they can install permanent patches. 



Amiram Shachar, CEO and co-founder of cloud security firm Upwind, commented, “Writing the most sensitive kernel code on the fly and deploying it straight to runtime is a very risky concept. I love the idea, but doing it with open source is very risky.” 



“There are countless Linux versions and distributions this has to work across. It needs to be tested, packaged and automated before anyone should trust it in production,” Shachar added.



Related: AI Inference Providers Failed to Detect Hacking and Prompt Theft, Says Dragos
Related: Amazon: China-Linked Hackers’ AI Agents Exploited Medical Software Zero-Day
Related: Industry Reactions to Major Outages and AI Patching Debate: Feedback Friday

[50] SecurityWeek - Elecbrakes Bluetooth Vulnerabilities Expose Trailers to Forced Braking
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 10:44:44 +0000
Link: https://www.securityweek.com/elecbrakes-bluetooth-vulnerabilities-expose-trailers-to-forced-braking/

[TAM METİN - 4340 kelime]
Elecbrakes Bluetooth Vulnerabilities Expose Trailers to Forced Braking

Critical Bluetooth flaws in Elecbrakes controllers could let nearby attackers force trailer braking, potentially causing accidents.

Flipboard





Reddit





Whatsapp





Whatsapp





Email

Vulnerabilities discovered in Bluetooth-enabled brake controllers made by Australian firm Elecbrakes could allow hackers to manipulate trailer brakes, potentially causing accidents, researchers warned.
The affected products include the EB2, VM1 and ELBC 2000 brake controllers from Elecbrakes. According to the cybersecurity agency CISA, which last week published an advisory on the vulnerabilities, the affected brake controllers are mainly used in Australia and New Zealand.



The controllers regulate the braking force applied to trailers, with the EB2 unit mounted on the trailer and the VM1 unit on the towing vehicle. The devices can be controlled remotely via a special remote or a smartphone app. 



Researchers from Australian IT and cybersecurity consultancy Element Digital — with help from AI — discovered three vulnerabilities in the braking controllers, including two critical missing authentication and insufficient replay-attack protection issues. 



They demonstrated that an unauthenticated attacker who is within Bluetooth range of the targeted vehicle (up to 100 meters or 328 feet) can use a laptop or phone to engage or release a trailer’s brakes or adjust the braking force, without any interaction required from the victim. 



“Your trailer’s brakes slamming to maximum while your tow vehicle keeps going is the exact recipe for a jackknife – one of the more reliably fatal things a vehicle combination can do,” the researchers warned.Advertisement. Scroll to continue reading.


The third vulnerability is a medium-severity issue related to the firmware update mechanism. Unauthorized firmware changes could enable persistent compromise of the controller, allowing an attacker to interfere with braking, brake lights, and other safety functions. However, the researchers noted that this risk is theoretical, as they did not demonstrate the full impact or attempt to install malicious firmware during their tests. 



Elecbrakes was notified about the findings in mid-May and released firmware updates with patches in early July for its EB2 and VM1 products. Element Digital disclosed technical details last week. 



Once the firmware patches are installed, an attacker can no longer access the brake controllers remotely without an initial pairing that requires physical access to the device and cannot be performed over Bluetooth.



The vendor is still working on a remediation plan for the older ELBC 2000 product. In a customer advisory published on Monday, Elecbrakes advised owners of the older ELBC controllers to maintain an active connection between the controller and their smartphone or wireless remote whenever the device is in use.



Elecbrakes told iTnews that nearly 40,000 app users had initially been alerted to the availability of the update, and that it had been installed on 60-70% of controllers used to connect to the system daily.



There is no evidence of malicious exploitation of these vulnerabilities. 



While the researchers praised Elecbrakes for quickly developing patches, they criticized the company over the lengthy disclosure process and its failure to initially identify the older ELBC 2000 as being affected.



Related: Peninsula Energy Halts Trading After Cyber Incident Hits US Uranium Mine

[51] Help Net Security - Exploited: Abandoned Idunno.AntiCsrf library opens legacy ASP.NET apps to RCE (CVE-2026-131914)
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 07:22:32 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/idunno-anticsrf-rce-cve-2026-131914-exploited/

[TAM METİN - 329 kelime]
Exploited: Abandoned Idunno.AntiCsrf library opens legacy ASP.NET apps to RCE (CVE-2026-131914)

The US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency added three new vulnerabilities to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities catalog: CVE-2026-132224, an Optimizely Web Experimentation vulnerability attackers leveraged to plant malicious JavaScript on high-profile websites; a Drupal core remote code execution bug (CVE-2026-132165); and CVE-2026-131914, a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability in Idunno AntiCsrf, a legacy ASP.NET anti-CSRF library.

CVE-2026-131914 exploitation detected

ASP.NET is a server-side web-application framework used for building dynamic web sites, apps and services.

Disclosed last week by Mandiant and Google Cloud researchers, CVE-2026-131914 can be triggered by sending a specially crafted HTTP POST request, with no prior authentication or valid CSRF token.

“The module reads data from an incoming form submission and reconstructs it into program objects without checking that the data is genuine or untampered. The vulnerable check runs before the application’s own page logic, so it can affect many pages on an affected site, not just ones that use a login form,” watchTowr explained.

A successful attack allows the attacker to execute arbitrary code in the context of the IIS worker process that runs the website, which may lead to complete compromise of the web application server.

“Attacker probes have been observed validating exploitation by triggering a web redirect on vulnerable applications,” watchTowr reported on Tuesday, and noted that legacy components like this library “tend to stay in place long after their projects end, sitting inside older ASP.NET applications that are still running in production.”

It’s unlikely that CVE-2026-131914 will be patched since the open-source project hasn’t been updated for many, many years.

Mandiant advised organizations to stop using Idunno.AntiCsrf and replace it with an actively supported alternative such as:

System.Web.Helpers.AntiForgery,

ASP.NET WebForms Page.ViewStateUserKey combined with MAC-verified/encrypted ViewState on postback forms, or

ASP.NET Core Anti-Request Forgery (IAntiforgery).

CISA has ordered US federal civilian agencies to address CVE-2026-131914 and the other two vulnerabilities by November 27, and to check for signs of exploitation.

web application security

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/idunno-anticsrf-rce-cve-2026-131914-exploited/, helpnetsecurity.com, 25.11.2026)

[52] Help Net Security - Credential harvesting goes from fringe activity to major exploitation trend
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 06:30:47 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/vulncheck-credential-harvesting-exploitat-records/

[TAM METİN - 556 kelime]
Credential harvesting goes from fringe activity to major exploitation trend

Requests to steal credentials made up 13% of all vulnerability exploitation recorded between January and November 2026. Their monthly share climbed from about 1% in January to between 25% and 35% in September and October, data from VulnCheck’s canary network shows.

The company tracked 694 exploited CVEs during those eleven months. Attackers used 127 of them (18%) at least once to go after database passwords, cloud keys, API tokens or SSH keys.

“Credential harvesting has become a core part of how attackers exploit vulnerabilities,” wrote Patrick Garrity, a security researcher at VulnCheck.

Laravel and .env files top the list

Application secrets were the most popular target. Laravel secrets drew requests from 4,784 distinct IP addresses, .env files from 2,840 and process environment variables from 2,341. Cloud credentials came next, with AWS keys sought by 1,687 IPs and Google Cloud credentials by 1,007.

Open source software, AI products and developer tools account for 77 of the 127 CVEs, while network edge devices account for only 10. The flaws are recent, with 57% carrying 2025 or 2026 IDs.

A sixfold jump in four months

Between July and October, monthly harvesting records grew from about 17,000 to 99,000. Scanning first focused on CVE-2025-30208, a file-read flaw in the Vite frontend tool, in August and September. It then shifted to CVE-2025-29927, a Next.js middleware bypass that produced 68,000 of October’s 99,000 records.

September’s peak share of 34.5% came partly from a drop in React2Shell exploitation that month while harvesting kept growing.

Lower scores and a bigger share

The median CVSS score of the vulnerabilities used for harvesting is 9.4. Medium-severity bugs made up only 6% of the list but produced 23% of all harvesting records. They include a Laravel log disclosure flaw (CVE-2024-29291) and a Mailpit SSRF bug (CVE-2026-21859), both rated 5.3.

File-read and SSRF flaws do not require code execution. With these bugs, Garrity explained, attackers can read secrets off a server without running anything on it.

Thieves add AI keys to the haul

VulnCheck first saw attackers hunting for AI credentials in May 2026, and the activity has turned up every month since. In total, the company recorded 1,756 attempts from 406 unique IPs, tied to 27 CVEs.

OpenAI keys drew 1,085 attempts and Anthropic’s Claude keys 778, ahead of Hugging Face (317), Google Gemini (271) and Groq (153). DeepSeek, Mistral and OpenRouter keys were also on the list. Some attackers asked servers for config files. Others searched environment variables after getting code execution or scanned file systems for key prefixes such as “sk-ant-” and “sk-proj-“.

Credential files for coding agents like Claude Code, OpenAI Codex and Cline came up too, though each was targeted by five or fewer IPs.

AI key hunting starts sooner after a flaw is first exploited. Attackers exploited older Langflow and Vite bugs for months before they started looking for AI keys through them. For most CVEs first exploited after mid-2026, that hunt followed within days or weeks, and for newer Langflow and AutoAgent bugs within a week.

“Credential harvesting rides on a small set of very recognizable exploit patterns. Alert on path traversal through static and asset prefixes. Most of these hit internet-facing apps that rarely see legitimate requests like this, so a single hit is worth investigating,” Garrity noted.

vulnerability assessment

[53] Help Net Security - gputaskman: Open-source task manager for GPUs
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 06:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/gputaskman-open-source-gpu-task-manager/

[TAM METİN - 422 kelime]
gputaskman: Open-source task manager for GPUs

gputaskman is an open-source task manager for GPUs that runs in a terminal on Windows, Linux, WSL, and Apple Silicon Macs. It shows which process is holding a machine’s GPU memory, how busy each process keeps the card, and which AI model sits behind the numbers, where it can find evidence for one.

Windows Task Manager can report a figure like 15 GB used. It cannot say whether those bytes are model weights, KV cache (the memory a language model uses to hold its context), a browser, or the desktop compositor. Under WSL, nvidia-smi lists no processes at all.

Every model row shows its evidence

To work out what’s loaded, gputaskman pieces together clues: driver counters, the libraries a process has loaded, files mapped into its memory, launch flags, and what local AI servers say about themselves. Every model it names comes with a note on how it knows. It calls a model confirmed only when two strong clues agree, and a file that just sits in an app’s folder stays a candidate. It doesn’t dump VRAM or read model names out of other processes’ memory.

In one live sample, Windows’ older per-process counter charged about 15.3 GiB to DWM, the desktop compositor, while the Local Usage counter read about 1.4 GiB. That older counter can charge one shared surface to several processes at once, so summing rows inflates the total. gputaskman prefers Local Usage.

Estimates carry limits too. Raising a gemma-3-270m server’s context setting from 4,096 to 32,768 produced an estimated 504 MB of new KV cache, but the server’s measured dedicated memory grew by 83 MB. Gemma 3 uses sliding-window attention, so for that model the estimate is a ceiling. If you take one thing from the numbers, treat the breakdowns as estimates and the counters as measurements.

Output and probes need handling

gputaskman’s saved output includes file paths, launch flags, model names and local ports, so a shared recording can describe a machine’s AI setup in detail. To read server details, the tool sends simple requests to AI servers already listening on the machine. If a server needs a key that gputaskman can’t see, the request gets turned away, and the server logs one failed attempt every 30 seconds. If you spot that pattern in your logs, it may be the monitor.

gputaskman is available for free on GitHub.

20 open-source cybersecurity tools to keep your team ready for anything

GitHub Agentic Autofix and the question of who owns a bad AI patch

[54] Help Net Security - Security+ builds the foundation. SSCP proves you can execute
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 05:30:30 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/isc2-systems-security-certified-practitioner-sscp-certification/

[TAM METİN - 1910 kelime]
Security+ builds the foundation. SSCP proves you can execute

Cybersecurity knowledge and operational readiness: why both matter

Consider this threat scenario: Systems go down, alerts escalate, response windows shrink. In today’s high-stakes world, cybersecurity professionals need more than theoretical knowledge — they need operational readiness.

For many practitioners, the journey begins with building a solid conceptual foundation, but the true test of a professional lies in their ability to maintain a “ready state,” evolving from a student of the craft to a mission-ready practitioner always operationally ready to defend critical infrastructure.

This evolution is best represented by two complementary certifications: CompTIA Security+ and ISC2 Systems Security Certified Practitioner (SSCP). Security+ builds the essential foundation, and SSCP proves you can execute the mission.

Why foundational knowledge matters

Many successful practitioners begin with an introduction to the core principles of their field. In cybersecurity, foundational training establishes the language and concepts required to contribute meaningfully. This is where Security+ plays a vital role. It serves as a broad entry point that introduces the “how and what” of the industry.

Foundational knowledge enables a practitioner to:

Establish a common language: It ensures every team member understands the same terminology, from the CIA (Confidentiality, Integrity and Availability) triad to the nuances of different threat actors.

Enter the career field: For those starting out or transitioning from IT roles, foundational training is the primary gatekeeper for entry-level positions.

Without a base of knowledge, individuals lack the framework to understand why certain protocols exist. They’re unable to secure a network if they fail to understand the fundamental protocols that govern it, and they cannot manage risk if they’re unable to identify vulnerabilities.

Security+ verifies the institutional knowledge that serves as an essential baseline for cybersecurity professionals. It provides a broad survey of critical domains, ensuring you understand:

Threats and vulnerabilities: Recognizing attack vectors and the threat actors behind them

Access control: The fundamental principles of who gets in and what they can do

Risk management: Applying core concepts like the CIA triad, least privilege and separation of duties

Incident response fundamentals: Understanding the theoretical life cycle of a breach, from preparation to recovery

Network security basics: The foundational protocols of data in motion

Why operational readiness matters

While foundational knowledge allows you to explain a concept, operational readiness demonstrates you’re ready to execute it under real-world constraints.

Operational readiness is about execution under pressure. It is the transition from learning concepts to applying them in real time. Employers value practitioners who don’t just know security but can monitor, implement, respond and adapt in environments where there is no room to second-guess.

Consider these three real-world scenarios:

1. The skeleton crew (Friday, 4:47 p.m.): Monitoring alerts show unusual data exfiltration. Your manager is gone, and the rest of the leadership team is unreachable. Do you have the operational judgment to move without hesitation, or do you freeze because the scenario wasn’t in the textbook?

2. The zero-day (Monday, 9:15 a.m.): A critical vulnerability is on the news. Your CIO demands to know if the organization is exposed and what your plan is. You have 60 seconds to provide an answer that dictates the organization’s next 48 hours.

3. The acquisition (Tuesday, 11:20 a.m.): Your company has just acquired a new firm. You have 48 hours to assess its security posture using only a messy network diagram and administrative credentials. Where do you start?

Where SSCP fits in: The operational standard

While foundational certifications provide the “basic training,” SSCP serves as the “operational qualification.” SSCP is specifically designed for professionals responsible for the hands-on implementation and administration of IT infrastructure.

Building upon foundational certifications that validate knowledge, SSCP validates operational capability. Certification requires one year of cumulative, hands-on operational experience. If you haven’t acquired the experience yet, you can pass the SSCP exam first and become an Associate of ISC2, then earn the required experience within two years.

SSCP focuses on seven critical domains of operational security:

1. Security concepts and practices: Implementing technical and administrative controls
2. Access controls: Managing identity life cycles and authentication methods like multifactor authentication (MFA) and single sign-on (SSO)
3. Risk identification, monitoring and analysis: Turning thousands of daily SIEM events into actionable intelligence
4. Incident response and recovery: Executing containment and coordinating recovery while the business is offline
5. Cryptography: Implementing encryption and managing PKI without breaking production functionality
6. Network and communications security: Segmenting networks and configuring firewalls while maintaining performance
7. Systems and application security: Securing cloud migrations and endpoint protection across diverse devices

The evolution from learning to readiness

Security+ helps professionals learn the “how and what” — the frameworks and procedures required of the industry. SSCP then validates the “why and when” — the operational judgment developed through proven experience. Together, they create the complete operational professional. As Adam Gorecki, SSCP, Information Systems Security Analyst, explains, “SSCP deepened my knowledge of information security and has given me new opportunities to further my career.”

For those in the military or DoD, this shift is even more distinct. Security+ is often the starting point for institutional knowledge, while the SSCP validates the mission-critical judgment built during active duty or contract work. SSCP maps directly to operational work roles that demand technical and tactical execution.

Are you in a ready state?

Cybersecurity is not a destination; it is a state of continuous development. While foundational knowledge is the entry point, operational readiness takes your capabilities to the next level. Organizations need practitioners who can move with intention when the pressure hits, who can turn “I understand the life cycle” into “I isolated the systems and briefed the CIO.”

Security+ and SSCP: Why the best cybersecurity professionals earn both

Together, they create the complete operational professional — here’s how

When navigating the complex world of cybersecurity, there can be a tendency to view certifications as an “either/or” choice. However, the defenders who are always operational and always ready understand that true mastery requires a synergy of different skill sets and a tiered approach to professional development.

That’s why, when considering CompTIA Security+ and ISC2 Systems Security Certified Practitioner (SSCP) certifications, the question isn’t whether one is better than the other. The better question is what each certification validates and why together they create a more complete cybersecurity professional.

Security+ and SSCP are not competitors but rather complementary milestones on a single path of professional development. With both, practitioners transition from understanding security concepts to commanding and securing mission-critical infrastructure.

What Security+ validates: The institutional foundation

Every cybersecurity career requires a starting point: a common language and a shared understanding of the threat landscape. Security+ has long served as this essential baseline. It focuses on the institutional knowledge every practitioner needs.

Security+ validates foundational cybersecurity knowledge, specifically the “how and what” of the field. This includes:

Broad cybersecurity understanding: Provides a survey of essential concepts, from the CIA triad (Confidentiality, Integrity and Availability) to basic risk management frameworks.

Common terminology: Establishes a vendor-neutral language that allows practitioners to communicate effectively across different IT environments.

Entry-level qualification: Ensures practitioners have the foundational background required of those entering the field.

Security+ does exactly what it’s designed to do: validate that a practitioner has the comprehensive knowledge base the industry depends on. What it isn’t designed to verify is proven on-the-job operational experience — the hands-on work managing systems in high-stakes environments. That’s not a shortcoming; it’s a different stage of the journey. And it’s precisely where SSCP picks up.

What SSCP validates: The operational edge

While Security+ tells an employer you have the knowledge, SSCP demonstrates that you have the readiness to execute. As the world’s premier security operations certification, SSCP shifts the focus from learning to operating in cybersecurity.

SSCP validates operational capability across seven critical domains, focusing on the “why and when,” the judgment developed through real-world work. Its value lies in several key areas:

Experience requirement: Unlike foundational certifications, SSCP requires one year of cumulative, hands-on operational experience. This ensures that holders haven’t just studied the theory — they have proven their capability in the field.

Applied implementation: It focuses on the day-to-day administration and monitoring of IT infrastructure, ensuring practitioners can implement technical, physical and administrative controls according to global best practices.

Real-world execution: SSCP tests your ability to handle real-world scenarios: situations where you must move without second-guessing, such as identifying anomalies in SIEM logs or executing containment during a ransomware attack.

IT Security Specialist Tiffany Temple, CISSP, SSCP, says SSCP helps her think and work in ways that allow her to see the bigger picture. “It enables me to react quickly to remediate issues in dire situations,” she says. It is this transition from conceptual awareness to operational action that distinguishes SSCPs.

Side-by-side: Knowledge vs. readiness

To understand why the most effective professionals earn both certifications, it is helpful to view them as two distinct but connected stages of a career path.

By holding both, you demonstrate real readiness. Security+ proves you have the comprehensive knowledge base, while SSCP proves you have the operational judgment to apply that knowledge effectively under pressure.

Supporting DoD 8140 readiness: Mission-critical standards

For those in the military, DoD civilian roles or government contracting, the combination of these two certifications is particularly powerful. Mission-critical environments require both understanding and execution, and a lack of readiness can have real-world consequences.

Both Security+ and SSCP are DoD 8140-qualified. In the DoD workforce qualification pathways, Security+ establishes the baseline qualification for many entry-level roles. However, as a professional moves into intermediate operational work roles or advanced technical support specialist positions, SSCP becomes the critical operational qualification.

For military personnel, active-duty experience, including network security, system administration and incident response, typically counts toward the SSCP experience requirement. SSCP allows these professionals to bridge their military experience to a globally recognized civilian credential respected across more than 170 countries.

Why employers value both: Signals of growth and credibility

Hiring managers increasingly look for complete operational professionals who have demonstrated a commitment to continuous professional growth. Holding both certifications signals several key traits:

1. Progression from theory to practice: The candidate has successfully transitioned from academic learning to hands-on application.
2. Operational credibility: The SSCP’s experience requirement provides a level of trust grounded in proven on-the-job capability.
3. Proven readiness: The candidate knows how to turn thousands of daily SIEM events into actionable intelligence and communicate risk to technical and business stakeholders.

The complete operational professional: Knowledge + execution

The formula for an effective cybersecurity professional is comprehensive yet simple: Knowledge + Execution = Readiness. Without a foundation, you lack the context to understand the broader mission. Without the operational proof of SSCP, you may freeze when the pressure hits — when alerts escalate at 4:47 p.m. on a Friday and leadership is unreachable.

Security+ builds the foundation. SSCP validates readiness to execute. Together, they form a career advantage that not only defends the organization’s infrastructure but also secures a practitioner’s professional future. By mastering the seven critical domains of operational security, you move beyond being a generalist and become a specialist who thrives under pressure.

Assess your readiness: Do you have the operational capability to handle real-world pressure? Evaluate yourself across the seven critical domains with the Operational Readiness Assessment.

Become an SSCP: Learn more about the world’s premier security operations certification: Explore SSCP Certification.

Learn more about SSCP training: Find the right training path to validate your hands-on expertise and achieve your operational qualification: Explore SSCP Training.

[55] Help Net Security - Holiday scam losses force some victims to cut back on essentials
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 05:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/25/mcafee-holiday-shopping-scams-report/

[TAM METİN - 624 kelime]
Holiday scam losses force some victims to cut back on essentials

The proportion of Americans who say they have fallen victim to a holiday shopping scam has nearly doubled since last year, according to new research from McAfee. Forty-two percent say they have fallen for one, up from 22% in 2025.

Confidence in spotting scams drops

Shoppers are less confident in their ability to detect scams than they were a year ago. Seventy-five percent of Americans say they can recognize an online scam, down from 90% in 2025. Many respondents said they had encountered a holiday deal or advertisement they initially believed was genuine but later discovered was a scam.

A majority of respondents said AI-generated holiday scams can look convincing, while most expressed greater concern about AI-generated scams than they did a year ago.

Twelve gifts tied to suspicious sites

Researchers analyzed searches for popular gifts and linked 12 products to suspicious website activity. The list includes Applebee’s gift cards, KPop Demon Hunters Rumi dolls, New Balance 9060 sneakers, Saucony Peregrine 16 running shoes, Chanel Rouge Allure lipstick, Bvlgari Aqva Marine cologne, AMC movie tickets, TAG Heuer Carrera watches, Pokémon Trading Card Game products, Gucci cashmere-wool blend tailored jackets, Pandora advent calendars and Hot Wheels Black Widow.

The median time between a search and the detection of suspicious activity was 13 seconds for AMC Theatres and 1 minute and 18 seconds for Pandora.

Top holiday gifts being targeted by scammers (Source: McAfee)

“Scammers know which gifts people want,” said threat researcher Charles McFarland. McAfee described the tactic: “Scammers use popular gifts to lure shoppers to lookalike sites.”

Researchers found suspicious sites offering KPop Demon Hunters dolls, popular sneakers and Pokémon cards. Searches for Mona Pika, a fan-made Pokémon card that depicts Pikachu in the style of the Mona Lisa, featured prominently in the Pokémon-related research, illustrating how niche collectibles and viral products can also attract scammers.

The findings concern suspicious website activity associated with searches for these products, instead of wrongdoing by the legitimate brands or retailers.

Familiar brands used as bait

Respondents most often recalled seeing Amazon (40%) and Temu (33%) impersonated in fake holiday deals. Nike, Apple, eBay and SHEIN were also mentioned.

Respondents said that a retailer’s resemblance to a familiar brand or the presence of positive customer reviews could lead them to overlook signs of a scam. Gift card and prize offers were the most commonly reported types of suspicious messages, followed by messages about package delivery problems and limited-time deals.

“Many rely on phishing, a tactic where scammers impersonate trusted businesses to trick people into clicking dangerous links, sharing personal information or making payments,” Brooke Seipel, Content Editor in Chief at McAfee, wrote.

Risk-taking for better prices

Sixty-eight percent of Americans reported engaging in at least one risky shopping behavior to secure a better holiday deal. Some made purchases from websites they were unsure about or from unfamiliar retailers. Others bought from sellers with few or no reviews or shared personal information to claim a discount.

Some shoppers said they had ignored a scam warning sign to avoid missing out on a deal. An unusually low price was the warning sign they overlooked most often.

Of respondents who lost money to a holiday shopping scam, 60% lost $100 or more, while 48% recovered none of their money.

Some scam victims spent additional money to replace a gift, while others could not afford a replacement. The financial consequences also included cutting back on everyday essentials, borrowing money or using credit to cover losses.

The year-over-year comparisons draw on separate surveys conducted in 2025 and 2026 using different research providers and sample sizes. McAfee also noted that the final methodology and product rankings for its Labs research were pending confirmation.

Cloudera helps enterprises deploy sovereign AI without proprietary lock-in

Cloudera has announced Cloudera for Digital Sovereignty, a solution and partner ecosystem designed to give organizations jurisdictional, operational, and technical control across their data, cloud, and AI lifecycle.

Bringing together Cloudera Anywhere Cloud, Cloudera’s Open Data Lakehouse and Unified Data Fabric, and natively integrated Mistral open-weight models and Mistral Forge, the solution enables organizations to deploy governed data and private AI across sovereign clouds, private data centers, and fully air-gapped environments.

Regulation, including privacy and data sovereignty requirements, is among the top three barriers to scaling AI, according to recent Cloudera research. Cloudera for Digital Sovereignty addresses this challenge by enabling organizations to:

Maintain verifiable sovereign control across data and AI with zero-trust access controls, policies, lineage, and governance that remain consistent across sovereign cloud and private infrastructure. This supports compliance readiness for requirements including the EU AI Act, DORA, CADA, NIS2, Seals Sovereignty Framework, and others globally.

Put sovereign AI into production securely by extending access and governance policies to the enterprise context that AI agents retrieve and use. Integrated Mistral open-weight models can run alongside governed data, enabling agentic workflows and local model customization while keeping sensitive enterprise intelligence within existing security boundaries.

Shift workloads as requirements change without costly data movement through Cloudera Anywhere Cloud’s hybrid and modular architecture, enabling compute to move across sovereign infrastructure providers and private data centers without refactoring applications or migrating or duplicating underlying data.

“AI moved sovereignty from a compliance question to an operational one,” said Patrick Moorhead, CEO and Chief Analyst at Moor Insights & Strategy.

Walling off sensitive workloads isn’t the answer; it just makes data and AI harder to manage. Cloudera has a long history in hybrid data management, and extending it to open-weight models across sovereign, on-prem, and public cloud is a logical step. The next step is how quickly customers get it into production.”

“Digital sovereignty has to be an architectural decision, not a compliance exercise after the fact,” said Sergio Gago, Chief Technology Officer at Cloudera.

“As AI becomes more autonomous and embedded in critical operations, enterprises need to know that their data, models, and infrastructure can remain under their control even as technology and regulatory requirements change. We’re giving organizations a foundation they can build on for the long term, so they can adopt the next generation of AI without having to rethink their sovereignty strategy every time the technology evolves.”

Expanding the sovereign AI ecosystem

Building on its strategic partnership with Mistral, Cloudera is expanding customer choice through a growing network of sovereign cloud and implementation partners. Cloudera delivers an end-to-end, zero-trust approach to sovereign data and AI while giving customers the flexibility to combine sovereign deployments with other cloud and on-premises infrastructure.

This allows organizations to apply strict sovereignty controls where required without forcing the rest of their data estate into the same architecture or creating isolated security boundaries.

[57] Help Net Security - Astelia blocks attack paths to vulnerabilities without waiting for patches
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 14:24:11 +0000
Link: https://www.helpnetsecurity.com/2026/11/24/astelia-autonomous-network-mitigation/

[TAM METİN - 552 kelime]
Astelia blocks attack paths to vulnerabilities without waiting for patches

Astelia has announced autonomous network mitigation, a new capability that defends against AI-driven exploits by blocking attack paths to reachable vulnerabilities at the network level. Organizations can now eliminate the 1% of vulnerabilities attackers can reach without waiting for a patch.

The mitigation workflow · 6 steps, 1 approval branch (Source: Astelia)

Frontier AI models have cut the time to exploit to almost zero. Manual triage and remediation can’t keep up, so organizations need to automate how they respond. Autonomous remediation has become the industry’s latest buzzword, but it isn’t a viable option for large enterprises, where every patch needs a change window so it doesn’t break important business processes. Often, there is no patch to apply at all because the vendor hasn’t released a fix yet, or, for older and end-of-life systems, never will.

Blocking threats at the network level avoids both problems and keeps pace with AI-driven exploits. Instead of changing the vulnerable system, Astelia blocks the path an attacker would use to reach it, automatically and within policies the organization sets, without waiting for a ticket or a change window. Nothing on the asset changes, so business-critical systems, including OT, stay online. It works whether or not a patch exists.

This approach is built on Astelia’s reachability analysis. Astelia maps the organization’s real network topology and uses agentic AI to analyze what each exploit requires. By correlating those exploit conditions with the topology, it determines which vulnerabilities attackers can reach, and through which paths. In one enterprise deployment, approximately 40 million vulnerabilities came down to fewer than 2,000 that were reachable.

For each reachable vulnerability, Astelia uses the same topology to find the most effective choke point on the attack path, then drives the mitigation there through the organization’s WAF or IPS/IDS. A single rule at that point protects every vulnerable server behind it, where patching would take one change per server. Because Astelia only mitigates paths it has proven reachable, it doesn’t add rules for exposure that doesn’t exist. Initial support covers WAF and IPS/IDS rules, with firewall rules planned.

Unlike a patch, which can break a system and can’t always be undone, every mitigation Astelia applies can be rolled back, so teams can act quickly without putting business operations at risk.

Each organization sets the guardrails. Through Astelia Agents, teams set up a dedicated autonomous network mitigation agent that applies mitigations on its own within the policies they define, and connect it to their other agents to respond to urgent threats such as zero-days and critical disclosures like the PHP2Shell remote code execution flaws of October 2026, which left end-of-life PHP servers with no official fix.

Teams can require human approval for specific environments or actions, and every action is logged and auditable. Astelia’s analysis continues to run on read-only integrations, and mitigation actions use scoped permissions that each organization enables and controls.

“For years, security teams have been told to patch faster,” said Alon Noy, CEO of Astelia. “That advice stops working when the exploit arrives before the change window does. The same network data that tells us a vulnerability is reachable tells us where to cut it off, so we block it there automatically and the exposure is closed before an attacker can use it.”

Colorado says cyberattack took down myColorado app, warns 150,000 had data stolen

A critical app used by thousands of Colorado residents for state services was taken down by a cyberattack last week.

The Governor’s Office of Information Technology (OIT) said the incident was discovered on the evening of November 18 and involved hackers breaching the myColorado app — which Colorado residents use for digital identification, document storage and accessing state government services.

OIT shut down the app and began an investigation. The app is still unavailable as of Tuesday afternoon.

The state noted that the app has 2.1 million users and the cyberattack likely involved the theft of data tied to nearly 150,000 people. Investigators declined to provide more information about what data may have been stolen by the cybercriminals.

“We know how much Coloradans rely on these services, and we understand how this is frustrating and inconvenient. Protecting our users’ data is our first priority, and we will bring systems back online as quickly as we can once security is restored,” said Sarah Tuneberg, executive director of OIT.

Colorado urged residents to bring their physical IDs if they are traveling for the Thanksgiving holiday.

Those seeking to access state services can still do so through Colorado agency websites.

The state previously dealt with major cyberattacks on the Department of Higher Education and ransomware attacks on several smaller municipalities.

Several city and state governments are currently dealing with ransomware attacks and cyber incidents, including agencies in New York, Mississippi and California. On Friday, West Virginia’s legislature was knocked offline due to a cyberattack.

Queens Public Library in New York City confirmed it has nearly recovered from a ransomware attack two weeks ago that shut down services at its 66 branches. The library, which serves the borough’s more than 2 million residents, has been forced to operate without digital services.

The attack was claimed on Sunday by The Gentlemen ransomware gang — a cybercriminal group that claimed 398 attacks last quarter. The group far surpassed all other ransomware gangs with the amount of claimed victims, according to researchers at Halcyon.

In Mississippi, Lauderdale County’s more than 70,000 residents were also impacted by a cyberattack on the sheriff’s office on Monday.

The law enforcement agency said it was forced to take its systems offline after the cyberattack. Several services, including the inmate roster listing, are no longer available to the public.

[59] The Record - California privacy agency director Kemp to leave after two-year tenure
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 19:30:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/california-privacy-agency-kemp

[TAM METİN - 330 kelime]
California privacy agency director Kemp to leave after two-year tenure

The director of the trailblazing California Privacy Protection Agency (CalPrivacy) on Tuesday announced his departure after nearly two years leading the organization as it implemented a first-in-the-nation platform allowing consumers to easily opt out of data sharing.

Tom Kemp made the announcement in a LinkedIn post highlighting his work on the so-called Delete Request and Opt-Out Platform (DROP). The system is now used by more than 600,000 Californians who can block data sharing and foil data brokers with the push of a button.

The platform debuted in January, but penalties for companies violating consumers’ data privacy wishes only went into effect in August. Each DROP user has, on average, forced more than 200 deletions by data brokers, Kemp said.

He will begin a new position in January, but did not disclose any details about his new role.

“I am proud that CalPrivacy under my tenure has made great progress in making it significantly easier for Californians to exercise their privacy rights, and that our work represents a case study of how to deliver innovative government services that can make a tangible difference,” he said.

Under Kemp’s stewardship, CalPrivacy also led the push to pass two pieces of legislation expanding consumer privacy, lobbied against federal preemption of California’s tough privacy laws and oversaw the issuance of the largest fine in CalPrivacy’s history.

The agency also adopted the country’s most comprehensive regulations mandating how industry deploys cybersecurity audits, risk assessments and automated decisionmaking technology. The latter refers to software, including algorithms and AI, that processes personal data to make decisions about individuals relating to employment and other significant events.

Earlier in her career Suzanne covered the Boston Police Department for the Boston Globe and two presidential campaign cycles for Newsweek. [60] The Record - Lawmakers warn ID verification companies to bolster security after September hack
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 17:49:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/idscan-data-breach-warning-congressional-letter

[TAM METİN - 566 kelime]
Lawmakers warn ID verification companies to bolster security after September hack

A bipartisan group of federal lawmakers on Monday sent a letter focused on security practices to several identity verification companies, citing a September breach that led to scans of more than 150 million people’s drivers’ licenses and other identification documents appearing for sale on the dark web.

The breach of IDScan led to the theft of customer data stored in the company’s cloud platform, spurring an FBI probe and identity theft and fraud concerns for the vast number of Canadian and U.S. victims.

The six lawmakers behind the letter pressured the identity verification firms to provide detailed answers about how they manage customers’ sensitive data and what they are doing to protect it against increasingly common and devastating hacks enabled by artificial intelligence.

They cited not only the risk of fraud and identity theft, but also pointed to the fact that foreign adversaries are increasingly able to “infer Americans’ behavior through commercial data linked to government credentials,” said the letter, signed by Sens. Brian Schatz (D-HI) and Marcia Blackburn (R-TN) with four others from the Senate and House.

“Given the difficulty of tracking these credentials and data sales, remedying this breach will pose substantial challenges even where no further crime is committed.”

Brian Krebs, the independent technology journalist who first reported the IDScan breach in September, said on social media Tuesday that in the past couple of months he has found more than two dozen identity verification companies that store scanned IDs, often by default.

Customers — the companies verifying users’ identities — often have to pay more to avoid the scans from continuing to be collected after verification, Krebs said.

Cybercriminals on the Russia-tied dark web marketplace Nexus began exfiltrating the data more than a year before officials discovered the purloined information online, Krebs reported.

In their letter, the lawmakers directed the identity verification companies to lessen risks by proactively hunting for system vulnerabilities, deploying zero-trust architecture and minimizing threats by limiting collection, sharing and storage of personally identifying information as much as possible.

“This breach’s scale warrants additional attention to the data-handling practices of companies like yours,” a letter to IDScan CEO Jimmy Roussel said, noting that in addition to the identification documents nearly 600,000 medical cards, employment authorizations and other sensitive data was exposed.

Many of the medical cards were for marijuana dispensaries, Krebs reported.

The peddled license scans included ultraviolet, infrared and other detailed images, intensifying worries about how criminals may use it.

A spokesperson for IDScan did not immediately respond to a request for comment.

The lawmakers also emphasized that the personally identifiable information marketed by Nexus included scans belonging to high-ranking military and intelligence officials.

“The consequences of this theft may be widespread and persistent,” the letter to Roussel said.

“Given the difficulty of tracking these credentials and data sales, remedying this breach will pose substantial challenges even where no further crime is committed.”

“Your duty to safeguard data is critical in light of AI-driven cyberattack capabilities, which may be utilized by cybercriminals for zero-day exploits.”

[61] The Record - Carnegie Endowment for International Peace says third-party breach leaked donation info
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 16:00:00 GMT
Link: https://therecord.media/carnegie-endowment-data-breach

[TAM METİN - 525 kelime]
Carnegie Endowment for International Peace says third-party breach leaked donation info

Some donors to the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace had their information leaked due to a cyberattack on a third-party vendor named Soapbox Engage.

The organization sent emails to past donors on Friday warning of the data breach, writing that the incident occurred on October 28. The threat actors were removed from the vendor’s system on October 30, they said.

Carnegie Endowment for International Peace spokesperson Sharmeen Aly confirmed to Recorded Future News that the vendor was Soapbox Engage — an online fundraising and engagement software platform for nonprofits.

Carnegie uses Soapbox Engage for online donations. The hackers stole names, email addresses, phone numbers and giving history.

“Due to an integration with Carnegie's database, the unauthorized users were able to temporarily gain access to contact information maintained by Carnegie,” Aly said. “Based on our investigation, a small percentage of our database was affected. No full credit card numbers were exposed.”

Aly added that the organization notified everyone in their database about the breach due to European data privacy laws. Carnegie hired a cybersecurity firm to confirm the hackers were removed from the system.

Aly did not say how many people were impacted by the incident. Founded in 1910, the think tank currently employs more than 200 experts. The institute supports research efforts, diplomacy and more.

In its last financial statement, the non-profit declared more than $522 million in assets and an operating revenue of about $51 million.

Soapbox Engage did not respond to requests for comment but the incident coincides with the disclosure of data breaches and cyberattacks by multiple digital donation platforms.

Fundraising software company DonorPerfect said it was impacted by a security incident last week, and on Friday GoFundMe said it identified a cybersecurity incident where hackers breached their systems in two separate attacks in October and November. GoFundMe reported the cyberattack to law enforcement.

“We have reviewed the data involved, and determined it included limited personal information such as names, postal addresses, phone numbers and, for some, dates of birth or other information provided voluntarily in registration forms,” the company said.

No card numbers or bank details were exposed and GoFundMe said they “addressed the known methods of unauthorized access, disabled unauthorized accounts, and are actively monitoring for further suspicious activity.”

In its statement, GoFundMe said the recent attacks on other fundraising platforms were evidence that organizations had to do a better job of protecting donor information.

“To help prevent something like this from happening again, we are identifying opportunities to further strengthen the safeguards protecting our platform and are using those findings to guide additional security improvements designed to help prevent similar incidents,” the company said.

The Nonprofit Times news outlet reported that the recent attacks on the fundraising platforms appeared to be coordinated and at least one incident involved the theft of funds.

CISA warns of exploited N-able bug as company restores email platform

The federal cybersecurity agency is giving U.S. government departments until Thanksgiving to patch a vulnerability impacting a critical email security platform that blocks spam, malware and other threats.

All federal agencies were urged to patch CVE-2026-134624 – a bug affecting N-able’s Mail Assure platform. The vulnerability carries a 9.3 severity score and was added to the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency’s (CISA) exploited vulnerabilities platform on Monday evening.

N-able has dealt with a week of outages tied to the vulnerability after hackers breached one of the company’s servers through the bug, causing damage to Mail Assure and SpamExperts services.

In a statement to Recorded Future News, N-able said it remediated the bug over the weekend and restored access to internal dashboards for customers.

Inbound and outbound email flow and spam filtering continued to operate throughout the incident, they added.

“Since completing these remediation actions, we have continued scanning and monitoring the Mail Assure environments and have found no evidence of exploitation beyond the single N-able-hosted server identified in our initial investigation,” a spokesperson said, adding that they hired incident responders to validate their findings.

The company pledged to publish a root cause analysis within the next two weeks.

N-able said the breach and service issues are not connected to more than 30 vulnerabilities impacting its N-central platform that were publicicized two weeks ago by a security researcher.

N-central is a popular remote management tool that thousands of IT teams use to monitor and run networks remotely. N-able took all hosted N-central environments offline and ordered customers to turn off their servers.

A bug in the company’s N-central platform was involved in a supply-chain attack by ransomware actors in August.

N-able was previously a business unit within software company SolarWinds before it was spun off as a separate cloud-based software solutions company offering tools to managed service providers.

[63] Unit 42 - NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 23:00:14 +0000
Link: https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cve-2026-123633-nginx-http2-vulnerability/

[TAM METİN - 2339 kelime]
Threat Research Center

NGINX HTTP/2 Use-After-Free: CVE-2026-123633

Published:November 24, 2026

Categories:Threat ResearchVulnerabilities

Tags:CVE-2026-123633MemN0psNGINXProxy

CVE-2026-123633 is a use-after-free vulnerability in NGINX HTTP/2 that may let an unauthenticated attacker cause a denial of service. In the CVE record, F5 says code execution may be possible when address space layout randomization (ASLR) is disabled or bypassed.

CVE-2026-123633 affects NGINX servers that accept HTTP/2 client connections and proxy requests to a backend when free worker connections run low. A fragmented request can leave an active connection marked reusable. Under connection pressure, NGINX can close that connection and free memory the active request still uses.

F5's CVE record scores the issue 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0. The NGINX security advisories page labels the issue medium and lists versions 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected. F5 credits memN0ps (Palo Alto Networks Unit 42) among the reporters.

NGINX 1.30.7 on the stable branch and 1.31.9 on the mainline branch contain the fix. F5 also lists affected NGINX Plus releases.

Administrators should review HTTP/2 reverse-proxy exposure and upgrade to a fixed NGINX release or a vendor package containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix.

Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products and services:

Cortex Agentic Endpoint Security

Organizations can access tailored adversary intelligence, direct analyst consultations, and specialized exposure tracking through Unit 42 Threat Intel Services and its Deep and Dark Web Service.

If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team.

While researching NGINX, I independently found the HTTP/2 connection-state defect now tracked as CVE-2026-123633.

An unauthenticated client can send a fragmented HTTP/2 request that leaves an active connection marked as reusable. When the server resolves a hostname during the request and free connection slots run low, NGINX can reclaim that connection mid-request and free memory its DNS resolver is still using.

I reproduced the use-after-free with AddressSanitizer (ASan) on builds of unmodified NGINX 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 source. The NGINX security advisories page lists 1.19.7 through 1.30.6 and 1.31.0 through 1.31.8 as affected and 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 as fixed releases.

I reported the HTTP/2 use-after-free privately to F5. F5's CVE record scores it 9.2 Critical under CVSS 4.0 and 8.1 High under CVSS 3.1. The NGINX security advisories page labels it Medium.

In my ASan test on a Debian guest, ASan stopped NGINX at the first read from freed DNS lookup memory.

The resolver path in my test stems from the HTTP/2 connection bug tracked as CVE-2026-123633. CVE-2026-123634 is a separate DNS resolver vulnerability fixed in the same release.

I also found a memory disclosure vulnerability that leaked a libc pointer. I used the address to calculate the libc base and work around ASLR when chaining the disclosure with CVE-2026-123633. I reported this additional bug to F5 as a separate issue. From an attacker VM, I achieved pre-authentication remote code execution on a target VM as the non-root NGINX worker. The chain required a non-default NGINX configuration, and some attempts did not open a shell.

F5 assessed the memory disclosure as a behavior limited to a non-typical configuration and has not assigned it a CVE at the time of writing, so I am leaving out its details and the exploit steps.

How the Connection State Defect Works

NGINX marks a connection reusable when it can close that connection under pressure. Stream creation should clear the reusable mark once a request starts, but ngx_http_v2_create_stream() leaves it set in affected releases.

When a client sends only part of an HTTP/2 HEADERS frame header, ngx_http_v2_handle_connection() marks the connection reusable and returns early. The code below shows that decision:

The reusable mark lets NGINX reclaim connections stalled on partial frames before they exhaust the worker's slots.

The client then sends the remaining header bytes. ngx_http_v2_create_stream() builds a live stream and increments the active stream counter, but returns with the reusable mark still set:

NGINX now has a live request on a connection that its cleanup logic still considers reusable.

The 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 fixes clear the reusable mark at stream creation with ngx_reusable_connection(h2c->connection, 0). Figure 1 shows the sequence in affected releases:

A partial frame header marks the connection reusable

NGINX creates a stream with the mark still set

A later socket allocation drains the live connection

Reaching the Resolver Use-After-Free

The stale reusable mark lets NGINX reclaim a live connection when it needs a free slot. In my test, a request-time DNS lookup opened a new socket and triggered the drain.

My test used an HTTP/2 proxy route with a variable upstream. The variable evaluated to a hostname that was neither a literal IP address nor a configured upstream group, so NGINX looked it up during the request. The lookup also had to open a new resolver UDP socket.

The worker opens a new UDP socket only if it has none for the selected nameserver. Cached answers and in-flight lookups also skip the socket-allocation drain I tested.

Opening the DNS socket needs a connection slot, so ngx_get_connection() runs a drain check first. ngx_drain_connections() acts once free slots fall to one sixteenth of worker_connections or below while at least one reusable connection exists. The drain function then selects entries from the tail of the reusable queue, sets each connection's close flag, and calls its read handler:

My test client closed its socket with zero linger after completing the frame, leaving NGINX with an active request on a reset connection. When connection pressure triggered the drain, the HTTP/2 read handler tried to send GOAWAY on that socket.

A successful write would have left the request running, but the write failed with Connection reset by peer, so NGINX finalized the connection and request. The ASan free stack passes through ngx_http_upstream_check_broken_connection().

Request cleanup reaches ngx_resolve_name_done(), which frees the resolver state while the original lookup still holds a pointer to it.

In ngx_resolve_name_locked(), the DNS send runs before timeout setup. The send can drain the connection and free ctx. When the send returns, timeout setup uses the stale pointer, as the code below shows:

If the drain freed ctx, ngx_resolver_set_timeout() reads from it:

ASan reported that NGINX read memory from a freed DNS lookup context. The free path ran through ngx_drain_connections(), ngx_http_v2_finalize_connection(), ngx_http_upstream_finalize_request() and ngx_resolve_name_done(). Source review showed that, if execution continues past the reported read, ngx_resolver_set_timeout() can also write ctx->event and ctx->ident through that stale pointer.

In the run where ASan reported the read, I split the 9-byte HEADERS frame header after 5 bytes. For the control, I sent the full frame header first and then the HPACK block. NGINX sent a DNS query and kept running. Figure 2 follows the calls from socket allocation to the use-after-free.

Test Setup and Limits

I ran the ASan test in an isolated Debian 13 guest. NGINX was built from official 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 source with Clang 19.1.7, HTTP/2, ASan and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer. I sent the HTTP/2 requests over the guest's loopback interface.

For the 1.31.8 run, I set worker_connections to 16 and opened 13 filler connections that NGINX had not marked reusable. Table 1 shows the sanitizer results alongside the released-source review.

Table 1. HTTP/2 use-after-free tests and source review.

Table 2 lists the conditions I used in the 1.31.8 ASan test.

Table 2. Conditions used in the 1.31.8 ASan test.

A source build includes the HTTP/2 module only when configured with --with-http_v2_module. Distribution packages commonly enable it.

The ASan evidence in this article comes from builds of 1.31.7 and 1.31.8 over loopback. The 1.31.8 test configuration set worker_connections to 16 instead of the documented default of 512, and ran NGINX with master_process off. The sanitizer aborted that single process. The public fix commit also names upstream connection setup as a drain trigger.

The two-VM demonstration combined CVE-2026-123633 with the separate memory disclosure mentioned in the Background section. The target ran NGINX 1.31.8 with a root master and a www-data worker under a non-default configuration. Figure 3 records the running processes, version, loaded configuration path and listener.

The first attempt from the attacker VM recovered a libc address but did not open a shell; the second opened an interactive shell as www-data. The shell's hostname, IP address and boot ID match the target screenshot. Figure 4 previews the accompanying recording, which shows the same session with waiting periods sped up.

Video 1 shows the session:

Video 1. Demonstration of the attacker VM session and the interactive shell opened on the target.

Detection and Hunting

In the ASan test, NGINX logged a connection-drain warning before the sanitizer aborted the process. The warning can also appear under ordinary connection pressure, while a worker exit can have unrelated causes. A release build may keep running after reading freed memory. Table 3 lists investigation leads for HTTP/2 reverse proxies.

Table 3. Signals to use in detection and hunting.

The warning in Table 3 is logged at most once per second per worker, so one line can cover several drain calls. Because NGINX defaults to the error log level (which omits warnings), configure error_log at the warn level (or a level that captures more detail, such as notice or info), then correlate the warning with traffic and worker state.

CVE-2026-123633 describes a use-after-free in HTTP/2 reverse proxies that are running low on worker connections. The path I reproduced used a variable proxy_pass hostname, a configured resolver and a new UDP socket. F5's CVE description covers a broader set of HTTP/2 reverse proxies.

Check the running configuration. A master process can select another file with -c, so check its command line before assuming that the packaged path is the live one. The command nginx -T prints the on-disk configuration, which can still differ from what the running workers loaded.

I only tested proxy_pass. Operators should also review variable targets in:

For a matching route, check the running build and how close the worker gets to its connection limit. I tested one DNS socket route, but upstream connection setup also allocates through ngx_get_connection(), so static upstream routes still need review.

NGINX 1.30.7 and 1.31.9 fix the HTTP/2 issue. No workaround is listed in the CVE-2026-123633 record. If an upgrade must wait, the following changes could reduce exposure:

Where the service allows it, disable HTTP/2 on exposed listeners. Check every relevant listen ... http2 parameter and, on NGINX 1.25.1 or later, any http2 on; setting in the http or server context. Either setting can keep HTTP/2 enabled. Disabling HTTP/2 can break gRPC clients and may increase connection use if other traffic falls back to HTTP/1.1. If HTTP/2 stays enabled on other listeners, the extra HTTP/1.1 connections can push the same worker closer to its worker_connections limit.

Raising worker_connections gives the worker more headroom under the same traffic, but an attacker can still exhaust the extra slots and the connection-state bug remains.

Test each change before deployment. After upgrading, confirm the running worker uses a binary containing the CVE-2026-123633 fix. A configuration reload (nginx -s reload) keeps the old binary running in memory, so perform a full restart or live binary upgrade.

The defect starts when an HTTP/2 connection stays marked reusable after a request begins. In my test, opening a DNS socket under connection pressure drained that live connection and freed resolver state that the request still used. ASan reported that NGINX read memory after it had been freed.

Defenders should identify affected HTTP/2 reverse proxies, look for worker exits around connection pressure, and install a fixed NGINX release or vendor backport. My resolver test shows one way to reach the use-after-free. F5's description covers HTTP/2 reverse proxies more broadly.

Connection-drain warnings containing "worker_connections are not enough, reusing connections"; check the running worker_connections value when interpreting them
