[64] Unit 42 - Recent Trends and Tactics of Agentic AI Attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 15:00:19 +0000
Link: https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/autonomous-ai-agent-threat-campaigns/

[TAM METİN - 4695 kelime]
Threat Research Center

Recent Trends and Tactics of Agentic AI Attacks

By:Noa DekelTom Fakterman

Published:November 24, 2026

Categories:MalwareThreat Research

Tags:AI agentsCredential HarvestingLateral MovementPrivilege escalationReconnaissanceRemote Access Trojan

Credential Harvesting

Attackers are weaponizing autonomous AI agents in real-world campaigns, creating a risk that until recently has only been theoretical. Between July and September 2026, we identified and tracked more than 10 global campaigns in which different attackers deployed autonomous AI agents to enhance their attacks. These agentic AI attackers operated at machine speed, compressing the attack lifecycle from weeks or days into mere hours.

These campaigns targeted diverse sectors, including government, logistics, hospitality, manufacturing, agriculture and education. In every observed campaign the attackers gained their initial foothold through web shells that were deployed via vulnerable web applications.

The novelty of the observed agentic campaigns was not in their techniques, but in their speed and efficiency. Following the path of least resistance with familiar open-source tools and lightweight custom scripts, the AI-driven operators matched the tradecraft of proficient human attackers while operating at machine scale, at times executing hundreds of unique commands in just minutes.

However, using AI agents came at the expense of operational stealth. The agents left unmistakable behavioral fingerprints that revealed the involvement of non-human operators, including:

Structured machine-readable output formats

Adaptive exploit selection

Chain-of-thought reasoning comments that appeared in shell commands

This article details the real-world scenarios that we observed, organized by the tactical patterns that define how agentic AI is being weaponized today.

Palo Alto Networks customers are better protected from the threats discussed above through the following products and services:

Advanced URL Filtering, Advanced DNS Security and Advanced IP Defense

Next-Generation Firewall with the Advanced Threat Prevention

Cortex Agentic Endpoint Security

Idira Privileged Access Management (PAM)

Idira Endpoint Privilege Manager (EPM)

Idira Secrets Manager

The Unit 42 AI Security Assessment  service can help identify and mitigate complex AI-enabled risks.

Unit 42 Continuous Frontier AI Defense is an offensive security service designed to assist organizations with continuously discovering hidden and unknown exposures in dynamic environments, validate real-world exploitability across attack paths through agentic testing and expert analysis, and provide prioritized remediation and virtual patching guidance to accelerate risk mitigation.

Organizations can access tailored adversary intelligence, direct analyst consultations, and specialized exposure tracking through Unit 42 Threat Intel Services and its Deep and Dark Web Service.

If you think you might have been compromised or have an urgent matter, contact the Unit 42 Incident Response team.

Background of Agentic Attacks

What Makes an Attack Agentic?

An agentic AI attacker is an autonomous AI system — usually a large language model (LLM) with tool-calling capabilities — that independently plans, executes and adapts attack sequences without continuous human guidance.remote

Unlike chatbots that respond to individual prompts, agentic AI systems operate in a loop: they observe their environment, reason about what to do next, take action and then observe the results to inform their next step.

How Agentic Attackers Are Changing the Threat Landscape

The weaponization of agentic AI represents an increased threat to security for several reasons:

Speed and scale: An agentic AI attacker operates at machine speed. In the campaigns we observed, AI agents executed hundreds of unique commands within minutes.

Lowered barrier to entry: Attackers can simply provide high-level guidance while the AI agent automates intricate technical phases. This includes complex actions such as compiling kernel exploits, navigating cross-platform lateral movement and systematically compromising cloud infrastructure.

Adaptive reasoning: Unlike script-based attacks that fail when they encounter unexpected conditions, agentic AI attackers employ reasoning to try alternative approaches when failures occur. We observed agents attempting five different privilege escalation techniques in sequence when earlier ones failed, compiling custom exploit code on-target when pre-built binaries didn’t work.

Cross-domain fluency: A single agentic AI attacker can operate across Linux, Windows, various cloud platforms, web applications, databases and network infrastructures. This breadth of expertise would typically require a team of specialists.

New challenges in threat attribution: Because AI agents generate unique, dynamic payloads on the fly without distinct behavioral fingerprints, attributing attacks to specific threat actors is increasingly difficult.

The Agentic AI Attack Cycle

Our analysis of more than 10 campaigns observed between July and September 2026 indicates that the most successful intrusions consistently combined human oversight with AI agents in a unified operational model:

Human sets the objective: The human operator identifies a target and provides initial access — typically a web shell on a vulnerable web application. In some cases, the human operator executes the first assessment of the target.

AI agent takes over: The AI agent autonomously performs further reconnaissance, credential harvesting, privilege escalation and lateral movement.

AI agent reports back: The AI agent structures its findings using machine-readable output formatting and periodically checks in with orchestration APIs.

Human makes strategic decisions: The human operator reviews the AI agent’s findings and directs high-level strategy. This may include instructing the agent to target the payment system or establish persistence.

AI agent executes: The AI agent carries out the human’s strategic directives with tactical autonomy.

Figure 1 shows the attack lifecycle.

Common Characteristics of Agentic AI Attacks

A comparative analysis of AI-assisted campaigns that we observed revealed several defining operational markers. These characteristics are strong indicators of an agentic attacker rather than a human operator:

The speed of the attack

Use of section headers

Overly descriptive comments

Command Execution Rate

One of the most pronounced characteristics of an attack performed by an AI operator is its ability to rapidly execute a high volume of commands.

In one attack we investigated, the AI attacker was active for only 140 minutes across two days. In this brief period of time, the attacker progressed from initial access via web shell, to performing reconnaissance and enumerating the victim’s cloud environment, website and databases. Throughout the attack, the AI agent executed about 800 unique commands at an inhuman pace.

In another campaign conducted over a 12-day period, the attacker executed more than 8,000 unique commands.

To understand the difference in operational speed between human and AI operators, we checked the median inter-command gap between two phases of the observed attacks. In a subset of analyzed attacks, the first phase featured manual reconnaissance and connectivity checks by human operators, with a median inter-command gap of 23.9 seconds.

In the second phase of the attack, the operation was passed to an agentic AI attacker. Once an AI operator took command, the median inter-command gap dropped to 1.4 seconds. Figure 2 shows the median inter-command gap difference between the two phases.

Headers and Delimiters

In every campaign that we observed, AI operators used section headers in shell commands and scripts, such as ===[Title]===. These headers help AI parse the results of the executed commands faster. In human-operated attacks such headers rarely appear, and are usually only seen when an attacker uses a pre-coded script.

Figure 3 shows an example of multiple commands executed by an AI operator, with section headers that separate the output of the commands.

Chain of Thought Comments

A common indicator of agentic AI-generated code is using “reasoning comments” embedded within technical scripts. In more than half of the observed campaigns, we identified multiple scripts containing conversational, step-by-step reasoning notes. These notes explained the logic of the AI operator’s script execution, as Figure 4 shows.

Operational Trends of Agentic AI Attacks in the Wild

In addition to the behavioral fingerprints distinguishing AI operators from human attackers, our investigation revealed consistent operational trends. Despite targeting diverse organizations, the agentic AI attackers followed a similar playbook:

Moving across platforms

Relying heavily on web shells for initial foothold and persistent C2

Adapting strategy in real time

Demonstrating explicit awareness of deployed security products

The following sections detail the trends that we observed across the different campaigns that we investigated.

Multi-Platform Attacks

Many observed attacks began on Linux web servers, but quickly spread to other platforms. Each campaign aimed to expand as widely as possible across the targeted network, regardless of platform or technology. This is an example of how AI agents continue to lower the barrier of entry for less-experienced operators. While a human attacker might take years to master each operating system (OS), agentic AI can seamlessly move between them and even compromise cloud infrastructure.

Our investigation found agentic AI attackers misusing cloud services across three major cloud providers. We assess that the exploitation was adaptive — the agent discovered the cloud platform and pivoted to platform-specific techniques without any pre-configuration.

In one campaign targeting a US-based hospitality and gaming company, upon discovering an AWS EC2 instance role, the AI agent attempted to steal credentials via the Instance Metadata Service (IMDS). When IMDSv1 was blocked, the agent immediately adapted and switched to IMDSv2 token acquisition, succeeding after multiple iterative attempts.

Once authenticated, the AI agent enumerated 20 AWS services across eight regions, exfiltrating information about the victim’s buckets, email service and secret management. The ability to enumerate is a basic part of bucket functionality, but in this case it was misused for malicious purposes. Additionally, upon discovering S3 bucket names in the configuration files, the agent immediately targeted payment and financial data paths.

This targeted pivot suggests that the human operator instructed the agent to prioritize high-value financial assets. Cortex XDR detected the agentic AI attacker spawning multiple processes enumerating the victim’s payment resources, as Figure 5 shows.

We assess that a human operator instructed the AI agent only to collect information in preparation for a more aggressive future takeover, as no harmful actions were taken. We observed the AI agent testing its ability to write into production cloud environments, immediately deleting test files and examining the bucket’s policy and Access Control List (ACL) to see if the bucket was publicly accessible or had exploitable policies.

The AI agents also demonstrated an ability to operate across multiple operating systems within a single attack chain. In one observed campaign, an AI agent initially compromised a Linux server via a PHP web shell, and was able to pivot to a separate Windows IIS server with stolen SMB credentials. The rate of the lateral movement was incredibly fast: Within two minutes of identifying the Windows IIS server, the agent began deploying ASPX web shells over SMB. Over the next 26 minutes it pushed webshells to more than 40 locations across the server, rotating between five different webshell variants and uploading custom web.config files to enable script execution.

Adaptive and Strategic Attacks

One of the features that makes agentic AI attackers more powerful than older models and automations is an agent’s ability to adapt its strategy based on what it discovers.

In one campaign, we observed the AI agent demonstrate a particularly adaptive strategy: After exploiting the initial victim, the agent autonomously identified and attacked the victim’s IT vendor and sister organizations.

After gaining an initial foothold on the server, the AI agent uncovered the credentials for the vendor’s self-managed GitLab instance on the victim’s compromised web server. Pivoting immediately, the agent probed the vendor’s site, mapped infrastructure via WHOIS and tried to brute force access to the vendor’s GitLab instance, attempting a supply-chain attack across the vendor’s client base.

During a brute-force attempt, the agent used real names of marketing team members. This targeting suggests that the AI agent may have performed additional open-source intelligence (OSINT) gathering to identify specific employees. No vulnerability in GitLab was exploited; the credentials were exposed through the compromised third-party web server.

When the AI agent read the server’s NGINX virtual-host configurations, it identified over a dozen related sister-company entities on the same internal network. It then systematically probed each entity:

Harvesting CSRF tokens from login portals

Attempting credential reuse with passwords stolen from the primary target

Launching four iterative SQL injection scripts against the parent company’s admin panel

The agentic attacker takeover flow is shown in Figure 6.

Beyond strategic thinking, agentic AI attackers can escalate and tailor their attacks rapidly based on new information. This adaptiveness includes:

Quickly creating custom payloads

Pivoting to new attack vectors

Reacting swiftly to tool and script failures

In another case, we observed an agentic AI attacker execute a port sweep using a script named scan.sh. Just 21 seconds after the port sweep, the agent ran a second script named scan2.sh. The second script contained tailored recon commands that matched the exact technologies discovered on each port by the first script. Figure 7 shows the content of both scripts.

Web Shell-Based Intrusions

Across all analyzed campaigns, initial network access was achieved by deploying web shells on internet-facing web servers. This trend reflects a preference among human operators for leveraging web shells when orchestrating agentic AI operations. Web shells provide a straightforward approach to establishing initial footholds at scale during opportunistic attacks.

As an alternative to a web shell, an attacker could establish a foothold on a victim’s machine using a reverse shell, a remote access Trojan (RAT) or remote monitoring and management (RMM) software. However, those methods typically require more operational effort and resources to create and maintain.

In some cases, we observed that instead of relying on compiled implants to send commands, the AI agents independently dropped and utilized web shells. This provided the agents with ongoing code execution and persistence.

Given the extensive use of web shells as an initial foothold, secondary payloads were routinely obfuscated using Base64 encoding — a standard technique in web shell-based intrusions. Any attacker piping multi-line code through a web shell’s sh -c interface will encode it, to avoid breaking the code on shell metacharacters, quotes and special symbols. What distinguishes agentic AI attackers from human operators is not the encoding itself, but the volume, uniqueness and variety of encoded payloads they produce.

In a typical human-operated web shell intrusion, an attacker might deliver a handful of pre-written tools via Base64, which are reusable and often appear across multiple victims with identical hashes. However, in the agentic campaigns we investigated, the pattern was different: AI agents delivered 40-100 unique Base64-encoded payloads in a single attack session, each one tailored for the specific target’s assets. No two payloads were identical across victims and no payload matched any known tool signature.

Evasion Techniques and Security Awareness

While the high volume and fast rate of command execution make agentic AI attackers “noisy,” they demonstrated explicit awareness of deployed security products. Apart from attempting to evade sensors, agentic attackers are now actively probing their configuration, understanding the enforcement boundaries of detection tools and attempting to debug why a security tool blocked an action. This level of security-aware behavior was observed across multiple campaigns and represents a significant escalation from automated attacks that we have observed in the past.

In one observed campaign, the AI agent deliberately excluded endpoint detection and response (EDR) directories from file discovery commands. The agent executed the same find command on six compromised endpoints, excluding three different security vendors, as seen in Figure 8.

Notably, the same agent also ran a separate security tool enumeration command, checking for the presence of 16 different security scanners (nmap, nessus, openvas, nikto, masscan, nuclei, lynis and others), mapping the defensive landscape before proceeding with its attack.

Our investigation revealed multiple cases where AI agents transitioned from simple evasion to active reconnaissance of installed security stacks. These agents methodically interrogated endpoint monitoring solutions to map their operational boundaries, as demonstrated in the following observed behaviors:

The AI agent attempted to enumerate and exfiltrate the endpoint’s security product local directory, specifically hunting for sensitive configuration data and stored credentials.

In a display of iterative debugging, the agent applied strace to the Microsoft Defender for Endpoint process (wdavdaemon), analyzing kernel-level system calls to reverse-engineer how the daemon monitors environment activity.

The Absence of Custom Tools and Malware

One of the most interesting outcomes from our campaign analysis was what we didn’t find. Although in the course of our investigation we extracted hundreds of unique script payloads, we did not recover a single compiled backdoor, custom C2 framework, DLL implant or shellcode loader.

In most cases, the AI agent’s entire custom arsenal consisted of relatively simple, single-use scripts like PHP web shells, PowerShell WMI backdoors or Python credential harvesters. This is a departure from the usual tooling profile of advanced attackers, who typically invest in compiled, persistent implants designed for long-term access.

Unlike these attackers, the observed agentic AI attackers generated purpose-built scripts on the fly, tailored to whatever they discovered at the same moment. Thus, instead of a single “one-size-fits-all” implant, the attacker’s progress within the victim’s network was achieved through smaller, single-step scripts. Cortex XDR detection demonstrates how the agentic AI attacker could generate and deploy different script types under the same process chain, as Figure 9 shows.

Reliance on Open-Source Tools and Exploits

Although we observed many cases of agentic AI attackers generating custom code on the fly, campaigns routinely incorporated existing, open-source tools before resorting to custom coding.

One recurring example is the Copy Fail exploit (CVE-2026-31431), a Linux kernel vulnerability that appeared across several organizations targeted in the observed campaigns. In three separate cases, the AI agent attempted to directly download the exploit from external sources — either via the exploit’s official GitHub repo, or from the exploit’s website.

In a different attack sequence, the AI agent first uploaded a pre-compiled PwnKit binary and tried multiple invocation methods. When that binary failed, the agent fetched a pre-compiled CVE-2024-1086 privilege escalation exploit. Only after exhausting both public binaries did the AI agent begin writing custom C source code on the target, iterating through compilers and linkers. When the compilation attempt also failed, the agent tried to rewrite the exploit logic in Python.

In another case that combined open-source tools with custom payloads, the AI agent followed a similar, but more layered approach. After failing to execute a precompiled CopyFail for local privilege escalation, it compiled the PwnKit exploit from source directly on the target. Subsequently, for network reconnaissance, the agent wrote a custom C network probe tool (charon_lite.c), and built custom Python spray scripts on top of it using the open-source paramiko library.

Operator Fingerprints in AI Agent-Enhanced Attacks

With the rise of AI agent-enhanced operations, fingerprinting operations with static payload signatures and behavioral tracking is becoming more difficult. We suggest alternative methods of fingerprinting agentic AI campaigns, relying on human intention, operational mishaps and infrastructure correlation.

During our research, we did not encounter behavioral markers that link agentic activity to specific LLM models or orchestration frameworks. While attack patterns like Base64 payload delivery and systematic reconnaissance commands are consistent, their implementations are too diverse to provide a unique behavioral fingerprint. Since AI-generated code is custom built for each target, signature-based attribution is largely ineffective.

However, it was clear that a human operator was behind these agentic infrastructures. A human decides which target to attack, when to launch the operation and which assets to compromise. Beyond operational intent, the human operators and their AI agents continue to leave behind key artifacts that provide crucial attribution leads:

Infrastructure

Hard-coded C2 addresses in payloads: In some cases, AI agents embed attacker-controlled IP addresses and network ranges directly into generated malware payloads and web shells. In one campaign, the C2 server 204.194.54[.]240 appeared in both PowerShell download cradles and cURL probes.
Agent orchestration infrastructure: Pivoting on the hard-coded 204.194.54[.]240 C2 server revealed that it was also hosting an instance of CyberStrikeAI, an AI-native offensive security platform. The presence of CyberStrikeAI allows us to pinpoint the use of a specific orchestration platform. This is the kind of infrastructure-level attribution that behavioral analysis alone cannot provide.
Cross-campaign infrastructure overlap: The malicious use of 204.194.54[.]240 is not an isolated incident. Recent threat research by ReliaQuest documented the same IP acting as a staging and reconnaissance node in a separate agent-driven attack. Their investigation also noted the presence of the CyberStrikeAI login page and open directories hosting privilege escalation tools on this address, corroborating the use of this specific infrastructure across multiple agentic campaigns.

Toolset

Accidental file paths from the attacker’s environment: In one campaign, we observed the accidental pasting of a local file path: /home/kali/exploitmasteryai/targets/warranty/shell5.php. The exploitmasteryai path fragment is the only concrete indicator of the AI attack framework’s identity across the entire campaign, and it was exposed purely through an operational security failure.
Linguistic artifacts leakage in code comments: Attackers interact with AI agents in a variety of languages, which results in code comments generated in those languages. Defenders can treat these as artifacts like native language comments in human-operated campaigns. In one example we encountered, the attackers used two identical versions of a WMI backdoor — one that contained comments in Chinese, and a newer version that was specified to be English only. Figure 10 shows both versions of the WMI backdoor.

The exploitmasteryai path fragment is the only concrete indicator of the AI attack framework’s identity across the entire campaign, and it was exposed purely through an operational security failure.

Linguistic artifacts leakage in code comments: Attackers interact with AI agents in a variety of languages, which results in code comments generated in those languages. Defenders can treat these as artifacts like native language comments in human-operated campaigns. In one example we encountered, the attackers used two identical versions of a WMI backdoor — one that contained comments in Chinese, and a newer version that was specified to be English only. Figure 10 shows both versions of the WMI backdoor.

Agentic AI attacks are no longer a theoretical concern, but an active threat targeting a broad range of industries. The campaigns we observed were aimed at government, logistics, manufacturing, hospitality, education and agriculture sectors. All of the attacks used the same method: A human operator points an AI agent at a vulnerable web application, and the agent takes it from there.

What we observed is not a new class of attack sophistication. The techniques themselves are the same ones that a skilled human attacker would employ. The agents follow well-established attack flows, rely on known CVEs and use the same open-source tools that human operators have used for years.

Where agentic AI attacks fundamentally differ is in their speed, volume and their ability to quickly and efficiently adapt to different types of platforms and operating systems. These feats allow agentic attackers to fully compromise a network in mere hours, where even an experienced attacker would take days or weeks.

However, in the attacks we witnessed, agentic attackers left behind unmistakable fingerprints that a human operator is unlikely to produce. These indications included chain-of-thought comments and specific content delimiters. Hunting for these signs could help defenders recognize and prevent this threat.

Ultimately, defending against agentic AI attacks requires AI-powered defenses, as these types of attacks are diverse, and require a solution that can identify patterns regardless of the specific tools, techniques or platforms involved.

Organizations running self-managed source code platforms should:

Enforce multi-factor authentication

Enable sign-in rate limiting

Restrict access by IP where possible

Keep instances up to date

GitLab publishes specific hardening recommendations, and provides application hardening guidance for self-managed deployments.

In the era of agentic AI attackers, it is crucial that security vendors and defenders embrace a proactive approach and start fighting AI with AI. Throughout the investigation, we used several AI-enhanced solutions to track, investigate and prevent this threat.

Cortex XDR’s AI engine analyzes billions of daily user, system and security events to automatically generate precise, high-fidelity prevention rules. [65] NCSC UK - Familiar weaknesses, exploited faster: defending against agentic cyber attacks
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/blogs/familiar-weaknesses-exploited-faster-defending-against-agentic-cyber-attacks

[TAM METİN - 2890 kelime]
Familiar weaknesses, exploited faster: defending against agentic cyber attacks

How to protect your organisation from cyber attacks created using ‘agentic offensive frameworks’.

ArtemisDiana by Getty Images

Since September 2026, the NCSC has seen a marked rise in AI-assisted cyber attacks. Organisations across multiple sectors are reporting incidents as a result of threat actors using ‘agentic offensive frameworks'; software originally designed for red teaming being applied beyond its defensive purpose.

The incidents involved automated identification and exploitation attempts across organisations' internet-facing services and assets in quick succession. This resulted in downtime to some services or the compromise of systems, providing attackers with a foothold for further agent-driven activity (or in some cases, human-driven activity within their network).

It’s worth noting that:

none of the weaknesses being exploited are new or novel

the tools the agents have access to are common and provide no new capability the typical threat actor would have access to

What has changed, is how quickly and persistently threat actors can conduct attacks – at low cost – using these frameworks combined with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers.

This blog explains how agentic offensive frameworks are being used, what this means for cyber defenders, and the steps you should take to help protect your organisation.

What is an agentic offensive framework?

An agentic offensive framework allows AI models to interact with a set of off-the-shelf cyber security tools for the purpose of probing digital systems for weaknesses. Typically, a framework will:

use an LLM to plan a sequence of attack steps

use the tools against a target

retrieve the results and decide what to do next

At the time of writing (November 2026), this type of activity has become synonymous with the ARTEX framework, a multi-agent penetration testing and red-teaming tool originally developed for authorised security testing. Different tools come and go (others include Cairn, PentAGI, RedAmon, Xalgorix and Darkmoon), so rather than concentrating on a particular framework, here we’ll focus on how the combination of a human attacker plus a capable model plus a framework creates an uplift in capability.

Compared with a human attacker working without tools, an agent plus a framework:

works quickly (it moves from discovery to exploitation in minutes)

is exhaustive in its search (it will try every endpoint, parameter and credential it finds)

instantly learns from failures (it creates memory, reads error messages, responds and adapts its next attempt)

is cheap to run, at scale (the cost of running automated attacks is low)

This does mean that today's frameworks are noisy. They trade stealth for speed, generate a lot of traffic and will interact with almost anything they find, which makes them easier to detect. This may not always be the case if future frameworks prioritise stealth.

Notably, the issues being exploited currently are not novel memory corruption vulnerabilities (or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities), but unmitigated weaknesses that are often the result of years of technical debt that organisations have accumulated. Agentic attacks are exploiting low hanging fruit such as:

credentials mistakenly committed to code repositories

misconfigurations in infrastructure as code (IAC)

default credentials or lack of authentication

abuse of weaknesses in APIs

Once an initial foothold has been gained, the agents explore the environment, exploiting the fact that many credentials in an organisation are over-privileged for their needs, and networks are often flat or interconnected.

Defending against agentic offensive frameworks

Organisations need strong operational discipline to avoid being overwhelmed by the volume, intensity and duration of attacks from agentic offensive frameworks. Answering the following questions will help you develop a holistic approach that can protect your organisation.

1 Do you know what’s exposed?Agents start by mapping every device and service that's reachable. You should know which systems and services are exposed to the internet, and who is responsible for them. The NCSC’s Asset Management Guidance can help with this. Services successfully compromised by agentic offensive frameworks are sometimes those that an organisation has forgotten about such as old websites and legacy equipment. Organisations should be conducting regular security reviews of the assets they know about and using either internal skills or a consultancy to perform reconnaissance against their perimeter.

Do you know what’s exposed?

Agents start by mapping every device and service that's reachable. You should know which systems and services are exposed to the internet, and who is responsible for them. The NCSC’s Asset Management Guidance can help with this.

Services successfully compromised by agentic offensive frameworks are sometimes those that an organisation has forgotten about such as old websites and legacy equipment. Organisations should be conducting regular security reviews of the assets they know about and using either internal skills or a consultancy to perform reconnaissance against their perimeter.

2 Can you take services off the public internet?Rather than try to harden all services individually, you should look at making services only available to authenticated users. There are a few ways this can be done: place services behind an identity aware proxy, ideally ensuring strong phishing resistant authentication, following modern zero trust methods use firewalls to restrict access to services unless traffic comes from the corporate VPN or internal ranges configure mutual TLS certificates to require authentication by a user prior to interacting with a service In most organisations, the majority of services currently accessible from the perimeter can be locked down quickly so they can only be accessed by staff or partners. Specific hardening can then focus on the remainder of services that can’t be locked down. If you were to assume everything you have connected to the internet is vulnerable, how would this change your approach to security?

Can you take services off the public internet?

Rather than try to harden all services individually, you should look at making services only available to authenticated users. There are a few ways this can be done:

place services behind an identity aware proxy, ideally ensuring strong phishing resistant authentication, following modern zero trust methods

use firewalls to restrict access to services unless traffic comes from the corporate VPN or internal ranges

configure mutual TLS certificates to require authentication by a user prior to interacting with a service

In most organisations, the majority of services currently accessible from the perimeter can be locked down quickly so they can only be accessed by staff or partners. Specific hardening can then focus on the remainder of services that can’t be locked down. If you were to assume everything you have connected to the internet is vulnerable, how would this change your approach to security?

3 Can you detect the use of agentic offensive frameworks?Agent activity tends to look different from human activity: bursts of requests followed by short thinking phases, a wide variety of endpoints and parameters (including garbage or example variants which would never work), and increased error rates. Are you able to correlate across log sources to detect these signals? Collect and retain logs needed to detect, investigate, and recover from an incident; the NCSC’s logging guidance can help with this. Like all threat actors, agents leave a trail and appropriate logging helps responders to understand what assets the agent interacted with (and what it did) in order to remediate. Unlike human threat actors, an AI agent reads every error it receives and uses that information to refine its next attempt. Error messages shown to external users should reveal as little as possible to reduce the efficacy of the next action, so make sure your error messages give nothing away.

Can you detect the use of agentic offensive frameworks?

Agent activity tends to look different from human activity: bursts of requests followed by short thinking phases, a wide variety of endpoints and parameters (including garbage or example variants which would never work), and increased error rates. Are you able to correlate across log sources to detect these signals? Collect and retain logs needed to detect, investigate, and recover from an incident; the NCSC’s logging guidance can help with this.

Like all threat actors, agents leave a trail and appropriate logging helps responders to understand what assets the agent interacted with (and what it did) in order to remediate. Unlike human threat actors, an AI agent reads every error it receives and uses that information to refine its next attempt. Error messages shown to external users should reveal as little as possible to reduce the efficacy of the next action, so make sure your error messages give nothing away.

4 Are known vulnerabilities patched and misconfigurations fixed?Agents can test known vulnerabilities and exploits across targets very quickly, so patching internet-facing systems needs to keep pace. The same applies to configuration, particularly identity and access (IAM) settings that quietly drift from original designs. To help identify, prioritise and fix vulnerabilities within assets that are accessible from the internet, you can use the NCSC’s external attack surface management guidance in conjunction with our vulnerability management guidance. Companies assured under the NCSC's penetration testing scheme (CHECK) can conduct penetration testing to help you discover vulnerabilities and misconfigurations. Smaller organisations should refer to our guidance on adversary simulation to see if it’s right for your organisation.

Are known vulnerabilities patched and misconfigurations fixed?

Agents can test known vulnerabilities and exploits across targets very quickly, so patching internet-facing systems needs to keep pace. The same applies to configuration, particularly identity and access (IAM) settings that quietly drift from original designs. To help identify, prioritise and fix vulnerabilities within assets that are accessible from the internet, you can use the NCSC’s external attack surface management guidance in conjunction with our vulnerability management guidance.

Companies assured under the NCSC's penetration testing scheme (CHECK) can conduct penetration testing to help you discover vulnerabilities and misconfigurations. Smaller organisations should refer to our guidance on adversary simulation to see if it’s right for your organisation.

5 Have you installed bot challenges to protect abuse targets?Account and identity flows, inventory, checkout, and resource-intensive endpoints (such as search, report generation or LLM-backed features) are high priority assets that should be protected by deploying ‘bot challenges'. These require human interaction in an attempt to prevent automated tools from abusing services. Challenges can be set to low interaction (essentially invisible to human users) unless risk signals become elevated. When deploying bot challenges, provide an accessible alternative when a challenge is triggered.

Have you installed bot challenges to protect abuse targets?

Account and identity flows, inventory, checkout, and resource-intensive endpoints (such as search, report generation or LLM-backed features) are high priority assets that should be protected by deploying ‘bot challenges'. These require human interaction in an attempt to prevent automated tools from abusing services. Challenges can be set to low interaction (essentially invisible to human users) unless risk signals become elevated. When deploying bot challenges, provide an accessible alternative when a challenge is triggered.

6 Are your APIs protected adequately?Bot challenges are presented in the browser, on pages such as login or submission forms, but if the underlying API doesn’t enforce the challenge, an attack can call the API directly and bypass it. Many APIs have limits to prevent large volumes of requests, perhaps from specific IP addresses. However, agents routinely rotate through proxies and VPNs, so blocking IP addresses is often ineffective. Instead you should: apply rate limits to accounts, API tokens and authenticated sessions look at behaviour as well as volume: lots of varied requests, rapid failures or rapid retries can be as telling as raw numbers read the NCSC’s API security guidance  If your organisation publishes public code repositories, enable pre-commit automation to prevent secrets being pushed. Our guidance on keeping secrets out of the codebase and using a cloud platform securely can help.

Are your APIs protected adequately?

Bot challenges are presented in the browser, on pages such as login or submission forms, but if the underlying API doesn’t enforce the challenge, an attack can call the API directly and bypass it. Many APIs have limits to prevent large volumes of requests, perhaps from specific IP addresses. However, agents routinely rotate through proxies and VPNs, so blocking IP addresses is often ineffective. Instead you should:

apply rate limits to accounts, API tokens and authenticated sessions

look at behaviour as well as volume: lots of varied requests, rapid failures or rapid retries can be as telling as raw numbers

read the NCSC’s API security guidance

If your organisation publishes public code repositories, enable pre-commit automation to prevent secrets being pushed. Our guidance on keeping secrets out of the codebase and using a cloud platform securely can help.

7 Who can create an account, and how quickly?If anyone is able to create an account in seconds, then account-based controls (including the rate limits above) are easy to get around. Agents can sign up for new accounts as fast as you can block them. Consider throttling account creation, and heavier throttling of sign-ups that use disposable email services. Agents can use default credentials and use tools to brute-force weak passwords. You should protect accounts with strong authentication and appropriate access controls in line with the NCSC’s authentication guidance.

Who can create an account, and how quickly?

If anyone is able to create an account in seconds, then account-based controls (including the rate limits above) are easy to get around. Agents can sign up for new accounts as fast as you can block them. Consider throttling account creation, and heavier throttling of sign-ups that use disposable email services. Agents can use default credentials and use tools to brute-force weak passwords. You should protect accounts with strong authentication and appropriate access controls in line with the NCSC’s authentication guidance.

8 Could you exploit an agent’s perseverance?The traits that make agents effective also make them susceptible to deception. A honeytoken or tripwire placed somewhere no legitimate user should touch gives a high-confidence alert the moment it’s used. An agent's perseverance and exhaustive searching mean decoy systems, tarpits and fake ‘successes’ can use up an agent's time or attempt budget, and keep it away from your real assets. Microsoft has reported that deception measures reduced the attack traffic reaching real systems during a recent campaign using deception strategies.

Could you exploit an agent’s perseverance?

The traits that make agents effective also make them susceptible to deception. A honeytoken or tripwire placed somewhere no legitimate user should touch gives a high-confidence alert the moment it’s used. An agent's perseverance and exhaustive searching mean decoy systems, tarpits and fake ‘successes’ can use up an agent's time or attempt budget, and keep it away from your real assets. Microsoft has reported that deception measures reduced the attack traffic reaching real systems during a recent campaign using deception strategies.

Cyber security fundamentals matter more than ever

The NCSC believes the adoption of AI-enabled cyber can close the gap between attackers and defenders. We recently blogged about unlocking the potential of agentic cyber defence across a range of areas including enhanced threat detection and automated incident response. However, it will take time for defensive AI-driven programmes to mature, so organisations can’t sit back and wait for agentic defence to protect them. AI-enabled cyber defence is not the sole answer to agentic attacks; the foundations of cyber security and basic cyber hygiene remain the priority.

As we stated in the NCSC’s 2026 ‘The State of the Cyber Nation’ Annual Review, AI does not make established cyber security controls obsolete. In fact building strong foundations has never mattered more. AI is making weaknesses easier to find and faster to exploit; a forgotten key or an unprotected API which might once have gone unnoticed for years can be found in minutes.

Nearly everything the NCSC has seen can be prevented by implementing cyber security best practice, the application of modern cyber security approaches like zero trust, and by the diligent application of technical controls in line with your organisation's technologies and processes. For organisations seeking a structured approach:

zero trust principles can help protect against agentic attacks; explicitly verify identity, ensure least privilege everywhere, and deploy controls as if attackers are already inside your network

the Cyber Assessment Framework helps organisations responsible for essential services or critical functions assess and improve their cyber resilience

the NCSC’s Cyber Essentials sets out the minimum technical controls for organisations, as recommended by the government

Although they may not always be applied in the right place, the controls that mitigate the threat from agentic offensive frameworks will already be familiar to many organisations. Today's frameworks may be replaced by quieter, more capable ones, and tools will change. But the cyber security fundamentals won't.

This is your chance to get ahead of the threat. Consider how your organisation would answer the questions above, and make sure your answers hold up against an attacker that never needs a break and never gets tired of asking.

Artificial intelligence

Cyber security professionals

[66] NCSC UK - Introducing CORNERSTONE: delivering external services for the NCSC
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 12:00:00 +0000
Link: https://www.ncsc.gov.uk/blogs/introducing-cornerstone-delivering-external-services-for-the-ncsc

[TAM METİN - 935 kelime]
Introducing CORNERSTONE: delivering external services for the NCSC

The NCSC is seeking industry feedback on a new delivery model for modernising external cyber security services.

miniseries via Getty Images

The NCSC's existing services have developed strong standards, trusted market relationships and significant reach. But as demand grows, we need to consider how these services can be delivered in ways that are scalable, sustainable, and responsive to the UK’s changing needs.

As a result, the NCSC is exploring how it can modernise the way its external services are designed, delivered and developed. We want to create an operating model that enables NCSC services to achieve greater impact, reach more organisations, respond more quickly to changing threats and technology, and make better use of the capabilities that exist across the cyber security industry.

CORNERSTONE is a multi-year project to develop a more scalable relationship between the NCSC and industry, with each focusing on where it can add most value. Our ambition for CORNERSTONE is not simply to outsource services currently run by the NCSC; we want to make our services more impactful, accessible and adaptable. This will allow the NCSC to focus on those areas where it brings unique insight as the national technical authority for cyber security.

What are we are looking for?

We want organisations with cyber security expertise and real-world experience of assurance and service delivery to challenge our thinking before any future competition. More specifically, we are seeking feedback on a proposal which sees industry partners delivering the following NCSC services:

The CHECK assured penetration testing scheme

The CHECK assured penetration testing scheme

The Cyber Resilience Test Facilities (CRTF)

The Cyber Resilience Test Facilities (CRTF)

A new Protective DNS (PDNS) assurance service

A new Protective DNS (PDNS) assurance service

Currently, CHECK and CRTF are distinct assurance schemes operated with significant NCSC involvement, while the existing PDNS contract is a different kind of service altogether. The proposed new PDNS assurance scheme would create something new rather than simply transfer the existing PDNS service into the arrangement.

The purpose of bringing these areas together is to explore whether there are common administrative, data and operating capabilities that can be provided more effectively. These could include:

onboarding and managing assured organisations

onboarding and managing assured organisations

running scheme administration and assurance processes

running scheme administration and assurance processes

managing and processing data securely

managing and processing data securely

providing common tools and infrastructure where appropriate

providing common tools and infrastructure where appropriate

producing management information and insight

producing management information and insight

supporting the provider community

supporting the provider community

continuously improving the way services operate

continuously improving the way services operate

Challenge our thinking!

Meaningful market engagement depends on hearing from organisations with real-world experience of cyber security assurance and operating at scale. We therefore encourage you to read the accompanying Market Engagement Prospectus (PDF), and provide feedback on the commercial and practical viability of our assumptions. These include:

this phase of CORNERSTONE will operate as a self-funding concession model

this phase of CORNERSTONE will operate as a self-funding concession model

the NCSC will remain the root of trust for badging and assuring schemes and services

the NCSC will remain the root of trust for badging and assuring schemes and services

the NCSC will remain the owner of the NCSC-assured brand

the NCSC will remain the owner of the NCSC-assured brand

organisations will work collaboratively with the NCSC to bring together expertise to deliver innovative new schemes and services

organisations will work collaboratively with the NCSC to bring together expertise to deliver innovative new schemes and services

delivery partners will work in accordance with our security requirements for managing and storing data

delivery partners will work in accordance with our security requirements for managing and storing data

delivery partners will ensure services are able to respond at pace to the changing technological and geopolitical environment

delivery partners will ensure services are able to respond at pace to the changing technological and geopolitical environment

Find out more at our Supplier Information Sessions (MS Teams meetings)

We’re hosting two identical Supplier Information Sessions over MS Teams on 2nd December and 9th December, 10:00 – 11:00. During these meetings, we will talk through our plans and provide an opportunity for interested organisations to hear more about CORNERSTONE. Questions and answers from these sessions will be captured and made available to all interested parties. You can register your place by clicking on one of the dates below:

2 December, 10:00-11:00 | MS Teams Registration

9 December, 10:00-11:00 | MS Teams Registration

Timelines and next steps

We anticipate beginning the tender process in Spring 2027 and are considering a contract length of around five years.  However, our longer-term ambition for CORNERSTONE is broader than the initial three services described above. We intend to explore a scalable operating model through which the NCSC can govern, assure and enable a wider portfolio of external services, including both cyber assurance and Active Cyber Defence services, where industry delivery is appropriate.

In the meantime, we welcome thoughts, comments and questions by 18th December at [email protected].

[67] CERT-EU - 2026-021: High Severity Vulnerability in Java SE and OpenJDK
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 18 Nov 2026 10:11:05 CET
Link: 
                https://cert.europa.eu/publications/security-advisories/2026-021/
            

[TAM METİN - 406 kelime | KAYNAK: proxy okuyucu]
Release Date:18-11-2026 09:11:05

_History:_

*   _18/11/2026 --- v1.0 -- Initial publication_

## Summary

On 17 November 2026, Oracle and the OpenJDK project released fixes for CVE-2026-114938, a high severity vulnerability in OpenJDK which could lead, under certain circumstances, in unauthenticated Remote Code Execution (RCE) [1, 2, 3].

CERT-EU recommends updating vulnerable version of OpenJDK, prioritising internet facing applications.

## Technical Details

The vulnerability **CVE-2026-114938**, with a CVSS score of 8.1, is a memory corruption vulnerability in OpenJDK which leads to Denial of Service (DoS) conditions, and in some cases to unauthenticated RCE [3].

Exploitation requires all of the following conditions:

*   The Java Virtual Machine (JVM) runs on an x86-64 CPU with `avx512f`, `avx512bw`, `avx512vbmi` and `bmi2` (Intel Xeon Ice Lake or later, AMD Zen 4 or later). ARM64 systems are not affected.
*   The intrinsic is active. This is the default when the CPU supports these features.
*   Attacker-controlled Base64 data reaches the decoder, for example through HTTP Basic authentication, JWT, SAML messages, cookies or TLS data.

## Affected Products

The following products are affected by this vulnerability [1, 2]:

*   Oracle Java SE 8 (performance builds) version 8u511-perf (fixed released: 8u513)
*   Oracle Java SE / OpenJDK 17 from 17.0.6 to 17.0.21 (fixed released: 17.0.21.1)
*   Oracle Java SE / OpenJDK 21 21.0.13 and earlier (fixed released: 21.0.13.1)
*   Oracle Java SE / OpenJDK 25 25.0.5 and earlier (fixed released: 25.0.5.1)
*   Oracle Java SE / OpenJDK 27 27.0.1 and earlier (fixed released: 27.0.1.1)
*   Oracle GraalVM for JDK 17 and 21 releases (fixed released: Refer to the vendor)

These products support many known applications such as Keycloak, some Atlassian products, JFrog Artifactory.

OpenJDK-based distributions are also affected: Eclipse Temurin, Amazon Corretto, Azul Zulu, Microsoft Build of OpenJDK, BellSoft Liberica, SAP SapMachine and Red Hat build of OpenJDK. These vendors release fixes on their own schedules. Products that include their own Java runtime are affected until their vendor supplies an update.

## Recommendations

CERT-EU recommends updating vulnerable version of OpenJDK, prioritising internet facing applications, and restarting all JVMs after the update.

### Mitigation

Where it is not possible to immediately update, it is possible to disable the intrinsic feature using the JVM option `-XX:+UnlockDiagnosticVMOptions -XX:-UseBASE64Intrinsics`. As an alternative, disable AVX-512 with `-XX:UseAVX=2` [3]. The second option can reduce performance. Both options need a JVM restart. To verify the setting, run `java -XX:+PrintFlagsFinal -version | grep UseBASE64Intrinsics`.

## References

[1] [https://openjdk.org/groups/vulnerability/advisories/2026-11-17](https://openjdk.org/groups/vulnerability/advisories/2026-11-17)

[2] [https://www.oracle.com/java/technologies/javase/17-0-21-1-relnotes.html](https://www.oracle.com/java/technologies/javase/17-0-21-1-relnotes.html)

[3] [https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/java4shell](https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/java4shell)

                https://cert.europa.eu/publications/security-advisories/2026-021/
            , cert.europa.eu, 25.11.2026)

[68] CrowdStrike - Reprioritizing Automated Leads with CrowdStrike Signal
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Nov 24, 2026 00:00:00-0800
Link: https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/reprioritizing-automated-leads-crowdstrike-signal/

[TAM METİN - 726 kelime]
In May, we pulled back the curtain on the self-learning AI models behind Automated Leads, an approach to threat detection designed to surface the subtle signs of an attack. It’s powered by CrowdStrike® Signal and delivered from the CrowdStrike Falcon® platform.

Signal (distinct from SGNL) scores every indicator by how unusual it is for that specific host. Most of these indicators are faint on their own; for example, a PowerShell download or routine administrative command. They fire too often to warrant analyst concern every time. CrowdStrike Signal looks for what genuinely stands out on a given host, whether that’s a single highly unusual indicator or a handful of weaker ones that cluster in time. It correlates them, together with any related detections, into a single lead. A lead can come together from one short burst of activity or build quietly over several days.

In September, we introduced the next step: a new prioritization layer that sits on top of CrowdStrike Signal and changes how those leads are scored and ordered. Generally available since September 9, 2026, it significantly reduces false positives while detecting more malicious activity.

The Challenge: Most Unusual Activity Is Benign

Unusual activity is everywhere, and most of it is benign: a software rollout, an administrator trying a new tool, a one-off maintenance script. Some of it is detected correctly without being an attack, such as adware or sanctioned red-team exercises. Real intrusions are rare by comparison, and they range from commodity malware to adversaries who make efforts to look like a system administrator.

For a security team with finite time, this creates a ranking problem. When unusual activity never stops, the order in which leads appear determines what gets investigated and what waits.

What’s New in CrowdStrike Signal

CrowdStrike Signal still decides what becomes a lead. The new prioritization layer decides where that lead belongs in the queue.

Previously, a lead's confidence reflected how unusual its activity was, taken as a whole. It now also reflects how closely that activity resembles what real intrusions look like. A noisy software deployment and a quiet intrusion can be equally unusual; the prioritization layer tells them apart, so the intrusion climbs the queue while the deployment drops down or out of it entirely.

What this means for your queue:

Reductions of up to 70% in lead volume, with fewer leads to review at every sensitivity setting. Some leads fall off the queue altogether; some are still available at lower thresholds, which you can choose to view with the confidence threshold control.

Better coverage of real threats: Activity that CrowdStrike threat hunters confirm as malicious still reaches the queue, and more of it does at the Moderate and Cautious settings.

How It Works: AI Agents on the Endpoint

AI agents are an emerging attack surface, and adversaries are already moving on it: The CrowdStrike 2026 Threat Hunting Report documents STARDUST CHOLLIMA poisoning AI framework packages. Agents running on tools such as Claude Code and ChatGPT Codex now run shell commands on endpoints across the business, frequently with broad permissions and without a person approving each step. While most of their actions are routine, an agent that is misused, prompt-injected, or hijacked runs the same ordinary commands an attacker would, with the full access of the user behind it. This activity can be difficult to identify as malicious.

With Automated Leads, CrowdStrike Signal can identify malicious activity without knowing whether an agent was involved. It watches what happens on the host and raises a lead when activity stands out, whether the commands came from a person, a piece of malware, or an AI agent. Recently detected Automated Leads, as detailed below, show what this looks like in practice. The intent behind the activity varied from case to case, but the path to the analyst was the same each time: The agent's actions reached the host as ordinary commands, CrowdStrike Signal correlated the activity that was out of character for that host into a lead, and the prioritization layer moved the lead to the top of the queue because the pattern matched what past intrusions also showed.

This capability complements CrowdStrike® Falcon Guardian™, which secures agents directly by discovering them, controlling which ones may run, and tracing each prompt to what happened on the system. Security teams using both can view malicious activity within agents or on the endpoint.

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/reprioritizing-automated-leads-crowdstrike-signal/, crowdstrike.com, 25.11.2026)

[71] Schneier on Security - We Get the Infrastructure We Pay For
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: 2026-11-24T12:06:50Z
Link: https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/we-get-the-infrastructure-we-pay-for.html

[TAM METİN - 366 kelime]
We Get the Infrastructure We Pay For

This essay originally appeared as part of a round table in The Free Press entitled “Is America’s Infrastructure Under Attack?”

Microsoft, the FAA, banks, United Airlines, the NSA—it’s been a really bad week in cyberspace. But, amazing as it might seem, these are not related. As far as we know, they’re not the result of cyberattacks. They’re not even related to AI. (Well, that’s mostly true. The NSA will probably never tell us what went wrong.) It’s really just a series of unfortunate coincidences.

Our computerized infrastructure is vulnerable and fragile. Much of it is old, and has been incrementally patched and upgraded. A surprising amount of it is maintained by hobbyists, without any recompense. And more and more of it is centralized: the same systems affecting many different aspects of our lives and society. In many cases, it just barely works. And when it fails, it tends to fail badly.

There are a lot of technological reasons why this is true, but they are all downstream of the economic ones. The technologies we rely on are built and maintained within our economic system—and that’s where the core problem lies.

Security, safety, resilience, redundancy, recoverability—these are all essential parts of reliable technology, but they are all costs.

More importantly, they are inefficient: costs that feel wasteful until something bad happens. Modern shareholder capitalism, and private equity in particular, squeeze all inefficiencies out of a company in pursuit of near-term profits. Competition, with multiple companies competing to do the same thing, is another inefficiency. Monopolization removes that in pursuit of near-term profits.

The result is brittle systems, single points of failure. They’re more susceptible to cyberattack—both human and AI-fueled—and more susceptible to random mistakes. The more we allow unregulated capitalism to privatize gains while socializing risks, the more we should expect these sorts of failures.

Fixing this is technical, but it’s political first. It requires changing incentives, and that involves regulation and control. “What to do” is easy; finding the political will to stand up to moneyed interests and do it is hard.

Tags: cybersecurity, economics of security, resilience

Posted on November 24, 2026 at 7:06 AM   •
   16 Comments

(XXXXXXX, AÇIK - https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/11/we-get-the-infrastructure-we-pay-for.html, schneier.com, 24.11.2026)

================================================================================

[72] The Cyber Express - AI Hacking Tools Are Finding Old Security Mistakes in Minutes, UK’s NCSC Warns
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 07:36:21 +0000
Link: https://thecyberexpress.com/agentic-cyber-attacks-are-rising/

[TAM METİN - 743 kelime]
AI Hacking Tools Are Finding Old Security Mistakes in Minutes, UK’s NCSC Warns

Agentic cyber attacks are not breaking in through new or clever flaws. According to the UK’s National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC), they are finding the mistakes organisations already have, such as a forgotten website, a password left in code or an API nobody secured, and they are finding them in minutes.

The NCSC says it has seen a marked rise in AI-assisted attacks since September 2026, with organisations across multiple sectors reporting incidents.

The attacks rely on agentic offensive frameworks. These are tools originally built for red teaming and authorised security testing that let AI models use common cyber security tools to probe systems for weaknesses.

A typical framework uses a large language model to plan attack steps, runs the tools against a target, reads the results and decides what to do next. At the time of writing, the NCSC says this activity has become closely associated with ARTEX, a multi-agent penetration testing tool. Others include Cairn, PentAGI, RedAmon, Xalgorix and Darkmoon.

In the incidents the NCSC has seen, these tools tried to identify and exploit internet-facing services in quick succession. This led to downtime for some services, and in other cases to compromised systems that gave attackers a foothold for further activity.

What Has Actually Changed?

The NCSC is clear on one point: none of the weaknesses being exploited are new, and the tools give attackers no new capability. What has changed is speed, persistence and cost.

Compared with a human attacker working alone, an AI agent with a framework:

Moves from discovery to exploitation in minutes

Tries every endpoint, parameter and credential it finds

Reads error messages and adapts after each failure

Is cheap to run at scale

Attackers are pairing these frameworks with cheap, capable open models or illegal API resellers.

Why Old Weaknesses Are the Main Target

The flaws being hit are not advanced memory corruption bugs, or even known but unpatched vulnerabilities. They are unfixed weaknesses that come from years of technical debt, including:

Credentials accidentally committed to code repositories

Misconfigurations in infrastructure as code

Default credentials or no authentication at all

Once inside, the agents take advantage of over-privileged credentials and flat networks to move further.

There is one upside for defenders. Today’s frameworks trade stealth for speed, so they are noisy and easier to detect. The NCSC warns that this may change if future tools are built to stay quiet.

How to Defend Against Agentic Cyber Attacks

The NCSC sets out eight questions organisations should be able to answer:

Do you know what’s exposed? Forgotten assets, such as old websites and legacy equipment, are often the ones compromised.

Can you take services off the public internet? Put them behind an identity-aware proxy, a VPN or mutual TLS so only authenticated users can reach them.

Can you detect agent activity? Look for bursts of requests, many varied endpoints, junk parameters and high error rates. Keep error messages vague so agents learn nothing from them.

Are vulnerabilities patched and misconfigurations fixed? Patching of internet-facing systems needs to keep pace with how fast agents test.

Are high-risk features protected with bot challenges? Logins, checkout, search and AI-powered features should require human interaction.

Are your APIs protected? Apply rate limits to accounts and tokens, since agents rotate IP addresses.

Who can create an account, and how quickly? Throttle sign-ups, especially those using disposable email addresses.

Can you use an agent’s persistence against it? Honeytokens, decoy systems and fake “successes” can waste an agent’s time and trigger alerts. Microsoft has reported that deception measures reduced attack traffic reaching real systems in a recent campaign.

Fundamentals Still Matter Most

The NCSC says AI-driven defence will help over time, but it will take years to mature, and organisations cannot wait for it. Nearly everything it has seen could have been prevented with existing best practice, zero trust principles and careful use of technical controls.

“Today’s frameworks may be replaced by quieter, more capable ones, and tools will change,” the NCSC wrote. But the cyber security fundamentals won’t.

[73] Industrial Cyber - Check Point tracks Q3 ransomware activity, declining ransom payments, growing fragmentation across 117 brands
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 15:11:54 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/ransomware/check-point-tracks-q3-ransomware-activity-declining-ransom-payments-growing-fragmentation-across-117-brands/

[TAM METİN - 1356 kelime]
Check Point tracks Q3 ransomware activity, declining ransom payments, growing fragmentation across 117 brands

New Check Point data reported that ransomware activity hit a record 2,830 victims in the third quarter of 2026, including 964 in July, 1,042 in August and 824 in September, even as the threat landscape fragmented across 117 active brands, up from 91 in the prior quarter. For the first time, the top 10 groups accounted for less than half of observed victims at 49%, down from 71% in Q1 and 58% in Q2, with Qilin leading at 366 victims ahead of The Gentlemen at 339 and LockBit falling outside the top 10.

The findings also show shifting payout dynamics and geographic concentration, with the U.S. accounting for 42% of victims and the top 10 countries representing 70% of cases, followed by Germany at 5% and Canada and Italy at 4% each. Meanwhile, average ransom payments dropped 45% quarter over quarter to just over $1 million, while median payments rose 167% to $400,000, unfolding alongside rival infighting such as ShinyHunters’ attack on Clop and law enforcement actions including arrests tied to ShinyHunters and Operation KillSwitch against KillSec.

In its report titled ‘State of Ransomware Q3 2026,’ Check Point highlighted that fragmentation has not removed established leaders. Qilin finished Q3 with 13% of published victims compared with 12% for The Gentlemen, while INC Ransom represented 4% of activity, and DeadLock accounted for 3.5%. Akira and Krybit each represented 3.4%, followed by SafePay and Storm at 2.7% each. DragonForce accounted for 2.4%, while Clop represented 2.1%.

LockBit moved in the opposite direction. The group published fewer victims in each successive quarter of 2026 and fell out of the top ten altogether in Q3. LockBit had previously re-established operations following the Operation Cronos disruption, but the resurgence highlighted in earlier Check Point Research did not continue through Q3.

“The shift is visible beyond victim shares alone. According to our data, 91 distinct ransomware brands posted at least one victim in Q2 2026, rising to 117 in Q3,” Check Point reported. “The share claimed by the ten most active groups fell over the same period, even as overall attack volume rose from 2,051 victims in Q2 to 2,830 in Q3. Of the 117 active brands in Q3, 51 had not claimed a victim in Q2; the majority of these, 42, made their first claimed attacks this quarter rather than resuming previously dormant activity. Some of this growth reflects short-lived actors responsible for only a single victim claim, but the broader pattern shows more, newer groups publishing victim numbers, not just the already established threat groups.”

Check Point reported that Business Services is far ahead of every other sector at 34%, which is more than the next two categories combined. Consumer Goods and Services and Industrial Manufacturing follow at 13% each, so the top three sectors together account for roughly 60% of the total.

Below them, the shares drop off sharply. Healthcare and Medical sits at 6.5% and Financial Services at 6.4%, followed by Information Technology at 4.7%, Government at 4.1% and Transportation and Logistics at 3.8%. Automotive (3.3%), Education (3.1%) and Real Estate (2.3%) come next. Energy and Utilities register 1.9% and Media and Entertainment 1.5%. At the bottom, Hospitality and Travel and Telecommunications each account for 0.9%, while Construction and Engineering and Associations and Nonprofits each come in at 0.4%.

One of the most notable trends observed throughout 2026 is the decreasing number of ransomware victims willing to pay, despite increasingly aggressive extortion demands. Organizations are becoming more willing to reject ransom demands, even when faced with the prospect of public data leaks. Beneath this broader trend, however, a more nuanced dynamic is emerging where the ransomware payment market appears to be splitting into two distinct segments.

According to Chainalysis, ransomware payment rates already dropped to an estimated 28% in 2025, the lowest level observed to date, even as overall attack volumes continued to rise. While ransomware operators remained highly active, a growing proportion of victims chose not to engage with extortion demands.

Reporting published by Coveware this quarter suggests this longer-term decline continues in 2026, with less than 1 in 4 ransomware events resulting in payment. The report also shows changes in the average and median payments, where the average payment declined by 45% quarter-over-quarter (QoQ) to just over $1 million, while the median payment increased by 167% to $400,000. Together, these figures may point to two separate market trends in which baseline payment expectations by the extortion group continue to rise for many smaller organizations, while the largest and most ambitious extortion demands could be encountering growing resistance.

The report also pointed out that a notable trend among larger organizations has been that at least four high-profile victims publicly faced major extortion demands during Q3 and refused to comply. Stadler Rail, the Berlin state government, and Manchester Airports Group openly rejected the attackers’ demands, and there is strong evidence that Fairlife also did not pay the ransom.

“If this pattern continues, it could place increasing pressure on the upper end of the ransomware market,” Check Point reported. “While smaller and mid-sized organizations continue to negotiate, the fact that several large organizations publicly rejected multi-million dollar ransom demands suggests that large payouts from high-profile victims may be less reliable sources of revenue for threat actors.”

The report recognized that the attention surrounding ShinyHunters during Q3 2026 was not limited to the Clop incident. “In mid-September, Dutch authorities arrested a 24-year-old suspect linked by multiple reports to the ‘Umbreon’ persona and an ongoing investigation into ShinyHunters operations. In late September, a second alleged ShinyHunters member was reportedly detained in Jordan and was said to be cooperating with US authorities. Around the same period, the group’s leak site became inaccessible.”

Check Point assesses that these developments occurred against a broader backdrop of increased law-enforcement pressure on cybercriminal ecosystems during Q3. Most notably, the ransomware operation KillSec was dismantled in a coordinated international action, Operation KillSwitch, led by Spanish and German authorities with Europol coordination, resulting in three arrests, including a suspected 16-year-old operator detained in Alicante, Spain, and the seizure of servers, domains, and cryptocurrency assets. While unrelated to ShinyHunters, the operation underscored increasingly aggressive enforcement by authorities this quarter.

Clearly, the report indicates that Q3 2026 shows a ransomware ecosystem that is expanding without losing its established leaders. Qilin and The Gentlemen continue to dominate the upper tier, while a growing number of groups compete across the wider landscape. At the same time, several high-profile organizations rejected multimillion-dollar ransom demands, adding pressure to an extortion model in which securing the largest payouts is becoming less certain. Rather than trying to anticipate which ransomware brand may be responsible for the next attack, organizations should focus on reducing the opportunities that operators commonly exploit, limiting what can be reached after a compromise, and preparing for incidents in which stolen data becomes part of the extortion.

With activity spread across 117 ransomware brands and significant turnover among lower-ranked groups, defenses cannot be built around a small set of known operators. Prevention should instead target the opportunities attackers depend on, including exposed systems, compromised identities, malicious files and access paths into critical environments. This fragmentation also makes exposure reduction particularly important. Organizations should identify internet-facing assets, exploitable vulnerabilities, excessive access and paths to sensitive systems before attackers can use them, since limiting lateral movement and access to critical data can reduce the impact of a breach even when initial access succeeds.

The payment cases from Q3 also show that ransomware response is no longer simply a question of whether encrypted systems can be restored from backup. Where attackers steal sensitive data, organizations need the ability to detect exfiltration, restrict access to high-value information and respond to the legal, regulatory and operational consequences of potential disclosure.

Organizations highlighted in the quarter demonstrate that refusing a ransom demand can still mean accepting significant consequences, including publication of stolen information. An effective ransomware plan therefore has to extend beyond technical recovery to cover incident response, legal and regulatory processes, communications and business continuity, along with a predefined framework for making extortion decisions.
