[74] Industrial Cyber - Osaki Electric ransomware attack disrupts smart meter production, raising supplier continuity concerns across Asia-Pacific
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Tarih: Tue, 24 Nov 2026 15:08:37 +0000
Link: https://industrialcyber.co/threats-attacks/osaki-electric-ransomware-attack-disrupts-smart-meter-production-raising-supplier-continuity-concerns-across-asia-pacific/

[TAM METİN - 1007 kelime]
Osaki Electric ransomware attack disrupts smart meter production, raising supplier continuity concerns across Asia-Pacific

The OT Information Sharing and Analysis Center (OT-ISAC) published a report on the ransomware attack against Japan-based electrical equipment manufacturer Osaki Electric, which disrupted smart meter production and shipments, highlighting supply chain risks for utilities and critical infrastructure operators. Public ransomware trackers have listed Osaki under the Qilin ransomware group, but the attribution remains unverified, as do claims of data theft and any direct compromise of operational technology or deployed smart meters.

The agency highlighted that the immediate concern is supplier continuity, not a confirmed attack on the power grid. Critical infrastructure operators should understand their dependence on equipment, spares, software and support, and validate how long they can operate if a supplier becomes unavailable. The disruption itself is confirmed, with high confidence. The manufacturer reported an operational stoppage as part of containment, though the incident does not establish direct compromise of industrial controllers or deployed meters.

Regional relevance is also high confidence. Osaki owns Singapore-headquartered EDMI, a metering technology provider with a significant Oceania business, although corporate relationships do not demonstrate that the incident propagated. The downstream risk is assessed with moderate confidence: a sustained interruption could delay replacements, new installations or support where operators rely on affected facilities or shared services, and actual dependencies and available stock will determine the consequence.

OT-ISAC said that the incident raises concerns about potential delays in smart meter deliveries, equipment replacements, new installations and technical support for utilities dependent on Osaki’s manufacturing operations and regional business network. The company owns Singapore-headquartered metering technology provider EDMI, which supplies metering devices, communications and software solutions to utilities and metering service providers. EDMI has more than 5 million meters connected to its Storm platform across Oceania, according to Osaki’s December 2025 disclosure. However, OT-ISAC said there is no public evidence that EDMI, its Storm platform, overseas subsidiaries or downstream utilities have been compromised or disrupted.

It also detailed that the response priority for operators is to establish supplier assurance, validate inventory and recovery alternatives, and monitor relevant trusted access, rather than indiscriminately disconnecting field devices.

Osaki confirmed on Nov. 18, 2026, that it had isolated systems and halted production-related operations at three facilities in Japan after detecting server data encryption. The affected sites include its Saitama R&D and Manufacturing Center, Iwate Osaki Electric and Osaki Platech. The company has mobilized an executive-led emergency response team, engaged external specialists, notified authorities and begun contacting customers, while investigating possible data leakage. The production shutdown was part of containment, but the company has not disclosed a recovery timeline or confirmed the extent of any data compromise.

The first report identified three Osaki Electric facilities affected by the production shutdown. The Saitama R&D and Manufacturing Center, which handles research and manufacturing, had its production-related systems shut down. Iwate Osaki Electric manufactures product assembly equipment, while Osaki Platech manufactures meter components.

The company mobilized an executive-led emergency response team, brought in external specialists, notified police and relevant authorities, and began contacting customers. Its investigation includes possible data leakage. The company’s Japanese-language notice also cautioned against impersonation attempts through email and telephone.

Several aspects of the incident remain unknown, including the initial access method, ransomware variant, attacker dwell time and scope of lateral movement. Although public ransomware trackers have listed Osaki under the Qilin ransomware group, the attribution has not been verified. The recovery timeline, financial losses, production backlog, availability of alternative production capacity and customer-specific delivery impacts also remain undisclosed. The investigation has yet to establish whether data was exfiltrated, operational technology, firmware or product integrity was directly compromised, or overseas subsidiaries, cloud services and customer environments were affected. No incident-specific indicators of compromise have been disclosed.

The production-system shutdown confirms operational disruption but does not establish that ransomware executed on PLCs (programmable logic controllers), HMIs (human-machine interfaces), SCADA (supervisory control and data acquisition systems), or other OT (operational technology) devices. The incident represents a ransomware attack on a supplier, rather than a demonstrated supply chain intrusion into its customers.

OT-ISAC called upon critical infrastructure operators to scale their response to actual exposure and operational consequence by focusing on supplier continuity and digital trust boundaries rather than indiscriminately disconnecting deployed field devices. Within the first 24 to 72 hours, procurement and operations teams should verify whether outstanding orders or spare-parts flows rely on Osaki Electric’s affected Japanese facilities and confirm supplier status through established channels, while security teams verify any payment or delivery changes via known contacts and watch for impersonation, unusual remote sessions, and credential changes.

Over the next seven to 30 days, engineering and continuity teams should map critical dependencies and sub-tier components, quantify stock coverage and maintenance deadlines, validate alternate approved products, local support, and offline workflows, and verify firmware, configuration, and equipment integrity. Concurrently, security teams should review vendor privileges, enforce multi-factor authentication, monitor sessions, rotate any confirmed exposed credentials, and coordinate changes safely.

Ongoing resilience requires leadership, risk, cybersecurity, engineering, and procurement teams to maintain a shared view of supplier tiers, incident notification obligations, recovery targets, and contingency plans—aligning where applicable with frameworks such as Singapore’s CII Supply Chain Programme and CII Code of Practice (including the 2026 CII Code) and NIST SP 800-161 Rev. 1.

Additionally, organizations should exercise disruptions involving supplier and support losses alongside internal ERP/MES, scheduling, testing, or calibration impacts, share verified indicators and lessons through trusted channels without accessing criminal leak sites or downloading stolen files, and reassess response actions if new developments emerge around production recovery, confirmed data exposure, regional business impact, or product integrity evidence.

Last week, Claroty disclosed that it found over 65,000 non-essential consumer devices connected to networks supporting critical infrastructure, including OT (operational technology), healthcare and commercial environments, raising concerns about network segmentation and cybersecurity governance. The findings are based on an analysis of more than 45 million devices across 30,000 critical infrastructure sites. The inventory included 16,745 gaming consoles, most located in hospitals, 44,059 smart home and entertainment devices, and 4,915 connected vehicles operating on OT networks.
